In: Transmission Lines: Theory, Types and Applications

Editor: Dana M. Welton

Chapter 2

## CURRENT TRANSMISSION FIBERS AND R&amp;DS ON FUTURE TRANSMISSION FIBERS

## Kazunori Mukasa, Katsunori Imamura and Takeshi Yagi

Fitel-Photonics Laboratory, Furukawa Electric Co. Ltd., Yawata-Kaigandori, Ichihara, Chiba, Japan

## ABSTRACT

This  chapter  covers  the  current  situation  for  optical  fibers  used  for  terrestrial  and submarine transmission systems. In the terrestrial systems, even though various types of transmission fibers, including non-zero dispersion shifted fibers (NZ-DSFs), have been developed and proposed, standard single mode fibers (SMFs) have been far more widely used all over the world. In the submarine systems, on the other hand, new optical fibers, such  as  NZ-DSFs  and  dispersion  management  lines  (DMLs),  have  been  developed, proposed  and  deployed  in  the  real  field  systems.  Among  them,  DMLs,  which  use dispersion  compensating fibers as a part of dispersion management lines together with SMFs, showed a quite high performance. Because SMFs and DMLs showed quite high and matured properties, it seems that no new fibers, except for improved SMFs and/or DMLs, will  be  required  at  the  moment.  However,  it  should  be  noted  that  the  internet traffic  is  rapidly  growing,  and  around  2015-2020,  it  is  expected  that  the  current transmission  fibers  will  become  inadequate.  Considering  the  circumstances,  there  are three  important  directions  in  terms  of  R&amp;D  for  future  high-capacity  transmission.  (1) Reducing non-linearity by means of enlarging Aeff and/or reducing attenuation loss. It is of particular importance in the case of transmission systems using new multi-level signal format. (2) Using a wider transmission band than the current C and/or L-Band with new transmission  fibers.  For  example,  holey  fibers  (HFs),  which  have  an  endlessly  single mode  property, are one of the interesting candidates. (3) Using space division multiplexing (SDM) by using multi-core fibers. A multi-core fiber literally multiples the core number within the fiber dimension, which results in multiple transmission capacity per one fiber. These three approaches may be combined with each other to increase the factor of capacity-improvement. Examples of novel R&amp;Ds on these transmission fibers will be discussed in the latter part of this chapter.

ISBN: 978-1-61761-300-5

© 2011 Nova Science Publishers, Inc.

## 1. INTRODUCTION

Transmission  fiber  is  one  of  the  most  important  and  critical  components  to  realize  a desirable long-haul, high-capacity transmission. Basically, many properties that transmission fibers possess degrade the transmission performances. For example, attenuation loss decreases the transmitting power and deteriorates the Optical Signal to Noise Ratio (OSNR). Dispersion and non-linearity also distort the signal qualities.

New transmission fibers have been developed to suppress these degradations, mainly by optimizing refractive index profiles of the fibers. Various types of index profiles have been proposed to improve optical properties. Some examples of index profiles are shown in Figure 1. In terrestrial systems, various types of NZ-DSFs, such as LEAF ® , True Wave ® (TW) -RS, and  ULS-NZ  have  been  developed  and  proposed.  However,  the  development  of  DCFs  for SMFs  has  weakened  the  necessity  for  NZ-DSFs.  Meanwhile,  Medial/Medium  Dispersion Fibers  (MDFs),  which  have  intermediate  dispersion  of  SMFs  and  NZ-DSFs,  such  as TeraLight TM and TW ® -Reach have also been developed. We will show our research results on MDFs later. However, SMFs have still been far more widely used as a main fiber in terrestrial systems up until now. On contrary, in the case of submarine systems, NZ-DSFs have been widely deployed as the next fiber of SMFs. As a next candidate of the NZ-DSFs, Dispersion Management Lines (DMLs) or Dispersion Management Fibers (DMFs), which combine two fiber  pairs  to  realize  zero  and  flat  dispersion  in  a  desirable  band,  were  developed.  In particular,  the  DML,  which  consists  of  SMF+RDF,  has  been  developed,  improved,  and achieved  real  field  deployment  now.  The  next-generation  DML,  Medial  Dispersion  DMLs (MD-DMLs), have been developed after that and the performances have been also improved. However,  new  multi-level  signal  formats,  which  have  a  higher  dispersion  tolerance,  have emerged and non-linearity has become a more critical issue, rather than reducing dispersion. Therefore, MD-DMLs have been struggling to replace conventional DMLs constituted with SMF+RDF. We will show some our research results on these NZ-DSFs and DMLs for the submarine systems.

Taking  these  current  situations  into  account,  three  important  R&amp;D  directions  will  be introduced  and  discussed  in  this  chapter.  One  is  a  reduction  of  non-linearity  to  utilize  the novel  multi-level  signal  format.  For  transmission  fibers,  a  reduction  of  loss  and/or  an enlargement of Aeff are necessary. The  second direction is  expanding transmission wavelengths. Instead of using just conventional C and L-Band (about 100nm), a wider band transmission  will  be  required  in  the  future.  Holey  fibers  (HFs),  which  have  an  ESM  (SM operation for any wavelength) property, are one of the strongest candidates for this purpose. Third,  is  the  use  of  a  new  multiplexing  technology,  namely  Space  Division  Multiplexing (SDM), in addition to the conventional Time Division Multiplexing (TDM) and Wavelength Division  Multiplexing  (WDM).  To  realize  the  SDM  transmission,  a  new-class  of  fiber,  a multi-core fiber, has been proposed. Fundamental R&amp;Ds on these three fiber types are very actively taking place to meet the demand of rapidly growing internet traffic in the future. We will show some examples of up-to-date research results on these novel transmission fibers.

Figure 1. Examples of refractive index profiles of optical fibers.

<!-- image -->

## 2. CURRENT TERRESTRIAL FIBERS

## 2.1. Overall Situation

NZ-DSFs  have  been  proposed  and  developed  to  solve  the  dispersion  problems  of conventional fibers, such as SMFs and Dispersion Shifted Fibers (DSFs) [1]. SMFs have very large dispersion, about 16ps/nm/km, in 1.55 μ m band, namely C-Band, and their transmission distance is limited by a signal distortion due to their large dispersion. Using C-Band is very attractive because silica-based transmission fibers generally have a low loss property in this band, and Erbium Doped Fiber Amplifiers (EDFAs) are effectively operational in this band as well. On the other hand, DSFs have zero dispersion in this region, so the signal distortion, due to dispersion, is effectively suppressed. However, we entered the age of WDM transmissions, and the four wave mixing (FWM) phenomenon became a serious problem in the case of DSFs due  to  very  small  dispersion.  In  the  case  of  NZ-DSFs,  on  the  other  hand,  zero  dispersion wavelength ( λ 0) was slightly shifted toward either a longer or shorter wavelength from zero dispersion  DSF,  so  the  FWM  phenomenon  would  be  effectively  suppressed,  while  also suppressing  the  signal  distortion  caused  by  large  dispersion.  Both  positive  and  negative dispersion  NZ-DSFs  have  been  developed,  and  in  particular,  positive  dispersion  NZ-DSFs have attracted attention as the next-generation terrestrial fibers.

However, the development of dispersion compensating fibers (DCFs) for SMFs changed the situation [2]. The dispersion problem of SMFs can be successfully solved by using DCFs. By  using  DCFs,  it  is  possible  to  compensate  not  only  dispersion  at  1.55 μ m but  also  their dispersion slope, which results in flat dispersion in C-Band after this compensation. Once the dispersion  problem  was  solved  by  DCFs,  the  developments  and  deployments  of  NZ-DSFs rapidly declined, since they have smaller Aeff than SMFs. Instead, improvements in SMFs have  gathered  more  attention.  Meanwhile,  medial  dispersion  fibers  (MDFs),  which  have intermediate dispersion of SMFs and NZ-DSFs, have also been proposed and are still being studied. However, the truth is that a strong trend in using SMFs for terrestrial lines, have not yet changed.

## 2.2. NZ-DSFs vs. SMFs for the Terrestrial Systems

As  described  before,  many  types  of  NZ-DSFs  have  been  developed  to  compete  with standard SMFs. The optical properties of NZ-DSFs, in addition to those of a SMF, are shown in table 1.

Table 1. Optical properties of NZ-DSFs and a SMF (1550nm)

|          | Dispersion ps/nm/km   |   Slope ps/nm 2 /km |   Aeff μ m 2 | Loss dB/km   | PMD ps/rkm   |
|----------|-----------------------|---------------------|--------------|--------------|--------------|
| LEAF ®   | 4-6                   |                0.09 |           72 | 0.19-0.20    | <0.1         |
| TW ® -RS | 4-6                   |                0.04 |           52 | 0.19-0.20    | <0.1         |
| ULS NZ   | 4-6                   |                0.02 |           45 | 0.19-0.20    | <0.1         |
| SMF      | 16                    |                0.06 |           80 | 0.19         | <0.1         |

Figure 2. Dispersion properties of SMF+DCF.

<!-- image -->

Table 2. Optical properties of a SMF and a high performance DCF

|        |   Dispersion ps/nm/km |   D.Slope ps/nm 2 /km |   Loss dB/km |   Aeff μ m 2 |   PMD ps/rkm | λ c nm   |
|--------|-----------------------|-----------------------|--------------|--------------|--------------|----------|
| SMF    |                    16 |                  0.06 |         0.19 |           80 |         0.03 | 1250     |
| HP-DCF |                  -250 |                 -0.94 |         0.6  |           15 |         0.1  | <1500    |

NZ-DSFs have a clear advantage, namely reduced local dispersion, which should become a key factor to take the place of standard SMFs. However, as described, large dispersion of SMFs have been successfully  compensated by  DCFs,  which  have  negative  dispersion  (D), negative  dispersion  slope  (S)  and  the  same  value  of  D/S  as  SMFs.  An  example  of  the dispersion properties of a SMF and a DCF as well as total link dispersion are shown in Figure 2. As shown in the Figure 2, zero and flat dispersion can be realized by using the SMF and the  DCF.  Since  the  DCF  has  a  large  absolute  value  of  local  dispersion,  which  is  100ps/nm/km, a required length of the DCF is much shorter than SMFs. These DCFs have relatively high attenuation loss and non-linearity, so it is advantageous to use as short length of  DCFs  as  possible.  They  are  packed  in  modules  and  generally  set  in  the  transmission stations.  DCFs  alleviate  the  dispersion  problem  of  SMFs,  therefore  SMFs  have  clear advantages, such as low loss, low non-linearity, low PMD, and last but not least, low cost (mass-production possibility). To change this situation, DCFs for NZ-DSFs have also been developed.  However,  large  Aeff  NZ-DSFs  tend  to  have  steep  dispersion  slope.  Large dispersion  slope  make  it  more  difficult  to  be  fully  compensated  by  DCFs,  so  residual dispersion remains large even after the dispersion compensation. Furthermore, applicability for  wide-band  transmission  as  well  as  Raman  amplification  has  become  more  important. Large Aeff NZ-DSFs, with steep dispersion slope, are inadequate for wide-band transmission and/or  Raman  amplification  systems  due  to  the  four  wave  mixing  (FWM)  problem.  Low dispersion slope NZ-DSFs can solve these problems, but these fibers have small Aeff, and are disadvantageous, in particular, for long-haul transmission systems. For these reasons, SMFs still are the most widely used as the standard terrestrial fiber all over the world.

Since SMFs have been widely used up until now, performance improvements of DCFs have  been  actively  investigated.  Recently,  DCFs  with  quite  high  performances  have  been commercialized.  In  particular,  dispersion  enlargement  is  very  important,  because  larger dispersion makes the required length of DCFs shorter. Table 2 shows optical properties of a high-performance  DCF.  Dispersion  of  the  DCF  has  expanded  to  -250ps/nm/km,  while maintaining  other  optical  properties  well.  Even  though  the  performance  improvements  of DCFs for NZ-DSFs have also been investigated, the SMF+HP-DCF is more advantageous considering  performances  of  the  total  systems.  Additionally,  system  requirements  are  also shifting to use large-Aeff fibers with large local dispersion, such as SMFs, accompanied by improvements of the new signal format.

## 2.3. Development of MDF

To take the place of SMFs, a new category of fiber, namely MDF, which has intermediate dispersion  of  NZ-DSFs  and  SMFs  and  takes  a  balance  between  them,  has  also  been

developed. MDFs have relatively large dispersion (7-13ps/nm/km) and low dispersion slope, which is  effective  to  avoid  the  FWM  phenomenon  in  a  wide  range  of  wavelengths.  Large dispersion and low dispersion slope, which result in large D/S, are also advantageous for their ease in dispersion compensation. To avoid undesirable non-linear effects, including the interband Raman effect,  which  is  caused  by  wide-band  transmissions,  e.g.  using  S  and  L-band simultaneously, a realization of large Aeff is also very important. For example, TeraLight TM and TW ® -REACH can be categorised in this fiber group. The optical properties of the MDFs are shown in table 3.

In Figure 3, dispersion properties of various types of fibers are shown. In C-Band, both NZ-DSFs and MDFs have smaller local dispersion than SMFs. Zero dispersion wavelength ( λ 0)  was  decided  by  local  dispersion  at  1550nm  and  dispersion  slope.  The  MDFs  are interesting since they have wider wavelength band without λ 0, which is essential to avoid the FWM phenomenon. They also have relatively large Aeff and low attenuation loss, and are designed to have a single mode region over target wavelength-band, e.g., S, C, and L-Band. Furthermore, DCFs for the MDFs were also developed. By developing MDFs, some problems of  NZ-DSFs  have  been  successfully  solved.  However,  as  described,  SMFs  are  far  more widely used than any other types of fibers, including MDFs, all over the world at this stage. Even though MDFs have relatively large Aeff and low loss, SMFs have better performances in terms of large Aeff, low loss, low PMD, and low cost, to name a few. Needless to say, the fabrication processes of the fibers have been matured, so not only SMFs but also NZ-DSFs and MDF showed quite low impurity loss and imperfection loss. However, SMFs can show lower  Rayleigh  scattering  loss,  since  they  generally  have  lower  delta  than  other  types  of fibers. Rayleigh scattering loss is fundamental unavoidable loss, like glass absorption losses, of the glass fibers, so SMFs have an advantage from this aspect. However, it should be noted some of NZ-DSFs and MDFs have been actually deployed in the real field.

[Ref.] e.g. Corning, OFS fitel, Alcatel, Fujikura homepage.

Table 3. Optical properties of MDFs （ 1550nm)

|              | Dispersion ps/nm/km   |   Slope ps/nm 2 /km |   Aeff μ m 2 | Loss dB/km   | PMD ps/rkm   |
|--------------|-----------------------|---------------------|--------------|--------------|--------------|
| TeraLight TM | ~8                    |                0.06 |           65 | 0.19-0.20    | <0.1         |
| TW ® -REACH  | ~8                    |                0.04 |           55 | 0.19-0.20    | <0.1         |

We  also  developed  some  MDFs  for  terrestrial  systems.  We  selected  a  segment  core profile (Figure 1-1), and optimized the profile to obtain large Aeff and low dispersion slope. Note we did not use a depressed layer (meaning Δ 2=0%) this time, so it was easy to fabricate and obtain low loss by avoiding two dopants (typically GeO2 and F) interferences. Δ 1 is set to almost the same value as that of SMFs to obtain low Rayleigh scattering loss, and every parameter  was  carefully  optimized  to  enlarge  Aeff  to  ~70 μ m 2 without  an  increase  in dispersion slope. Based on the simulation results, the MDF was fabricated by the silica-based VAD method. A Δ +  layer  was  realized  by  GeO2  doping  and  the Δ value  was  adjusted  by controlling the GeO2 dopant percentage for each layer during the VAD process. The VAD preform was drawn to a 125 μ m-diameter fiber with 2-layer UV-resin coating using a drawing tower. Fabrication results of the new NZ-DSFs are shown in table 4. The NZ-DSFs have large Aeff, of about 70 μ m 2 ,  and low dispersion slope of 0.06ps/nm 2 /km. Attenuation loss was as low as 0.17-0.18dB/km. The attenuation and  dispersion  spectra  are  shown  in  Figure  4.  As shown in the Figure 4, low attenuation loss and non-zero dispersion was obtained over a wide range of wavelengths including Raman pump region (for example, pumping around 1450nm for C-Band amplification). Attenuation loss over S, C and L-band was less than 0.215dB/km by reducing the Rayleigh scattering loss. The total non-zero dispersion region remained lower than conventional SMFs (about 16ps/nm/km) was about 200nm. The cutoff wavelength (2m cutoff wavelengths were 1228nm for No.1 and 1295nm for No.2) and bending loss were low enough to realize wide-band transmission. From these results, ultra wide-band transmission and Raman amplification adaptability were successfully achieved with this fiber.

Figure 4. Attenuation and dispersion spectra.

Using the same type of profile, but with a depressed layer ( Δ 2) as shown in Figure 1-2, it is possible to reduce dispersion slope by sacrificing Aeff value. The depressed layer (about 0.15%)  was  controlled  by  F-doping.  Obtained  optical  properties  of  fabricated  fibers  are shown  in  table  5.  The  depressed  layer  is  advantageous  to  realize  lower  dispersion  slope. However, attenuation loss was increased due to interference between two dopants. Aeff was also reduced to about 60 μ m 2 .

As  confirmed,  large  Aeff  and  low  dispersion  slope  is  always  trade-off  relation.  To improve optical properties, new types of profile are required. For example, a large-Aeff MDF was realized  by  using  a  ring  profile  (Figure  1-3)  as  shown  in  table  6.  This  profile  is  very advantageous for the aspect of low local dispersion, dispersion slope and large Aeff as shown in [3-5]. The relation of dispersion slope and Aeff for various types of fibers are summarized in  Figure  5.  As  shown  in  the  Figure  5,  the  ring  profiles  clearly  have  an  advantage  for  this relationship. A splice loss problem was still a big issue because the electric field of this type of  fiber  was  quite  different  from  conventional  fibers.  For  this  problem,  we  developed  a thermal MFD expansion (MEX) splice technique. The MEX splice technique is very effective to  reduce  the  splice  loss  of  special  fibers.  By  using  the  MEX  splice,  the  problem  was successfully solved. However, the complicated profile resulted in high production costs and high attenuation loss, and it has been difficult to compete with conventional fibers up until now.

Figure 5. Relationship of dispersion slope and Aeff of various fibers.

<!-- image -->

## 2.4. New Challenges with SMFs

Since SMFs still have been used as a main fiber in terrestrial systems, optical-property improvements of SMFs are actively studied recently. There are two directions, namely the attenuation loss reduction and the enlargement of Aeff. PSCFs (Pure Silica Core Fibers) with low  attenuation  loss  have  been  reported  [6].  The  reported  loss  was  less  than  0.170dB/km. Low  attenuation  loss  of  these  fibers  was  realized  by  means  of  a  PSC  technique  with  a depressed clad, which could potentially become an issue in terms of mass-production. On the other hand, an attenuation loss reduction with GeO2/SiO2 fibers has also been investigated by optimizing  dopant,  which  contributes  to  Rayleigh  scattering  loss  [7-12].  Note  imperfection losses  of  these  fibers,  caused  by  the  glass  viscosity  mismatch,  were  also  optimized. Meanwhile,  as described, expansion of Aeff was also actively studied [13]. These developments were performed for submarine transmission systems at first, and also actively investigated  for  terrestrial  systems  now.  This  issue  will  be  discussed  in  detail  later.  Apart from  these  two  improvements,  namely  the  enlargement  of  Aeff  and  the  attenuation  loss reduction of SMFs, other types of SMFs have been developed for short reach applications including Fiber to The Home (FTTH). For example, new SMFs with an optimized dopantcomposition for the stimulated Bullillum scattering (SBS) suppression [14] as well as SMFs with a high bending resistance [15] have been also developed. However, we will not describe them  in  detail  in  this  chapter,  as  we  are  focusing  on  the  transmission  fibers  for  long-haul transmission this time.

## 3. CURRENT SUBMARINE FIBERS

## 3.1. Overall Situation

Large Aeff NZ-DSFs and reduced dispersion slope NZ-DSFs with local dispersion of -3 ~ -2ps/nm/km have been developed for submarine transmission systems (table 7) [16]. These

NZ-DSFs have been widely deployed in submarine systems, even though they (NZ-DSFs) have not been widely used in terrestrial systems. A low dispersion penalty is very important since  it  is  not  possible  to  use  DCF  modules  in  submarine  transmission  systems.  Instead, accumulated dispersion can be compensated by the SMF cables. However, since both NZDSFs and SMFs have positive dispersion slope, accumulated dispersion can be compensated only in the center wavelengths region, and other channels have larger accumulated dispersion as shown in Figure 6.

To solve the problems, Dispersion Management Line (DML), such as SMF+RDF or MDDML, have been proposed (Figure 7) [17,18]. The problem of a larger dispersion deviation and splicing issues, in the case of installment or repair, still needs to be addressed, but this type  of  fiber  has  many  advantages  for  the  aspect  of  low  dispersion  slope  and  large  Aeff. Therefore, submarine transmission lines will be replaced from the NZ-DSF-base to the DMLbase sooner or later. In actuality, the improved SMF+RDF (SLA+IDF) has already been used in real submarine systems.

Table 7. NZ-DSFs for submarine transmission systems

|               | Dispersion ps/nm/km   |   Slope ps/nm 2 /km |   Loss dB/km |   Aeff μ m 2 |
|---------------|-----------------------|---------------------|--------------|--------------|
| Large Aeff    | -2or-3                |                0.1  |         0.21 |           75 |
| Reduced Slope | -2or-3                |                0.05 |         0.21 |           50 |

Figure 6. Accumulated dispersion of submarine NZ-DSFs.

Figure 7. Accumulated dispersion of submarine NZ-DSFs and DMLs.

## 3.2. Using NZ-DSFs

To realize 10Gb/s ultra long-haul WDM transmission, reducing the dispersion penalty is very important. SMFs have low attenuation loss of about 0.19dB/km and large Aeff of about 80 μ m 2 , but dispersion is about 16ps/nm/km, and the transmission length or capacity has been limited by the dispersion property. So, a new type of NZ-DSFs has been developed to obtain large Aeff, small local dispersion and low dispersion slope.

At  first,  NZ-DSFs  were  designed  to  have  small  negative  local  dispersion  (-3  ~  2ps/nm/km) and Aeff as large as possible. The Dual Shaped Core (DSC) profile (Figure 1-4), which had been used for conventional DSFs, was selected because of its simple structure and feasibility for the VAD-based fabrication method. The DSC profile is also attractive for its aspect of low dispersion and large Aeff. For this profile, large b/a (wider side-core layer) is very  effective  in  spreading  its  electric  field  distribution,  which  results  in  large  Aeff,  but bending  loss  is  also  increased  with  enlarged  Aeff.  Therefore,  it  is  important  to  select parameters  which  maintain  the  low  bending  loss  property.  In  submarine  systems,  macrobending loss at 20mm diameter should be less than 10dB/m. In addition to that, whole CBand  (for  example,  1530nm-1570nm)  will  be  used  for  WDM  transmission,  so  cutoff wavelength should be less than 1530nm. The optical properties of a NZ-DSF fabricated by the VAD method are shown in table 8. Large Aeff of 84 μ m 2 was obtained, keeping low local dispersion. Low attenuation and low bending loss could also be obtained. Cutoff wavelength measured  by  a  2m  sample  was  about  1500nm.  However,  dispersion  slope  was  as  large  as 0.14ps/nm 2 /km. It would become difficult to realize wide-band WDM transmission with the fibers.  Therefore,  the  segment  core  profile  (Figure  1-1)  was  selected  to  reduce  dispersion slope. Adding a silica layer between Δ 1 and Δ 3 is effective in changing waveguide dispersion and reducing dispersion slope while keeping large Aeff. The fabrication process became more complicated due to the 3-layer core, but the simulation results showed lower dispersion slope than was possible with the DSC profile. Fabrication results of the Segment-core NZ-DSFs are also shown in table 8. As shown in the table 8, lower dispersion slope was obtained keeping large  Aeff,  about  the  same  level  as  DSC  NZ-DSF.  Cutoff  wavelength  measured  by  a  2m sample was confirmed to be about 1500nm. Attenuation loss over C-Band (1530nm-1570nm) was less than 0.23dB/km.

Attenuation  loss  of  the  NZ-DSFs  afterward  was  lowered  to  ~0.200dB/km  by  the  mass production. Not only large Aeff NZ-DSFs, but also NZ-DSFs with reduced dispersion slope were also developed for submarine systems with smaller Aeff of ~50 μ m 2 and dispersion slope of  ~0.05ps/nm 2 /km  using  the  segment  core  profile  (Figure  1-1). Attenuation  loss of ~0.200dB/km was obtained for the reduced slope NZ-DSFs as well. These two NZ-DSFs, a large  Aeff  type  and  a  reduced  slope  type,  are  sometimes  used  by  combining  each  other  to satisfy reasonably low dispersion slope of ~0.07ps/nm 2 /km and large Aeff of about 65 μ m 2 . Even  though  DMLs,  such  as  SMF+RDF  and  MD-DMLs,  have  been  developed  to  replace these types of NZ-DSFs, NZ-DSFs have still been widely used in current submarine systems.

## 3.3. SMF+RDF (DMLs) vs. NZ-DSFs for Submarine Systems

## 3.3.1. SMF+RDF

To realize long-haul high-capacity WDM transmissions, a new type of transmission fiber, reverse  dispersion  fiber  (RDF),  was  proposed  [17].  RDFs  have  negative  dispersion  and negative dispersion slope, the reverse of those of SMFs and a ratio of dispersion and its slope (D/S) around 1.55 μ m is almost equal to that of SMFs, so wide-band dispersion flatness will be obtained with SMF plus RDF. The transmission line consists of SMFs and RDFs called DML or Dispersion Management Fiber (DMF). SMFs have such attractive optical properties, such as low attenuation loss, low non-linearity and low Polarization Mode Dispersion (PMD) as  described before. Initial RDFs were designed considering a dispersion compensation for standard SMFs by approximately 1:1 length ratio. Obtained dispersion flatness by the SMF plus  RDF should be indicated by equation (1).  The  closer  the  CR  (Compensating  Rate)  to 100%, the wider dispersion flatness will be obtained in a total line.

<!-- formula-not-decoded -->

In  other  words,  the  Ratio  of  Dispersion  to  its  Slope  (D/S)  of  the  RDF  close  to  that  of SMFs,  is  desirable  for  wide  band  dispersion  flatness.  SMFs  and  RDFs  will  be  joined  to construct a network as shown in Figure 8. The connecting order would be lower non-linear SMFs after EDFAs, and high non-linearity RDFs after SMFs, to avoid non-linear problems.

To  obtain  the  desirable  dispersion  property,  the  W-shaped  profile  (Figure  1-5)  was chosen  and  parameters  were  optimized  [17].  Table  9  shows  typical  characteristics  of  the initial  RDFs  fabricated  by  the  VAD  method.  Typical  absolute  dispersion  was  20ps/nm/km and  a  high  compensating  rate  for  SMFs  was  confirmed.  Attenuation  loss  was  about 0.240dB/km.  Other  optical  properties,  such  as  cutoff  wavelength,  bending  loss  and  PMD, were also confirmed to be good enough for submarine transmission systems.

Figure 8. An example of system configuration consists of SMF+RDF.

By combining SMFs and RDFs, zero and flat dispersion can be obtained in C-Band. Total Aeff, namely equivalent Aeff, calculated by considering fiber non-linearity and power density using equation (2), was about 50 μ m 2 .

<!-- formula-not-decoded -->

Even though SMF+IDF showed low attenuation loss in C-band, large splice loss was one of the big problems, which needs to be solved. Splice loss of SMFs plus initial RDFs with a commercially  available  splice  machine  was  about  1.0dB/splice,  because  of  the  mode  field diameter (MFD) and delta mismatches. However, by using the MEX splice, splice loss of the SMFs plus RDFs became about 0.1dB as shown in Figure 9. This technique is also effective for  various  types  of  RDFs  described  later.  The  biggest  difference  between  NZ-DSFs  and DMLs are wavelength dependence of dispersion. A DML composed of SMF plus RDF has very small wavelength dependence of dispersion, so the SMF plus RDF is advantageous for wide-band WDM transmission. Many transmission experiments results have proved the high performances of the SMF plus initial RDF [19,20]. For example, 10Gb/s, 16ch., and 8,303km transmission was reported in reference [19]. The cabling test using RDFs also showed reliable performances [21,22].

However,  even  with  DML,  further  optical  performance  improvements,  such  as  lower non-linearity, have been required to realize higher capacity transmission. So, in addition to the  improvements  of  RDFs,  the  optical  properties  improvements  of  SMFs  had  also  been required  to  realize  high-capacity  trans-oceanic  transmissions  [23-32].  From  here,  improved SMFs and RDFs for the submarine systems will be discussed and explained.

Figure 9. A splice loss distribution of SMF+RDF by the MEX technique.

<!-- image -->

## 3.3.2. EA-SMF

As described, SMFs are characterized with attenuation loss of about 0.19dB/km and Aeff about 80 μ m 2 , and these optical properties are very attractive to obtain low non-linearity in a total line. However, on the other hand, equivalent Aeff of SMFs plus initial RDFs was about 50 μ m 2 ,  still  small  compared  to  submarine  NZ-DSFs'  combination  with  equivalent  Aeff  of ~65 μ m 2 , for example. To suppress non-linear phenomena in a total line, small dispersion or large Aeff would be required for SMFs. Therefore, a new type of SMF (Enlarged Aeff SMF: EA-SMF)  has  been  developed.  EA-SMFs  were  designed  to  achieve  similar  dispersion  as conventional SMFs, because large dispersion would shorten the SMF length ratio and cause higher non-linearity and higher loss in a total line. In addition to this, if dispersion was too high, large accumulated dispersion would be generated, and it would become an issue in the case  of  high-bit-rate  transmission.  The  Dual  Shaped  Core  (DSC)  profile  (Figure  1-4)  was selected and optimized to have as large Aeff as possible. The DSC profile is also attractive in terms of its low dispersion and large Aeff, because an electrical field is distributed not only in the centre core but also the side-core region. A wide range of parameters were studied for the DSC-profile.  Small Δ 1  is  generally  advantageous,  because  large  Aeff  can  be  obtained  by decreasing Δ 1. On the other hand, fibers become weak against bending loss if Δ 1 is too low, so appropriate Δ 1 needs to be chosen. By optimizing the profile, Aeff of about 100 μ m 2 and dispersion as low as conventional SMFs were obtained. Cutoff wavelength and bending loss were also  designed  to  be  adaptable  for  submarine  systems  by  optimizing  every  parameter. Note in submarine systems, the electrical power supply for amplifiers is one of the limiting factors,  so  in  most  cases,  only  C-Band  is  used  for  submarine  transmissions,  though  many experiments in laboratories use both C-Band and L-Band. A fabrication result of an EA-SMF is shown in table 10. Large Aeff of 100 μ m 2 was obtained keeping low dispersion property. Total non-linearity would be improved by using the EA-SMF instead of conventional SMFs. Dispersion slope and D/S was also kept at about the same level as conventional SMFs, so both dispersion and dispersion slope would be compensated by RDFs. Low attenuation, low bending loss and low PMD could also be obtained. Cutoff wavelength measured by a 22m sample was about 1500nm.

Table 10. Fabrication results of an EA-SMF

|        | Dispersion   | Slope       | D/S   | Loss   | Aeff 2   | PMD    | Bend   |
|--------|--------------|-------------|-------|--------|----------|--------|--------|
|        | ps/nm/km     | ps/nm 2 /km | nm    | dB/km  | μ m      | ps/rkm | dB/m   |
| EA-SMF | 17.5         | 0.062       | 286   | 0.189  | 100      | 0.04   | 5      |

Figure 10. Attenuation loss spectrum of an EA-SMF.

<!-- image -->

The attenuation spectrum of the EA-SMF is shown in Figure 10. As shown in the figure, very  low  and  flat  characteristics  could  be  confirmed  over  a  wide  range  of  wavelengths. Attenuation loss of 1550nm was about 0.19dB/km and attenuation loss, which was less than 0.20dB/km, was ranged over 100nm, so flat and low attenuation would be obtained in a total line. The stability and mechanical test for submarine cable requirements also confirmed and showed good performances. Note, even though dispersion became a bit larger, EA-SMFs with the W-shaped profile (Figure 1-5), which had similar optical properties, was also developed [32].

Figure 11. Profiles of Conventional EA-SMFs(Left) and FEA-SMFs(Right).

<!-- image -->

Table 11. Fabrication results of an FEA-SMF

|         | Dispersion   | Slope       | D/S   | Loss   | Aeff   | PMD    | Bend   |
|---------|--------------|-------------|-------|--------|--------|--------|--------|
|         | ps/nm/km     | ps/nm 2 /km | nm    | dB/km  | μ m 2  | ps/rkm | dB/m   |
| FEA-SMF | 19.5         | 0.060       | 323   | 0.169  | 105    | 0.04   | 5      |

Figure 12. Attenuation loss spectrum of an FEA-SMF.

<!-- image -->

As described above, EA-SMFs had successfully enlarged Aeff, keeping other characteristics about the same level as conventional SMFs by using the DSC profile. The pure silica core (PSC) method or the fully fluorine doped (FF) method was applied for EA-SMFs as shown in Figure 11 to reduce the Rayleigh scattering loss of EA-SMFs. Most of the light transmits through inside the center-core, so low absolute Δ1 as shown in the Figure 11 is very effective  to  reduce  Rayleigh  scattering  loss,  which  is  strongly  affected  by  dopants  as described  previously.  The  profile  structure  was  kept  the  same  as  conventional  EA-SMFs, which meant almost the same properties with EA-SMFs would be expected, except for lower attenuation  loss.  Fully  Fluorine  doped  EA-SMF  (we  will  call  it  FEA-SMF  hereafter)  was fabricated by the VAD method again. In the case of FF method, a Δ - layer was realized by fluorine doping and a little amount of fluorine was added to the centre core region, to adjust the viscosity of the core region. Otherwise, the center core takes a lot of tension during the drawing and the imperfection loss would be increased. Fabrication results of an FEA-SMF are shown in table 11. The attenuation loss spectrum of the FEA-SMF was shown in Figure 12. As shown in the table 11 and the Figure 12, very low attenuation loss was obtained, keeping other optical properties about the same level as the conventional EA-SMFs. Loss at the lowest wavelength  was  0.166dB/km.  Again,  PSC  or  FF  EA-SMFs  with  a  W-shaped  profile  have been developed and proposed [24]. Actually, since the W-shaped profile has a better bending loss performance, it is more widely used as a standard EA-SMF and FF/PSC EA-SMF. Even though the SMF plus initial RDF was advantageous for the aspect of low dispersion slope and the FWM suppression, total equivalent Aeff was about 50 μ m 2 , because Aeff of RDFs is small compared to SMFs or NZ-DSFs. With the development of EA-SMFs with Aeff of &gt;100 μ m 2 , equivalent Aeff of the EA-SMF plus initial RDF was enlarged to 55 μ m 2 .

## 3.3.3. ED-RDF

Improvements of RDFs have been also studied using a new profile, namely W-side-core profile  (Figure  1-2).  At  first,  attenuation  and  non-linearity  improvements  of  RDFs  keeping low  local  dispersion  of  about  -20ps/nm/km  were  investigated.  The  W  plus  side-core  layer profile was carefully optimized, keeping dispersion of about -20ps/nm/km and D/S value of about 300nm. In particular, optimum delta was carefully chosen, because delta has a close

relationship  with  Aeff  and  attenuation  loss.  We  also  designed  it  to  have  low  bending  loss, lower than 5dB/m at 20mm diameter, because bending-loss influenced to attenuation loss of 1.55 μ m in the case of initial RDFs. A low-loss RDF (LL-RDF) was carefully fabricated to obtain  very  low  loss  considering  the  before-mentioned  points.  OH  induced  loss  was  also suppressed to prevent the 1.55 μ m loss increase by the fabrication process optimization. The results  of  the  fabrication  are  shown  in  table  12.  Attenuation  loss  was  at  the  low  value  of 0.205dB/km, keeping the same level of local dispersion as initial RDFs. Aeff was enlarged to 34 μ m 2 , so non-linear phenomena would be suppressed. Cutoff wavelength measured at 22m was  about  1400nm,  so  wide-band  transmission  would  be  realized.  Figure  13  shows  the attenuation  spectrum  of  an  LL-RDF  (Loss  spectrum  of  initial  RDF  is  also  shown  for comparison). As shown in the Figure 13, wide-band flatness was confirmed. OH induced loss was relatively small (about 1.1dB/km) at 1385nm peak and loss in C-band and L-band was much lower than the loss properties obtained by conventional RDFs. The lowest attenuation loss was less than 0.20dB/km (0.196dB/km at the lowest wavelength); about the same level as NZ-DSFs. This is the lowest loss in dispersion compensating type fibers.

The total  optical  properties  of  EA-SMF  plus  LL-RDF  and  those  of  conventional  SMF plus RDF are shown in table 13. As shown in the table 13, ultra low attenuation loss, as low as  0.197dB/km,  about  zero  dispersion  slope  and  large  equivalent  Aeff  of  70 μ m 2 ,  were obtained simultaneously in a total line. These characteristics showed much improvement from the initial SMF+RDF.

Figure 14 shows the wavelength dependences of dispersion and attenuation in C-Band. As  shown  in  the  Figure  14,  dispersion  deviation  from  1530nm  to  1570nm  was  only  +/0.05ps/nm/km, which is even more flat than conventional SMFs plus RDFs, and the value is a typical value of current DMLs. The total attenuation loss was lower than 0.205dB/km. Both showed very flat properties over a wide range of wavelengths. In conclusion, merits of using the new profile were successfully confirmed by LL-RDFs.

However, equivalent Aeff can be more drastically improved by changing the length ratio of  SMFs  vs.  RDFs.  To  change  the  length  ratio,  RDFs  with  enlarged  absolute  dispersion values  (ED-RDFs)  were  developed.  The  W  +  side-core  profile  (Figure  1-2)  was  selected again  to  enlarge  both  Aeff  and  absolute  dispersion,  while  preventing  the  bending  loss increase.  Every  parameter  was  carefully  optimized  by  the  computer  simulation  and  the simulation  results  realized  dispersion  of  -45ps/nm/km  and  -60ps/nm/km,  keeping  a  good compensation  rate.  We  will  call  this  type  of  RDF,  E45-RDF  and  E60-RDF,  hereafter. Fabrication results are shown in table 14 and both negative dispersion and dispersion slope could be successfully achieved. Large Aeff of about 28 μ m 2 was obtained for E45-RDF and 23 μ m 2 for  E60-RDF. The W plus side-core layer profile successfully prevented attenuation and bending loss increase.

Figure 14. Attenuation loss and dispersion spectrum of a transmission line with an EA-SMF plus an LL-RDF.

The total characteristics of the link with EA-SMFs and the newly developed ED-RDFs connecting to manage dispersion at 1.55 μ m to about zero in a 50km span is shown in table 15.  Splice  loss  was  assumed  as  0.1dB  that  was  a  typical  value  of  DMLs  using  the  MEX technique.  From  the  calculation,  total  equivalent  Aeff  was  enlarged  to  about  75 μ m 2 .  Total dispersion slope was less than 0.01ps/nm 2 /km, so dispersion deviation would be quite small, less  than  +/-0.05ps/nm/km  in  whole  C-band,  which  was  almost  same  deviation  as  those obtained with the EA-SMF plus LL-RDF. Because EA-SMFs had attenuation loss of about 0.19dB/km at 1.55 μ m and the length of ED-RDFs had been shortened, total attenuation loss was about 0.205dB/km.

At this stage, since E45-RDF is the most matured and showed the best performance, it is widely  used  as  a  standard  RDF  now.  IDFs  (Inverse  dispersion  fibers),  which  have  local dispersion of between -45 to -40ps/nm/km and even larger Aeff, have been developed and commercialized now.

## 3.4. Development of MD-DMLs (P-MDF+N-MDF)

## 3.4.1. Initial MDFs

To solve the trade-off between flat dispersion and large Aeff with NZ-DSFs, a new type of transmission line, Dispersion Management Line (DML), consists of SMF plus RDF, has developed  and  showed  very  good  performance.  Using  the  new  SMF  plus  RDF,  many transmission experiments results  have  been  reported,  and  high  performance  was  confirmed with the SMF plus RDF [33-39]. However, to realize high-bit-rate transmissions over 40Gb/s, very special  techniques  were  required,  such  as  Raman  amplification,  special  signal  format, advanced FEC, fiber multiple configurations, to name a few. To realize 40Gb/s transmission without such special techniques, low accumulated dispersion fibers were also required as the next generation DML [18].

A new type of transmission line, namely MD-DML, which consists of Positive Medial Dispersion  Fiber  (P-MDF)  and  Negative  Medial  Dispersion  Fiber  (N-MDF),  has  been proposed to respond to these requirements [18,40]. MDFs have medial absolute dispersion of NZ-DSF  and  SMF,  and  successfully  suppress  large  accumulated  dispersion  without  any particular non-linear problems in a total line. The typical optical properties of conventional PMDF and N-MDF are summarized in table 16.

Figure 15. Accumulated disperis on characteristics (50km span).

|-------------------------------|-----------|-----------|

| Clad non-circularity [%]      | 0.1       | 0.1       |
| Core ovality [mm]             | 0.1       | 0.1       |
| Loss [dB/km] @1550nm          | 0.20      | 0.22      |
| Dispersion [ps/nm/km] @1550nm | 13        | -13       |
| Slope [ps/nm 2 /km] @1550nm   | 0.07      | -0.07     |
| λ cc [nm]                     | 1500      | 1500      |
| Aeff [ μ m 2 ] @1550nm        | 95        | 32        |
| Bend loss at 20f [dB/m]       | 10        | 10        |
| PMD [ps/rkm]                  | 0.04      | 0.06      |

Table 16. Typical characteristics of initial MD-DMLs

<!-- image -->

The  schematic  accumulated  dispersion  characteristics  of  various  types  of  transmission links  are  shown  in  Figure  15.  As  shown  in  the  Figure  15,  the  MD-DML  is  advantageous because of its low accumulated dispersion compared to the SMF plus RDF. The maximum accumulated  dispersion  of  50km  span  is  only  350ps/nm,  which  is  only  55%  of  SMF  plus RDF. So, this new type of link was confirmed to be very suitable for the high-bit-rate WDM transmission.  However,  calculated  equivalent  Aeff  was  about  60-65 μ m 2 ,  smaller  than  the SMF+RDF (more than 70 μ m 2 ). The accumulated dispersion of MDF link had certainly been suppressed, but this was still large compared to NZ-DSF as shown in Figure 15. So, the new types of MDFs have been required.

## 3.4.2. Improved MDFs

To solve this problem, Enlarged Aeff MDF (MDF EA ), with new types of index profiles, was selected and fabricated [41], because it was difficult to improve optical performances, such as Aeff and local dispersion, with conventional type profiles. Selected index profiles of P-MDF EA and N- MDF EA are shown in Figure 16. With these complicated new profiles, we carefully optimized every profile parameter to obtain suppressed absolute dispersion to about 10ps/nm/km  and  large  Aeff.  From  the  simulation,  Aeff  of  110 μ m 2 and  40 μ m 2 each  was obtained, keeping absolute dispersion of about 10-11ps/nm/km. Based on the simulation, both P- and N-MDF EA were actually fabricated by the VAD method. Fabrication results are shown in table 17.

Figure 16.1 A profile of P-MDF EA . Figure 16.2 A profile of N-MDF EA .

|----------|-----------------------|---------------------|----------|--------------|--------------|--------------|-------------|

Aeff was enlarged to about 110 μ m 2 and 40 μ m 2 each, keeping low absolute dispersion of about 11ps/nm/km. However, fibers with complicated profile like MDF EA tend to have large attenuation loss and large PMD. Actually, attenuation loss of P-MDF EA was relatively large, 0.245dB/km,  but  that  of  N-MDF EA were  suppressed  to  a  low  level.  PMD  was  also  low enough for both P- and N-MDF EA . The other big issue is splice loss of MDF EA . As described, centre-depressed profiles as shown in the Figure 16 tend to generate high splice loss because the  electric  field  deviates  from  Gaussian  distribution.  Figure  17  shows  an  example  of  the electric field of P-MDF EA . However, splice loss lower than 0.1dB were obtained by using the MEX  splice  technique.  Total  characteristics  of  P-MDF EA plus  N-MDF EA assuming  to  be connected about 1:1 length was shown in table 18. As shown in the table, ultra low dispersion slope  as  0.002ps/nm 2 /km  and large equivalent Aeff of about  90 μ m 2 were obtained simultaneously. Attenuation loss was 0.228dB/km in total.

|         |   Dispersion ps/nm/km |   Slope ps/nm 2 /km |   D/S nm |   Loss dB/km |   Aeff μ m 2 |   PMD ps/rkm |   Bend dB/m |
|---------|-----------------------|---------------------|----------|--------------|--------------|--------------|-------------|

Even though a very high performance was realized by MDF EA ,  high attenuation loss as well as complicated profile became one of the biggest hindrances to compete with EA-SMF + E45-RDF.  Further  improvements  of  MD-DMLs  were  attempted  to  compete  with  EASMF+E45-RDF, so some of the studies will be introduced briefly.  As  for  P-MDF,  further enlargement of Aeff from the P-MDF EA was also investigated using the same index profile as P-MDF EA [42].  Super  large  Aeff  of  ~150 μ m 2 could  be  obtained  as  a  limitation  value  by shifting effective cutoff wavelength ( λ cc) to around 1550nm within the bending loss limit. It means that it is multi-mode in a short length, but after transmitting few tens or hundreds of meter,  it  becomes  SM.  As  shown  in  table  19,  dispersion  around  10ps/nm/km  and  Aeff  of 150 μ m 2 ,  could  be  realized.  Attenuation loss, PMD and every other characteristic were also confirmed to be sufficient. λ cc (measured with a 100m sample) was little less than 1550nm for both fibers.

With N-MDFs, drastic improvements seemed to be very difficult, considering trade-off relations with low dispersion slope, large Aeff, low attenuation and low bending-loss by the conventional method. So, new methods will be required to further improve the characteristics of  conventional  N-MDFs.  We  have  noticed  a  phenomenon  that  in  some  cases,  only fundamental mode transmits through D- even if D- was designed as multi-mode, in the case of connecting D- after a single mode fiber. We considered using this phenomenon and call this  method a  MDST method (Multi-mode Design and Single-mode Transmission method) hereafter. Figure 18 shows the concept of the MDST method. This MDST method is effective for  negative dispersion fiber as N-MDFs, because N-MDFs are always used by connecting after  P-MDFs, which have single mode characteristics. By using this new concept (MDST method), we actually designed and fabricated a new type of N-MDF. The fabrication results of the new N-MDF, with typical optical properties of conventional N-MDFs for comparison, were  shown  in  table  20.  Optical  properties  were  drastically  improved  by  using  the  new method. Figure 19 shows the attenuation loss spectrum of the fabricated N-MDF. Attenuation was  measured  by  connecting  N-MDFs  with  SMFs.  Then,  we  obtained  the  attenuation spectrum of the N-MDFs by subtracting the attenuation of the SMFs from the total loss. A low and flat attenuation spectrum over S, C and L-Band were confirmed. When we measured attenuation with only N-MDF (W/O SMF), attenuation loss was increased by the influence of

higher  order  modes  as  shown  in  a  dotted  curve  of  Figure  19.  Therefore,  this  method  is applicable only for fibers such as N-MDFs and RDFs, which are used by connecting them after single mode transmission fibers as P-MDFs.

Even  though,  these  special  techniques  realized  quite  high  performance,  they  have  not been used in the real field. As for the P-MDFs, even though super large Aeff of 150 μ m 2 has been  achieved,  high  attenuation  loss,  in  addition  to  the  complicated  profile,  prevents  them from achieving the real field deployment. As for the MDST N-MDFs, dispersion and PMD could be measured relatively stably in cases measuring with SMF, but still have little noise. In addition  to  that,  some  different  designs  have  very  large  noise  with  dispersion,  PMD  and attenuation  loss,  so  the  designs  must  be  carefully  optimized  to  avoid  such  an  undesirable effect.  Though  this  method  looks  quite  attractive,  we  still  need  deeper  investigations  to achieve the real field deployment.

## 3.5. New Challenges with DMLs

As described, even though various types of SMFs, RDFs and MDFs have been developed and their performances have been improved, the standard DML, which is EA-SMF + E45RDF, has been continuously used. One reason is that the developments and improvements of new  signal  format  make  dispersion  tolerance  much  higher.  Therefore,  the  necessity  of dispersion  reduced  fibers,  such  as  MDF,  has  declined.  On  the  other  hand,  non-linearity  is considered as a big issue that needs to be addressed. It is the same trend as terrestrial systems. Among them, the loss reduction of EA-SMFs is of great interest. A PSC EA-SMF with the lowest loss of ~0.15dB/km, have been reported [29]. This value seems to be very close to the silica-glass limit considering actual fabrication process. The loss reduction, in addition with the enlargement of Aeff, which will be described in detail later, is a very hot topic even now. The loss reduction and the enlargement of Aeff with E45-RDF have also studied and the same type of fiber, namely IDF, has achieved attenuation loss of 0.235dB/km and Aeff more than 30 μ m 2 [32].

## 4. FUTURE TRANSMISSION FIBER

## 4.1. Overall Situations

Taking these current situations, which were described before, into account, there seem to be  three  important  R&amp;D  directions  that  need  to  be  considered:  (1)  Enlarging  Aeff,  (2) Widening the transmission band, and (3) Using Space Division Multiplexing (SDM). We will describe these three investigations hereafter.

## 4.2. R&amp;D on Low Non-Linear Fibers

Recently,  it  is  said  that  the  maximum  transmission  capacity  and  distance  is  limited primarily  by  nonlinear  effects.  Consequently,  any  technology  that  enables  significantly enhanced  capacity  and  distance  to  be  transmitted  without  nonlinear  distortion  is  of  great interest  for  the  further  development.  Several  studies  on  enlarging  Aeff  of  fibers  have  been performed and some research results will be shown.

## 4.2.1. SMF with a large fiber diameter

As  aforementioned,  one  approach  to  improve  the  optical  performance  with  SMF/EASMF is to enlarge Aeff, and the other approach is to reduce the attenuation loss, which have been  described  previously.  However,  the  enlargement  of  Aeff  generally  accompanies  both Macro-  and  Micro-bending  loss increase, and attenuation loss will be increased by macro/micro-bending loss as a result. It is well known that micro-bending loss is influenced by Δ ,  mode filed diameter (MFD), fiber diameter, coating diameter, and Young modulus of silica  glass  and  coating.  In  other  words,  micro-bending  loss  can  be  suppressed  by,  for example,  enlarging  fiber  diameter.  Therefore,  we  focused  on  obtaining  both  low  macrobending  loss  and  very  large  Aeff  simultaneously.  Since  loss  is  also  very  important,  we

selected to use the FF method, so low attenuation loss would be expected. The W shaped core profile (Figure 1-4) was selected for this investigation, because this profile is advantageous to obtain large Aeff, keeping low macro-bending loss. As for profile parameters, low Δ 1 is very effective to realize very large Aeff as shown in the Figure 20. However, as described, low Δ 1 generally  accompanies  macro-bending  loss  increase,  so  we  chose  the  profile  parameters, which realized bending loss at 20mm φ to be 5dB/m. In the case of profile shown in the Figure 20, Aeff of 160 μ m 2 was realized with Δ 1 around 0.2% and cutoff wavelength of 1460nm.

Figure 20. A simulation result of LA-SMFs with the W-shaped profile.

<!-- image -->

Figure 21. Relations of cutoff wavelength and Aeff.

<!-- image -->

Table 21. Fabrication results of a large fiber diameter FEA-SMF

|            | Dispersion   | Slope       | Cutoff   | Loss   | Aeff   | PMD    | Bend   |
|------------|--------------|-------------|----------|--------|--------|--------|--------|
|            | ps/nm/km     | ps/nm 2 /km | nm       | dB/km  | μ m 2  | ps/rkm | dB/m   |
| LD-FEA-SMF | 21.7         | 0.061       | 1462     | 0.173  | 159    | 0.04   | 4      |

Figure 22. Attenuation loss of a LD-FEA-SMF.

<!-- image -->

Δ 2 or ratio of diameter (b/a) did not have much impact on Aeff property, but effects the macro-bending loss property, so these parameters were also optimized every time. Figure 21 summarizes simulation results in terms of cutoff wavelength vs. Aeff. This time, a fiber with Aeff  of  160 μ m 2 and  cutoff  wavelength  of  1460nm  (the  edge  of  S-Band)  was  selected  and fabricated. The expected MFD would be about 1.2times larger than conventional EA-SMFs, so the fiber diameter was set to ~1.2 times larger than the conventional value of 125 μ m. The obtained optical properties are shown in table 21. By using the large fiber diameter method, micro-bending loss was suppressed, and attenuation loss of 0.173dB/km and ultra large Aeff as 159 μ m 2 were realized simultaneously. Bending loss at 20mm φ was ~5dB/m keeping cutoff wavelength and PMD low enough. Dispersion became as large as 21.7ps/nm/km, but it could be compensated by RDFs or DCFs. Note if we choose higher cutoff wavelength, e.g. 1530nm, it is possible to enlarge Aeff further. However, we need to use an even larger fiber diameter, which deteriorates the fiber reliability, in order to prevent micro-bending loss increase.

Attenuation  loss  spectrum  is  shown  in  Figure  22.  Low  attenuation  loss  was  confirmed over a wide range of wavelengths without any significant loss increase due to the macro- or micro- bending loss increase. OH loss is little larger than conventional EA-SMFs or FEASMFs, because the electrical field is spread for not only silica core region but also depressed clad region, but still low, about 0.45dB/km. So, this SMF is very attractive for the aspect of large  Aeff  and  low  attenuation  loss.  By  permitting  the  larger  bending  loss  property,  even larger Aeff of 211 μ m 2 was realized and reported [43].

Other  than  W-shaped  profile,  fibers  with  a  trench  profile  were  also  studied  as  a  new generation EA-SMF. For example, a EA-SMF with a trench profile realized Aeff of 120 μ m 2 and attenuation loss of 0.185dB/km, without enlarging the fiber diameter [44].

## 4.2.2. Holey fibers

As  was  explained,  many  profile-  and  process-optimizations  have  been  done  with conventional  silica  EA-SMFs.  However,  it  is  approaching  the  limitation  by  using  the conventional silica-based solid fibers. It is interesting to speculate as to what the future holds for  the  development  of  new  transmission  fibers.  The  design  possibilities  provided  by microstructured holey and photonic band-gap fibers (HFs/PBGFs) [45] mean that these new fiber  types  could  emerge  as  one  of  the  biggest  candidates  for  next  generation  transmission

fibers. These fiber types have the potential to offer radical improvements in fiber properties including:  reduced  losses,  increased  mode  areas  and  increased  operational  bandwidths, because of the existence of air holes their refractive index is about 1.0 and they have large difference with silica glass. The technology progresses have made it possible to realize low attenuation loss even with HFs and PBGFs [46, 47].

So, a further improvement possibility of large Aeff fiber, using holey structure, will be discussed.  We  will  also  discuss  a  potential  of  PBGFs  later.  Figure  23  shows  the  cross sectional  structure  of  simulated  HFs.  As  shown  in  the  figure,  regularly  arranged  air  holes make  a  hexagonal  shape  and  the  signal  light  will  be  transmitted  around  a  centre  defect (central silica part). Then, by changing parameters, namely hole pitch ( Λ ) and hole diameter divided  by  pitch  (d/ Λ ),  we  examined  whether  further  optical  properties  improvement  from conventional  fibers  would  be  possible  by  mean  of  the  FEM  simulation.  There  are  many reports  on  developments  of  large  Aeff  HFs  used  for  fiber  laser  applications,  such  as amplification  media  and  high-power  delivery,  but  large-Aeff  HFs  for  telecommunications, which requires much lower macrobending loss, e.g. &lt;10dB/m at 20mm φ , have not been fully studied  yet.  An  example  of  simulation  results  are  shown  in  Figure  24.  The  limits  due  to macrobending loss and endlessly single mode guidance as well as a fiber profile, which can realize a comparable Aeff to EA-SMF (dot), are also shown in the Figure 24. As can be seen, HF designs offering comparable or even larger Aeff than EA-SMF are possible, which offer single mode  guidance  over  a  far broader wavelength  range  than is possible within conventional silica fibers. There are also reports that the micro bending losses potentially be suppressed by the existence of air holes [48]. Even though, more detailed study on macroand micro-bending loss properties of large-Aeff HFs will be needed in the future, it is also a very interesting issue whether HFs have higher resistance against fiber bending. In particular, if  they  can  reduce  microbending  loss  without  increasing  the  fiber  diameter,  they  are advantageous in terms of fiber reliabilities.

Figure 23. A cross section and profile parameters of holey fibers.

<!-- image -->

Figure 24. Relations of PCF parameters and Aeff.

<!-- image -->

## 4.2.3. Using Photonic Bandgap Fiber

Hollow Core Photonic Band Gap Fiber (HC-PBGF) technology offers a non-linearity per unit  length  that  can  be  one  to  two  orders  of  magnitude  less  than  that  of  solid  EA-SMFs. Moreover,  it  may  also  ultimately  offer  the  possibility  of  lower  transmission  losses  [49]. Assessing the potential impact of HC-PBG fibers in future transmission systems thus appears a  topic  worthy  of  consideration.  The  potentials  of  PBGFs  were  numerically  studied  by  the Finite Element Method (FEM) simulation. A hexagonal structure with d/ Λ of  about 0.97 to avoid the surface mode [50] as well as Λ of 4.05 μ m to have the photonic bandgap centring at around 1.55 μ m telecom band was selected. The core was formed by omitting the central 3layers  (19-cells)  in  order  to  have  small  overlap  of  the  field  and  the  silica  glass.  The  ring numbers of seven layers were chosen in order to suppress the confinement loss increase. The simulated properties are summarised in table 22 and Figure 25. As shown in the Figure 25 and the table 22, the overlap of the field and the air is 99.3% and the effective Aeff assuming n2 of 2.2x10 -20 m 2 /W (n2 of the silica glass) was calculated to be 100,00 μ m 2 (corresponding to the γ of 9.0*E-4) using the overlap value and an equation cited in ref [51].

Table 22. Optical properties of the simulated PBGF

| Layer   | Loss      | Dispersion   | Slope       | Aeff    | n2         | Power   |
|---------|-----------|--------------|-------------|---------|------------|---------|
|         | dB/km     | ps/nm/km     | ps/nm 2 /km | μ m 2   | m 2 /W     | %       |
| 7 layer | 3.1×10 -6 | 30.0         | 0.300       | 100,000 | 2.2×10 -20 | 99.3    |

|--------|------------------|-----------------------|---------------------|----------|--------------|-------------|

Since the PBGFs have a relatively large dispersion coefficient, about 30ps/nm/km, and small  Dispersion/Slope  (D/S)  of  around  100nm  even  with  the  idealized  case,  dispersion compensation  should  be  required  to  use  the  PBGFs  for  the  high-capacity  dense  WDM transmission.  A  W+side-core  profile  (Figure  1-2)  was  selected  and  carefully  optimized  to realize the dispersion compensation of PBGFs. Examples of the simulation results are shown in table 23 (Sim-1 to -5). As shown in the table 23, dispersions of from -150ps/nm/km to 350ps/nm/km  can  be  realized. Δ 2,  deeper  than  -0.7%,  is  a  key  point  to  realize  both  large dispersion and small D/S, of around 100nm simultaneously, while keeping low bending loss. We actually fabricated the DCF sim-1 as an example by the VAD method. Fabrication results of  a  3.0km  DCF  sample  are  shown  in  table  23  (Fab-1).  The  total  dispersion  of  the  PBGF (Simulation)  +DCF  fab-1  (Measured)  showed  the  small  residual  dispersion  such  as  +/0.5ps/nm/km  over  whole  C-band.  To  improve  the  system  performance,  larger  absolute dispersions are desirable and they can be realized as shown in table 23.

The performances of the PBGF + DCFs were evaluated by the VPIsysytems ® software and  compared  with  the  performances  of  conventional  SMF  +  HP-DCFs  [52].  A  system configuration (the SMF + HP-DCF case) is shown in Figure 26. The 32ch. CS-RZ signals with  channel  spacing  of  100GHz  were  input  (separately  for  odd  and  even  channels)  and amplified by the EDFAs with a gain the same as the total net losses of TL+DCF and an NF of 5dB. The optical properties of the fibers used for the simulation are summarized in table 24. We assumed a zero flat dispersion this time and the performances were evaluated by means of the power penalties at BER of 10 -9 from back-to-back. The ch.11, which should have enough non-linear  interference  with  other  channels,  was  selected  as  an  evaluation  sample  and  the input power was optimized to obtain the lowest power penalty every time. The power penalty of 2.1dB over 500km SMF (LEAF ® showed almost the same performance) was determined as a reference and the 500km PBGFs+DCFs (appropriate lengths) were evaluated by changing the attenuation losses of the PBGFs. An inset of Figure 27 shows the losses of the PBGF vs. power penalties  of  the  500km  PBGF  +  DCFs  and  Figure  27  shows  the  analyzed  penalties component of the SMF+DCF, LEAF+DCF and PBGFs (0.22dB/km and 0.30dB/km)+DCF. As shown in the figure, PBGFs + DCFs showed better performance than the conventional SMF+DCF or NZ-DSF+DCF even for the PBGF with high attenuation losses due to the low non-linearity  of  the  PBGF.  The  calculated  non-linear  phase  shift  [52]  of  the  PBGF+DCFs (assuming the loss of the PBGFs was same as the SMF) was smaller than the SMF+DCF by a factor of 25.5 as shown in table 25.

Other than PBGF+DCF WDM transmission systems, dispersion-shifted PBGFs for NonRepeatered  Transmission  Systems  (NRTS)  have  also  been  reported  [53].  In  this  paper,  a PBGF with 19-cell defect core and 7 layers of air holes with a d/ Λ of ~0.97 and Λ = 4.33 μ m was considered. λ 0 of the fundamental mode is 1.56 μ m and the dispersion of this fiber was 5.13ps/nm/km at 1550nm, meaning that it could be used as a negative-dispersion NZ-DSF at this  wavelength.  A  band  gap  was  centered  at  1650nm  for  which  the  overlap  of  the fundamental mode and air was 99.3%. In this paper, the high potentials of the PBGFs for long-haul non-repeater transmission were also demonstrated.

## 4.3. R&amp;D on Wide-Band Transmission Fibers

As discussed, fibers with enlarged Aeff (low non-linearity) are one important issue. The other important issue is widening the bandwidth. Holey fibers are of particular interest, since they  have  unique  properties,  e.g.  an  endlessly  SM  (ESM)  property,  which  is  difficult  to achieve by using solid TFs. For example, a new band, namely 1.0 μ m band, is recognized as the next generation band. However, SMFs have λ c around 1250nm, and they are difficult to be  adapted.  In  addition  to  that,  Aeff  of  conventional  solid  fibers  have  large  wavelength dependence. Meanwhile, a potential problem that HFs have is a bending loss increase from short wavelengths as shown in Figure 28.

Therefore, the structure of holey fibers needs to be carefully optimized. It was revealed by careful simulation that realizing large Aeff, keeping the ESM property and bending loss at 1050nm  &lt;5dB/m  (20mm φ ),  is  possible  [54].  By  using  the  optimized  HFs,  it  should  be possible to construct a new transmission system using conventional telecom band as well as new transmission band around 1.0mm, as shown in Figure 29. Even though loss reduction of HFs is  an  issue  needs  to  be  considered  by  fiber  process  optimization,  a  low-loss  HF  with attenuation loss of 0.18dB/km at 1550nm, which is comparable to conventional solid SMFs, has been reported [55]. Furthermore, by using HFs, an ultra wide-band transmission, not only telecom-band and 1.0 μ m band but also 800nm band and even visible band, has been reported [56, 57]. The results indicate, by using HFs, we can use any bands in the future, which opens the  possibility  for  ultra  high-capacity  transmissions  with  much  broader  new  transmission bands. To realize a wide-band high capacity transmission, DCFs will also be required. The development  of  DCFs,  which  can  compensate  dispersion  in  the  telecom  band  as  well  as 1.0 μ m band by using, e.g. HFs or HOM-fibers, is an important issue. A research result has been reported in [13]

## 4.4. R&amp;D on Multi-Core Fibers

## 4.4.1. Background

Since an enormous increase of future network traffic is expected, some transmission fiber breakthroughs are necessary. From these perspectives, the transmission lines with Pb/s/fiber capabilities have been reported using holey fibers (HFs) [56,57], as described in the previous section. Other breakthrough, which has been paid considerable attention, is multi-core fibers (MCFs)  to  realize  space  division  multiplexing  (SDM)  transmission.  Of  course,  multi-core holey  fibers  (MC-HFs),  where  each  core  can  realize  ultra  wide-band  transmission  and Pb/s/core transmission capability, have a potential for the ultimate high capacity [58,59]. An image of increasing capacity is summarized in Figure 30. Not only the transmission capacity, but  we  also  focused  on  the  possibility  to  extend  the  transmission  distance.  The  optimized design  had  the  transmission  distance  of  over  100  km  for  all  7  cores  keeping  Pb/s/core capacities. The results were also confirmed by sample-fiber fabrications.

Figure 30. An image of transmission capacity increase by using multi-core holey fibers.

<!-- image -->

## 4.4.2. Fiber design

Firstly,  to  realize  the  long-distance  SDM  transmission,  it  is  essential  to  propagate  the lights  through  each  core  individually.  To  estimate  the  interference  between  cores,  we calculated  a  coupling  length  of  the  MC-HFs  with  7-cores  as  shown  in  Figure  31.  The coupling  length  is  defined  as  the  length  where  100  %  of  the  optical  power  of  one  core transfers  to  another  core.  If  the  transmission  distance  is  much  shorter  than  the  coupling length,  the  interference  between  the  cores  can  be  ignored.  So,  we  defined  the  transmission distance as 1/100 of the coupling length and examined the relation against the core intervals (Figure 31). Here, the d/ Λ was set to be 0.43, where the strict endlessly single mode could be realized  [60]  and  the  results  in  the  case  of Λ of  5,  7,  9 μ m  were  compared.  From  the simulation, it was found that it would be possible to extend the transmission distance by over 100 km when enlarging the core intervals by more than 9 layers.

Figure 31. Multi-core fiber structure and simulation results.

<!-- image -->

Secondly, ring numbers at the outer region of the cores need to be optimized to suppress the  confinement  loss.  Simulation  results  revealed  that  at  least  5  layers  were  required  to suppress the confinement loss to &lt;0.01 dB/km at 1550 nm, when the d/ Λ was 0.43.

Last but not least, each core needs to be optimized to realize the wide band transmission characteristics for the ultra large capacity transmission. The  bandwidth  of  the  short wavelength region is limited by the macro bending loss. In spite of the limit, an ultra wide band transmission from 500 nm (visible region) to 1700 nm could be realized with low macro bending loss in the case of Λ = 5 μ m.

From these results, it was revealed that 7- cores MC-HF with d/ Λ = 0.43, Λ = 5 μ m, core interval = 10 layers and outer ring number = 5 layers could realize the transmission over 100 km in the wide band transmission region over 500-1700 nm.

## 4.4.3. Fiber fabrication

To  confirm  the  optical  properties  of  the  MC-HF,  a  MC-HF  with  7-cores  was  actually fabricated.  The  sample  length  was  1  km  and  the  cross-section  of  the  fabricated  MC-HF  is shown in Figure 32. A uniform and desirable structure could be obtained from the optimized fabrication process.

The optical properties, measured at the center core (core A) and one of the outer cores (core  B),  are  shown  in  the  table  within  the  Figure  32.  Almost  the  same  properties  as  the simulation results were confirmed. There were almost no discrepancies between core A and B and no influences of the confinement loss. The low macro bending losses were confirmed in the  wavelength  region  above  500  nm,  so  this  fiber  was  confirmed  to  be  applicable  for  the ultra wide band transmission. Crosstalk of two cores after 1km transmission was less than 60dB, so we could expect less -35dB crosstalk to be obtained after 100km transmission. This value matches the simulation results and is low enough for practical applications.

Even though multi-core HFs are attractive in that they can realize both multi-core and ultra  wide-band  transmission,  which  means  an  ultimate  high-capacity  potential,  multi-core solid fibers are also attractive, and have been actively studied recently as well [61].

| Fiber   | Loss   | Dispersion   | D. Slope   | Aeff   |
|---------|--------|--------------|------------|--------|
|         | dB/krn |              |            |        |
|         | 2.4    | 41.2         | 0.07 2     | 38.7   |
| Core B  | 24     | 40.2         | 0.071      | 41.8   |

## 5. SUMMARY

This  chapter  covered  two  topics.  One  is  the  current  situation  of  transmission  fibers  in terrestrial  and  submarine systems. Not only the current situation but the R&amp;D trials before reaching the current situation were also described with detailed R&amp;D examples. Second topic is  three  R&amp;D  directions  for  the  future.  Some  research  results  were  described  to  help understand the up-to-date R&amp;D situations.
