In: Transmission Lines: Theory, Types and Applications Editor: Dana M. Welton

Chapter 3

## RECENT DEVELOPMENTS IN TRANSMISSION POLE DYNAMIC ANALYSIS AND DESIGN

## Kaoshan Dai and Shen-En Chen

Department of Civil and Environmental Engineering, University of North Carolina at Charlotte, Charlotte, North Carolina

## ABSTRACT

Transmission  poles  are  extensively  used  as  support  structures  in  electric  power transmission and distribution lines. Design of pole structures primarily follows reliabilitybased procedures using stress analysis of these structures for weight, wind/ice loading, and effects either due to conductor breaking or due to collapse of adjacent structures.

Understanding  transmission  pole  dynamic  performance  is  essential  for  reliable design of these support structures against wind, earthquakes, and other time-variant loads. Fundamental frequencies used in design guidelines are mostly approximate values and lack the details to warrant actual pole behaviors. Therefore, field studies of transmission pole free vibration are presented in this chapter to provide the scientific basis for more detailed  designs.  Parameters  that  have  influence  on  the  pole  modal  behaviors,  such  as material properties, prestress, and boundary conditions, are discussed. Complex coupling issues  between  the  pole  and  the  power  line  are  also  investigated  along  with  an introduction to some simple but reliable approaches to model the transmission poles.

Commercial  blasts  can  induce  ground  movements,  air  vibrations,  flyrock  and subsidence.  There  is  a  lack  of  industry-wide  study  on  how  transmission  structures respond  to  blasting  and  there  are  no  widely  accepted  guidelines  to  regulate  blast operations near transmission structures. To help the power industry protect transmission assets  against  blast  impact,  an  approach  to  establish  a  blast  limit  based  on  actual structural  response  is  presented.  Details  of  ground  vibration  measurement,  structural response analysis, and blast design consideration are introduced, which provide scientific data and a systematic research procedure that allow engineers to design a blast plan based on actual structural responses.

ISBN: 978-1-61761-300-5

© 2011 Nova Science Publishers, Inc.

## INTRODUCTION

Pole  type  structures  are  widely  used  in  electrical  power  transmission  and  distribution lines  to  support  phase  conductors.  Transmission  poles  can  be  wood,  steel,  or  concrete. Although  wood  poles  remain  popular  for  relatively  shorter  span  and  lower  voltage  power lines, steel and concrete structures have become cost-effective options for greater magnitude loads.  Advances  in  pole  fabrication  techniques  have  led  to  the  increasing  availability  of stronger members such as tubular steel poles and prestressed concrete poles.

Design of transmission poles is primarily based on codes and guidelines [ASCE 1991, 2009; ASCE/PCI 1997; ASCE/SEI 2005]. Prevailing practices use reliability-based procedures by analyzing the transmission line for weight, wind/ice loading, and effects either due  to  conductor/insulator  breakage  or  due  to  collapse  of  adjacent  structures.  Prestressed concrete  poles  are  fabricated  by  centrifugal  casting  method.  The  design  of  prestressed concrete  poles  is  a  process  that  mainly  involves  considerations  of  the  ultimate  flexural strength, cracking strength, shear and torsion capacity, and pole deflection [ASCE/PCI 1997]. Tubular steel poles can be self-supporting or guyed structures. Design of a steel pole is based on ultimate strength method using factored design loads. General stability and local buckling are  one  of  the  main  design  concerns  for  a  pole  subjected  to  compressive  forces.  Tension, shear, bending and combined stresses caused by a combination of loads should not be beyond the material strength [ASCE/SEI 2006].

Typically, transmission structures are not designed for ground vibration because loading effects caused by wind/ice combinations and broken wires usually exceed earthquake effects [ASCE 1991, 2009]. However, this may change as a result of the increasing demand on wider transmission  line  spans-which  increase  the  criticality  of  the  overall  stability  of  a  single structure [Ghobarah et al. 1996; Li et al. 2005]. To date, transmission line responses against ground-induced vibrations have not been addressed by design codes or guidelines.

Dynamic  behaviors  of  transmission  structures  are  of  concern  since  wind  loads  are predominant in structural design considerations. Hence, vibrational characteristics of power lines as well as the support structures have been studied extensively. But most studies focused on  transmission  lattice  towers  and  cables,  very  few  reports  about  power  transmission  pole vibration can be found in published literature. In areas where commercial blasting is used for mining  or  construction,  nearby  power  transmission  facilities  can  be  endangered.  Blastinduced ground vibrations, air pressure, flyrock damage, and subsidence are typical concerns of a blasting operation. Ground motions caused by explosions are different from earthquakes in duration and dominant frequencies. Various aspects of blast impacts on transmission poles have been studied [Conner et al. 2007; Dai and Chen 2007a; Dai and Chen 2007b; Dai and Chen 2008; Dai and Chen 2009; Dai et al. 2009; Dai and Chen 2010a; Dai and Chen 2010b]. A summary of research results of transmission pole free vibration as well as pole structural responses under blast induced ground vibrations are presented in this chapter.

## STRUCTURAL DYNAMICS OF THE POWER TRANSMISSION SYSTEM

A transmission structure is designed to support a continuous system of overhead ground wires.  Free  vibration  behavior  of  a  pole  structure  is  controlled  by  the  structure's  mass,

stiffness, and damping. Since real structures have an infinite number of degree-of-freedoms, free vibration equations of motion of a power transmission line can be expressed as:

<!-- formula-not-decoded -->

where ሾ ܯ ሿ is  mass  matrix,  including  the  support  structure  and  its  sub-members,  attached wires, and hardware for an operative power line; ሾ ܿ ሿ is the damping matrix and for a viscous damping type that is usually used, it is a function of support structure foundation conditions, aerodynamic drag, member connections, hysteresis behavior of the members for a power line system; ሾ ܭ ሿ is  stiffness matrix and it is determined by transmission structure size, material, and foundation boundary. Power cables also contribute to stiffness matrix for the power line. ሼ ݔ ሷሽ , ሼ ݔ ሶሽ , and ሼ ݔ ሽ are acceleration, velocity, and displacement vector, respectively.

Free vibration of a structure can be described as natural frequencies and mode shapes. The natural frequency of a structure is the number of complete cycles the structure vibrates in one second. For a reasonable design, natural frequencies of transmission poles should avoid the dominant frequencies of the expected forcing function. Mode shapes are the structure's displacement  configurations  during  vibration.  Each  mode  shape  corresponds  to  a  natural frequency.  Typical  vibration  modes  for  a  transmission  structure  include  bending,  torsional, and sometimes, local vibration modes. Damping of structural vibration indicates the energy dissipation. Generally, there are three idealized damping (coulomb, viscous, and structural) and  for  civil  engineering  structures,  viscous  or  structural  damping  is  the  most  commonly used. Viscous damping is proportional to the velocity of structure vibration; while structural damping is caused by hysteresis loss during structure member vibration.

For  an  operational  power  line,  conductor  motions  can  be  coupled  with  the  support transmission  structure  vibration.  This  makes  the  entire  system  vibration  a  very  complex problem.  Moreover,  local  vibration  modes  such  as  individual  member  vibration,  combined with  main  structure  vibration,  may  lead  to  a  complicated  vibration  mechanism  for  the transmission  structure.  The  initiation  of  transmission  structure  vibrations  are  commonly caused by wind loads acting directly on the support structure or conductors, or by foundation excitations  due  to  ground  motions.  Forced  vibration  equations  of  motion  of  a  power transmission line can be expressed as:

<!-- formula-not-decoded -->

in which ሼ ܨ ሽ is the external force vector.

If the external forces are ground vibrations, equations of motion can be described as:

<!-- formula-not-decoded -->

where ݔ ௚ ሺ ݐ ሻ is ground acceleration.

ሷ

In power line design, conductor vibrations are sometimes controlled by dampers. Windinduced  vibrations  can  be  prevented  or  reduced  by  properly  installing  spacer  dampers  or tuned  mass  dampers.  Conductor  vibrations  include  aeolian,  subconductor  oscillation,  and galloping. The natural frequency range for aeolian motion is 3 to 150 Hz; for subconductor it

is 0.15 to 10 Hz; and for galloping it is 0.08 to 3 Hz. Conductor galloping can cause damages to flexible support structures. The approximate range in the fundamental frequency for freestanding poles is 0.5-1.0 Hz. For lattice towers and H-frame structures, they are 2.0-4.0 Hz and  1.0-2.0  Hz,  respectively.  But  these  values  are  based  on  a  limited  review  of  typical suspension structures. Damping is difficult to determine and 0.02 is a conservative estimate for the pole structure and 0.04 is used for lattice towers [ASCE 1991, 2009].

Typically, earthquakes are not included in the transmission structure design force because loads caused by wind/ice combinations generally exceed ground vibration effects induced by earthquakes.

## PREVIOUS STUDIES ON TRANSMISSION STRUCTURES

Dynamic behaviors of transmission structures focus on either their free vibrations or their responses under time-variant loads. Transmission towers are mostly studied. Both numerical simulations and physical experiments are used for dynamic analyses of power transmission structures.

Simplified  numerical  procedures  were  introduced  by  Ozono  et  al.  [1988]  to  study characteristics of in-plane free vibration. The tower-conductor coupled system was considered  in  their  model.  It  was  found  that  at  higher  frequencies,  a  group  of  natural frequencies of the coupled model was observed that are close to the natural frequencies of the same  freestanding  tower.  Ozono  et  al.  [1992]  further  examined  the  in-plane  dynamic interaction  between  a  tower  and  conductors  and  explained  mechanism  of  the  in-plane  free vibration of transmission line system. They found that the contribution of a conductor to the dynamic tension force transmitted to  a  tower  was  more  significant  among  transverse-wave modes.  Both  theoretical  and  experimental  studies  were  performed  by  Rao  et  al.  [2004]  to evaluate natural frequencies of the towers. The paper introduces a non-dimensional parameter to take into account effects of increased transmission tower deformations.

Forced vibrations are one of the main concerns for electric power transmission structure design.  The  complexity  caused  by  the  coupling  between  cables  and  supported  structures increases the difficulty in analyzing them. Field measurements of transmission line vibration were conducted by Momomura et al. [1997]. They collected data of tower vibration under wind  in  a  mountainous  area.  The  vibration  characteristics  of  the  tower  indicated  that conductors  had  strong  influence  on  tower  dynamic  behaviors.  The  authors  concluded  that mode shapes of the tower with conductors in a frequency range of 1 Hz or less were similar to the  fundamental mode shape of the tower without conductors attached. Yasui et al. [1999] studied  wind-induced  power  transmission  tower  vibration  coupled  with  power  lines.  They found that differences in the way how the tower supports the power line had an influence on the  response  characteristics.  It  is  shown  that  peak  factors  computed  from  a  time  series response  were  greater  than  those  computed  from  power  spectrum  density.  For  the  same support types, the peak factors of member axial forces were greater than those of transverse direction  displacements.  Structural  analysis  of  transmission  towers  under  wind  loads  were also  performed  by  Battista  et  al.  [2003]  through  the  finite  element  method.  Fundamental frequency of the transmission tower, useful in early design stages, could be estimated by a simplified two degree-of-freedom analytical model. To reduce the tower top horizontal along-

wind  displacements  in  the  cross-line  direction,  nonlinear  pendulum-like  dampers  were developed.  Savory  et  al.  [2008]  compared  wind-induced  foundation  loads  measured  on  a transmission line tower in the field with those calculated using design codes. The comparison indicated that existing design codes provided reliable transformation of the local wind effects to tower foundations.

Due to the lack of research in the area of seismic analysis of transmission lines, El-Attar [1997]  realized  the  significance  of  studying  the  response  of  transmission  lines  under earthquakes in order to evaluate current design codes. Both power lines and steel towers were modeled and seismic responses were calculated and compared with wind and ice load effects. Research  results  showed  that  displacements  and  internal  forces  in  transmission  lines  were substantial under ground vibration and it was suggested that cable motion during earthquakes should be included in the design of line clearances. The studies also indicated that forces in tower members due to earthquakes might exceed those caused by the wind loads. Li et al. [2005]  proposed  simplified  models  for  transmission  tower-lines  that  include  tower-line interaction. The validity of simplified models was examined through shaking-table experiments on scaled coupled transmission line systems. A simplified analysis method was proposed  for  seismic  response  calculation  of  the  coupled  tower-conductor  system.  It  was found that the models yielded better results for out-of-plane vibrations than those for in-plane vibrations.

Effects  due  to  conductors/insulators  breakage  are  another  loading  aspect  in  power transmission dynamics. McClure and Lapointe [2003] studied shock load effects on overhead power lines,  caused  by  conductor  rupture.  The  authors  successfully  applied  a  macroscopic modeling  approach  to  a  case  study,  which  could  capture  salient  features  of  shock  load propagation. The approach could be used to study other loading scenarios, such as sudden iceshedding  effects  from  conductors  or  sudden  failure  of  other  components  like  towers  or suspension  strings.  Using  ADINA,  Tucker  [2007]  studied  dynamic  effects  of  broken suspension insulator and multiple tower failures on power transmission lines. The numerical model for the scenario of a broken insulator failure was compared with the published fullscale test data. Numerical analysis of tower failure was validated through a small-scale test. The study provided a better understanding of cascade failure of transmission lines.

Although  there  is  a  variety  of  research  regarding  transmission  structure  responses  to dynamic loads, there is a lack of systematic study on the forced vibration of transmission pole structures. With the increasing usage of prestressed concrete poles and tubular steel poles in power  transmission  lines,  their  structural  responses  under  dynamic  loads  need  to  be understood.

## FREE VIBRATION OF THE POWER TRANSMISSION POLE LINES

Free vibration of power transmission poles is the vibrational behavior of these structures without loads. Free vibration parameters (natural frequencies and mode shapes) give insight information about dynamic behaviors and are necessary information in structural design of the power transmission structure under time variant loads. There is little published research discussing the free vibration of transmission pole structures: Lantrip [1995] and Chen et al. [2006]  reported  modal  tests  and  finite  element  analysis  on  different  size  concrete  poles;

Polyzois  et  al.  [1998]  used  the  finite  element  method  to  obtain  natural  frequencies  of composite  poles.  All  of  the  above  studies  were  based  on  free-free  or  cantilevered  beam models. Dai and Chen [2008; 2009] studied the effects of soil conditions on embedded poles as well as the coupling phenomena between the pole and the cables.

## Free Vibration of Single Poles

A series of studies on free vibration behaviors or modal behaviors of transmission poles are reported herein. Tests were conducted by impact excitation to seek eigensolutions of the pole  vibration.  Modal  analysis  was  also  performed  through  finite  element  modeling.  The sensitivity  of  some  physically  significant  parameters  on  modal  characteristics  of  concrete poles was numerically investigated.

Figure 1. Modal testing configurations.

<!-- image -->

Modal testing is an experimental technique that involves measuring frequency response functions (FRFs) of a structure. Impact modal tests use impact excitation to obtain FRFs that contain information about a system in forms of natural frequencies and mode shapes. Modal tests were performed on two prestressed concrete poles with different boundary conditions: simple-supported,  suspended,  and  embedded  with  soils  or  gravels  (Figure  1).  Testing equipments used in the experiments consist of accelerometers, a data acquisition system, and an instrumented hammer. The tests were conducted through mounting an accelerator on the structure (response outputs) and roving the instrumented hammer (force excitation inputs) as shown in Figure 2. Through analysis of the input-output data (Figure 3), the FRFs were then processed  to  extract  natural  frequencies  and  corresponding  mode  shapes.  Typical  mode shapes  of  pole  vibration  with  embedded  and  suspended  conditions  are  shown  in  Figure  4. Figure 6. Prestress effects on natural frequencies.

<!-- image -->

In order to investigate the sensitivity of frequency to prestress level, modal analysis was conducted with varied prestressing forces by introducing different initial stress in strands. The correlation between  prestress force  change  and the change  of corresponding natural frequencies is shown in Figure 6. It is indicated that the higher the mode, the more sensitive of  the  prestress  effect.  Overall,  the  research  concludes  that prestress  is  not  a  very  sensitive parameter for frequency shifts, which agrees with previous studies [Dallasta and Dezi 1996; Deak 1996; Hamed and Frostig 2006; Jain and Goel 1996]. Therefore, prestress effects can be neglected during numerical elastic modal analysis of prestressed concrete structures.

The  sensitivity  of  concrete  strength  was  studied  by  adjusting  the  concrete  modulus  of elasticity, which was obtained through Equation (4) [PCI 2004]. With the change of concrete strength,  natural  frequencies  of  pole  vibration  are  shown  in  Figure  7.  From  the  numerical investigation, it  was found that frequencies of the pole vibration deviate within 13% range when concrete strength varies from -40% to 30% of its original value (11000 psi).

<!-- formula-not-decoded -->

where: ܧ ௖ ( psi ) is concrete modulus of elasticity; ݂ ௖ ᇱ ( psi )  is  concrete compressive strength, and ݓ ௖ ( lb/ft 3 ) is concrete density.

Figure 7. Relation between concrete strength and pole natural frequencies.

<!-- image -->

Single pole structures are usually directly embedded. Loading conditions on an embedded pole may include high overturning moments with relatively low axial and lateral shear loads. Soil-structure interaction (SSI) effects could be considered by introducing soil springs, which represent the stiffness of annular material. Rojas-Gonzalez et al. [1991], for example, utilized the four-spring nonlinear subgrade modulus model to predict the load-deformation of direct embedded  poles.  Based  on  their  model,  with  linear  elastic  assumption,  soil-structure interaction can be modeled as a set of linear translational and rotational springs and dampers, which represent surrounding annular material (backfill materials and soil).

Assuming ሾKሿ is  the  system  stiffness  matrix,  which  is  composed  of  pole  structure stiffness matrix ( ൣK ୮ ൧ ) and linear soil spring stiffness matrix ( ሾK ୱ ሿ ). The governing equation for basic structural dynamic behaviors of the pole can be expressed in the form of mass and stiffness matrices:

where ൣM୮൧ is the mass matrix of the pole; and ሼRሽ is the boundary reaction.

where ߱ is natural frequency of the pole with soil-spring boundary conditions, and ሼφሽ is the corresponding mode shape vector.

Again ANSYS ® [2005] was used for parametric study: The pole structure was modeled with tapered beam elements; Boundary conditions were considered by adding a series of liner elastic massless springs along the bury part of the pole structure (Figure 8).

where ∆ ݉ is  the  additional  mass  to  the  pole  when  considering  conductor  effects; ݈ is  the horizontal distance between two poles; ݍ denotes  the  conductor  mass  of  a  unit  length;  and ݂ ሺ ݈ ሻ is a factor determined from:

in  which ݈ ଴ is  the  limit  span;  when  the  span  exceed  this  value,  cable  effects  need  to  be considered. For example, ݈ ଴ =656 feet for the mid-hard site. Elastic constant ( ܧ ௦ )  of  the soil springs  along  embedded  pole  was  estimated  as  160  ksi  for  those  models  with  embedded boundary conditions.

The pole structures were modeled with tapered beam elements [ANSYS 2007] for all the models. For the coupled pole-lines: the cables, including conductors, shield wires, and guy wires, were modeled with tension-only truss elements; insulators were created with uniaxial spar element; and arms that connect insulators and pole structures were modeled with elastic beam  elements  or  spar  element,  depending  on  whether  the  connections  transfer  rotations. When SSI is considered, mass-less spring elements were added to the tampered beams' nodes along the pole burial length.

Geometric and material information for these two poles either provided by the industry partner  or  by  referring  to  available  publications  [Bansal  et  al.  1995;  Kálmám  et  al.  2007; Kumosa et al. 2002; McClure and Lapointe 2003; Shehata et al. 2005]. These properties were listed in Tables 3 and 4.

The detailed finite element models for the two power lines were shown in Figure 11. To model  correctly  the  cable  and  attachment  effects,  nonlinear  static  analyses  were  first conducted under gravity loads to sag the cables. The sag-to-span ratio is defined as maximum sag divided by span of the cable. In concrete pole models, the sag ratio value for conductors was  0.04  and  was  0.03  for  shield  wires  approximately.  In  steel  pole  models,  the  sag  ratio values  were  0.05  for  conductors  and  0.02  for  shield  wires.  Mass  elements  (MASS21) calculated based on Equations (9)-(11) (Table 5) were added at the model nodes where cables connect the main structure in the simplified models.

The Block Lanczos eigenvalue solver, using the Lanczos algorithm in which the Lanczos recursion  is  performed  with  a  block  of  vectors,  was  then  used  in  modal  analysis  [ANSYS 2005].

Numerical analysis results showed strong coupling phenomena between the pole structure and the cables. Cable vibration dominated in lower modes. These modes can be called 'cable leading modes' (Figure 12). The cable components (shield wires, conductors and guy cables) of the coupled system vibrated and their dynamic effects transmitted to the pole structure. The pole  structure  vibration  at  lower  modes  was  not  easily  identified  due  to  significant  cable deformation  in  these  modes.  These  'cable  leading  modes'  played  important  roles  in  wind

load analysis of electric transmission structures. At higher modes, however, local vibration of the pole structure dominated, which can be called as 'pole leading modes' (Figures 13-14). It was found that natural frequencies of the in-plane vibration were usually smaller than the outof-plane counterparts at the same mode shapes, which agrees well with the theory proposed by  Li  et  al  [2005].  These  'pole  leading  modes'  are  important  in  the  study  of  dynamic responses of the coupled transmission structure under strong ground movements.

Figure 12. Representative cable leading modes.

<!-- image -->

Figure 13. Typical pole leading modes of concrete pole-line system.

<!-- image -->

Figure 14. Typical pole leading modes of steel pole-line system.

<!-- image -->

The  coupling  between  the  pole  structure  and  the  cables  makes  free  vibration  of transmission  lines  a  complex  problem.  Various  possibilities  of  the  cable  vibration  offer different scenarios of interaction with the main structure vibration for a certain mode. This increases the difficulty in identifying a pole vibration mode, especially at lower modes, where cable vibration dominated.

Figure 15 shows the first bending mode of a pole structure coupled with cable vibration. Different  cable  vibrations  couple  with  the  pole  cantilever  deformation  with  several  natural frequencies for both in-plane and out-of-plane vibrations.

Simplified  models  were  developed  based  on  the  method  proposed  by  Li  et  al  [2005]. These models greatly reduce computation time and analysis efforts. Figure 16 shows the first four modes of the simplified concrete pole model. Figure 17 shows the second to the fourth modes of the simplified steel pole with guy wires. When a guy cable was simplified into one tension-only truss element, all the modes are pole leading modes (Figure 18); so the steel pole bending modes are easily identified. Out-of-plan vibration mode shapes are similar to the inplan  ones  for  the  same  mode,  but  the  natural  frequencies  are  different  due  to  different additional masses at these two directions.

At  the  same  vibration  modes,  natural  frequencies  are  smaller  for  the  models  with  soil springs.  Mode  shapes,  however,  did  not  change  much.  The  method  developed  by  Li  et  al. [2005] considers the soil effects by site classification. Therefore, simplified  models established  by  following  this  approach  is  able  to  yield  relatively  accurate  results  without adding soil springs at the embedded portion of the pole.

Natural frequencies obtained from modal analyses of the different pole-line system FE models were compared with those from modal testing.

Overall, they match well. When only the pole structure was modeled, frequency values were larger than the corresponding experimentally identified results. When cable effects were included in the models, calculated frequencies became closer to test results. Simplified pole system  models developed based on Li et al. [2005] showed better results than the isolated pole models.

## BLAST EFFECTS ON POWER TRANSMISSION POLES

Blast activities can bring adverse influences to nearby structures. Common practices are to set up a ground vibration restriction and to implement some protection measures. Detailed study  of  blasting  impact  on  a  structure  should  consider  both  characterization  of  ground vibration and structural responses. The establishment of design response spectra and a ground vibration  limit  for  transmission  structures  near  surface  mine  blasting  is  reported  in  the flowing.

The response spectrum is presented in the form of plots of maximum peak responses to an excitation versus natural frequencies. Damping ratio ߦ of  0.02  can  be  assumed [LoredoSouza and Davenport 2003]; response spectra of ground motions can then be generated using Duhamel's  integral.  Typical  spectra  are  shown  in  Figures  23  to  25.  Pseudo  peak  ground vibrations are the peak values of these spectra. The amplification factors, the ratio of average pseudo peak ground motions at designated frequency ranges via corresponding PPA, PPV and PPS can be obtained. The average value for amplification factor is determined as 3.39.

A target ground motion curve can be generated based on empirical criteria. Given a PPV, PPA and PPS can then be calculated following the empirical relations between PPA, PPV and PPS  (Figure  22).  The  principal  spectrum  frequency  can  be  estimated  from  frequency distributions.  Plotting  target  velocity  with  predicted  acceleration  and  displacement  in  a tripartite  paper  results  in  a  target  ground  motion  curve.  A  response  spectrum  can  then  be constructed by multiplying the target ground motion with the average amplification factor and is drawn again into a tripartite plot. Take the blast limit PPV=2 in/s for example: Figure 26 is the target ground vibration curve in tripartite format, in which PPA and PPS were obtained from relations in Figure 22; Figure 27 is the response spectrum designed through multiplying with  the  amplification  factor.  Following  the  same  way,  other  anticipated  design  spectral curves with different levels of PPV can be developed.

## Transmission Pole Responses under Blast Loads

Spectrum  analysis  is  to  calculate  maximum  structural  responses  based  on  design spectrum  and  structure  modal  characteristics.  Spectrum  analysis  of  two  pole  structures (Figure 10) was performed using the FE software, ANSYS ® . The FE models for the poles are the simplified models, which include an isolated concrete/steel pole models with additional conductor mass attached. Response spectra designed based on blast measurements were used as  input  excitations.  Structural  responses  of  pole  structures  at  various  blast  limits  based  on different levels of peak particle velocity criteria were numerically investigated.

Spectrum analysis was performed by importing design response spectra in two horizontal directions  (in-plane  and  out-of-plane)  and  the  vertical  direction.  The  in-plane  horizontal direction is parallel with the conductors whereas the out-of-plane direction is perpendicular to the conductors. For the response spectrum designed based on 2 in/s PPV, the first principal stress  distributions  are  shown  in  Figures  28  and  29.  The  maximum  displacements  are calculated as 0.14 in for the concrete pole and 0.12 in for the steel pole at 2 in/s PPV criterion. Following  the  same  procedure,  more  spectrum  analyses  were  performed  with  a  series  of response spectra designed based on different target PPV criteria.

Time  history  analysis,  or  transient  dynamic  analysis,  a  technique  used  to  obtain  timevarying displacements, stresses, and forces of a structure excited by time-dependent loads is an uncommon design practice due to its time consuming computation. Dai and Chen [2010b] performed time history analyses and concluded that time domain analysis resulted in more stringent design requirements for the transmission pole structures. where ݂ ௖ ᇱ is compressive strength of concrete.

Comparison  was  made  between  the  maximum  first  principal  stress  results  ( σ 1)  from numerical analyses with material allowable stress ( σ 1allowable) for the concrete pole (Table 6).

From Table 6, it is shown that 2 in/s PPV criterion always gives a safe blast design. If economic reasons are taken into account, the peak particle velocity limit may be able to be relaxed to 3 in/s. Although the maximum first principal result from the time history analysis at  3  in/s  PPV is greater than concrete modulus of rupture, the concrete pole may not crack since other factors such as soil structure interaction effects may take place. The validity of this  2  in/s  PPV  criterion  was  then  examined  by  moment  capacity  and  tip  displacement comparisons. From the above analyses, the concrete pole ultimate capacity is controlled by the first principal stress. 2 in/s PPV ground vibration is designated to be a blast limit. Similar analysis  work  was  carried  out  for  the  guyed  steel  pole  line  system.  It  is  concluded  that structural  responses for the two poles (Figure 10) under the 2 in/s PPV ground motion are both within their elastic safety ranges.

There are of course other factors that may need to be taken into account, such as soilstructure interaction and material nonlinearity. Also, for other types of transmission structures, like self-supported lattice towers, this criterion might be conservative. But for the guyed  tower  structures,  engineers  should  design  blasts  with  cautions,  especially  when explosions  can  cause  very  strong  vertical  direction  ground  motions.  There  have  been observations of local buckling of the guyed lattice tower caused by vertical ground vibration.

## Blast Design Considerations for Power Transmission Structure Protection

Blast activities can not only generate strong ground vibrations, but also high air pressure and other adverse influences on nearby structures. A comprehensive design is needed in order to reduce any detrimental damages to these structures.

Explosive detonations cause a change in air pressure, which reflects on contact with a structure, imparting an impulse upon the structure. This air pressure issue is the main concern for  objects  with  large  surfaces  and  it  is  usually  not  a  big  problem  for  power  transmission

structures because no matter lattice towers and pole structures do not have large surface areas. Ground movements induced by blasting propagate to a structure. The effects they can bring include not only excitations for the structure, which are the focus in blast limit development, but also instability issues for the foundation. For power transmission pole structures, which are  usually  directly  embedded,  soil  condition  is  a  very  important  consideration  to  design  a safe blast plan. Soft soils and ground fissures are potential geological instabilities during blast practices. Therefore, it is crucial that these problems can be recognized prior to detonations. Rock fragments and debris are propelled during explosions, if not properly controlled, can damage power cables or even the support pole structure. For power lines, the penalties of the flyrock problem are much more severe than ground vibrations. Therefore, a reasonable blast plan  needs  to  include  a  comprehensive  consideration  of  many  factors.  A  preliminary procedure was developed to help with a blast design. The design begins with a field survey to identify nearby structures that may be endangered as well as geological conditions that may affect blast wave propagation. A reasonable ground vibration limit can be developed through structural  response  analyses  as  previously  described.  Foundation  excitation  inputs  in  these analyses can be collected in the field or use historical data. But geological conditions for the sites where the data are from and where the power lines stand should be similar. With this blast  limit  and  with  available  information  of  geological  medium  in  which  blasting  wave propagates,  empirical  relations  between  the  charge  weight  of  blasting  agents,  the  distance from blast to structure, and the ground vibration limit at structure foundations can be used to back-calculate  the  blast  scale  at  the  explosion  end.  Moreover,  additional  procedures  and precautions  may  be  necessary  to  prevent  other  adverse  effects  caused  by  blasts,  such  as flyrock and ground instability. The considerations discussed previously are summarized as the flowing, by taking the two pole supported power lines (Figure 10) discussed previously as an example:

1. Geological  profiling  should  be  conducted  for  site  characterization.  It  was  used  to identify any potential of geological problems, such as fractures.
2. A  pre-blast  survey  on  structures  within  1500  feet  of  the  excavation  area  was performed to make note of existing structural conditions.
3. Structural  response  analyses  were  performed  to  develop  a  blast  limit  for  these structures. The maximum ground vibration limit of 2 in/s (PPV), and an airblast level of 130 decibels was specified.
4. Empirical equations between the blast limit, distance from blast to structure, and the charge weight were used to design the blast scale.
5. Any  landowner  within  ½  mile  radius  of  the  blast  was  notified  of  the  date  and approximate  time  of  blasts.  In  addition,  blasts  were  warned  with  an  audible  horn immediately prior.
6. A  'safe  zone'  perimeter  of  550  feet  was  applied.  The  use  of  blasting  mats  was implemented to control flyrock debris when necessary.
7. Each critical blast was monitored by vibration measurement equipments placed close to the foundation of the structure.
8. Post-blast inspection was conducted to detail any changes from the initial pre-blast survey.
9. All relevant blast procedures and monitoring results were recorded for retention by the facility.

## SUMMARY

Dynamic stability considerations are critical to power transmission pole structures due to their  light  weights  and  the  potential  adverse  impacts  to  power  delivery.  Free  vibration behaviors  of  several  typical  single  poles  were  studied  through  impact  modal  tests  and analyses. Fundamental frequencies obtained from experiments conducted on actual structures are  more than 1 Hz, which go beyond the range specified by the current design guidelines (ASCE 1991, 2009). The need for a detailed study is indicated since dynamic stability is an important  parameter  in  the  specific  transmission  line  design.  Through  numerical  analyses, factors that have influences on pole free vibration such as concrete strength, prestress level, and  soil-structure-interaction  were  investigated,  which  was  also  extended  to  include  the structure-cable  coupling  issues  that  may  be  detrimental  for  a  power  line  stability.  Two  inoperation  pole  supported  transmission  lines  were  studied.  To  reduce  the  complexity  of  the numerical analysis, simplified finite element models were developed for both these structures based on modal performance similarity.

Blast  activities  in  mining  areas  sometimes  endanger  nearby  power  transmission  lines. Currently, there are no guidelines to help design a blast to protect these power facilities. It is significant to develop a scientific-based approach for blast plan design. An example of two active power lines was presented in this chapter to demonstrate the development of response spectra as well as a blast limit. Other adverse effects induced by blasts, such as flyrock and foundation instability, were also discussed for a comprehensive blast design. Following the procedure described in this study, engineers can develop a reasonable blast plan for the power transmission structures.

Chapter 4

## ELECTRIC TRANSMISSION LINE APPROACH TO NON-ELECTRIC TRANSMISSION LINES

## R. Uklejewski and T. Czapski

Dept. of Electroengineering &amp; Medical Bioengineering, Faculty of Mathematics, Physics and Technical Sciences, Kazimierz Wielki University in Bydgoszcz, Poland

## 1. INTRODUCTION

The notions and methods developed during more than 100 years in the electric parameterdistributed circuits theory have been successfully applied to solve various problems concerning the electric transmission lines working at low frequencies 50 Hz or 60 Hz, and also the microwave electronic circuits [15]. However, they can also be applied to analyse and solve  one-dimensional  problems  occurring  in  various  domains  of  the  continuum  physics, especially in the coupled fields problems: mechano-electric, acousto-electric, thermo-electric, thermo-elastic, thermo-elasto-diffusive, etc .

It will be shown in this chapter that such applications are possible and successful only on the  basis  of  a  proper  system  of  analogies  between  the  electric  quantities  and  non-electric quantities. The definition and the construction method of the proper systems of such analogies can be given on the basis of  the  algebraic  theory  of  dimensional  analysis  developed  by  S. Drobot [8]. The examples of the applications of the electric transmission line approach to: (1) porous vibroisolators under harmonic loading, (2) one-dimensional problems of thermodiffusion  in  elastic  solids  (and,  in  particular,  to  one-dimensional  problems  of  the theory of thermoelasticity), (3) heat and moisture transport in two-layer building walls, and (4) the acoustoelasto-electric waves transmission in normal and osteoporotic long bones are given.

## 2. CONSTRUCTION OF ELECTRO-MECHANICAL ANALOGIES SYSTEMS BY MEANS OF DIMENSIONAL ANALYSIS

## 2.1. System of Electro - Mechanical Analogies

Definition and construction method. The definition of the system of electro - mechanical analogies  will  be  formulated  on  the  basis  of  the  algebraic  theory  of  dimensional  analysis presented in [8]. The fundamental idea is here the notion of the dimensional space over the reals II (r). The dimensions of all physical quantities ([voltage], [mass], [length], …) are the elements of the space II (r). Two operations over the elements of the space II are determined by the following axioms

- internal operation: multiplication of elements

<!-- formula-not-decoded -->

<!-- formula-not-decoded -->

<!-- formula-not-decoded -->

- external operation: raising to a power

<!-- formula-not-decoded -->

<!-- formula-not-decoded -->

<!-- formula-not-decoded -->

<!-- formula-not-decoded -->

<!-- formula-not-decoded -->

The  neutral  element  of  internal  operation  in  the  space Π (R)  is  element  [1]  ,  i.e.  the dimension of any dimensionless quantity.

As  it  was  shown  by  Drobot  [8],  the  dimensional  space Π over  the  reals  R,  with  the operations  given  by  (2.1)  -  (2.8),  is  isomorphic  with  a  certain  vector  space Σ (R).  The isomorphism ϕ ( ) ( Π ℜ → ∑ ℜ) : is defined as follows

<!-- formula-not-decoded -->

<!-- formula-not-decoded -->

where α , β are  vectors.  The  multiplication  of  dimensions  in  space Π corresponds  to  the addition of vectors in space Σ , whereas the raising of dimensions to a real power corresponds to the multiplication of vectors by real numbers.

Since in algebra the isomorphic structures are identified, thus - following Drobot - the dimensions may be considered as vectors [8].

The other notions concerning the space Π such as: the set of fundamental dimensions, the dimensional  independence  of  elements  and  the  dimensional  combination  of  dimensions correspond to the following notions of the vector space theory, i.e. respectively: the basis of the vector space, the linear independence of vectors and the linear combination of vectors [8].

In the algebraic theory of dimensional spaces all the theorems dealing with vector spaces are valid. For example, the dimensions A1,  A2,  …, An are dimensionally independent if and only if

<!-- formula-not-decoded -->

where i a i n i A ∏= 1 -  is  a  dimensional combination of dimensions n A A A ..., 2, 1, and [1] is the neutral element of multiplication in space II.

Definition of the electromechanical analogies system . Let Π m be the set of dimensions of all the mechanical quantities, whereas Π e - the set of dimensions of all the electric quantities in the dimensional space Π . It is easy to show, by means of axioms (2.1) - (2.8), that the sets Π m and Π e are the subspaces of the dimensional space Π .

DEFINITION * .  Each  isomorphism Ψ 1, Ψ 2,  …, Ψ n  of  the  dimensional  space  of  the electric quantities Π e  in  the dimensional space of the mechanic quantities Π m will be called the system of electro - mechanical analogies, see Figure 1.

Figure 1. The system of electro-mechanical analogies as an isomorphism of dimensional spaces: Π e and Π m .

<!-- image -->

Now, we formulate the theorem which determines the necessary and sufficient condition for the existence of an isomorphism of two dimensional spaces having the finite number of fundamental elements (units). Similarly as in the vector space theory, the following theorem holds true [16]:

THEOREM  * . The dimensional spaces Π m and Π e are isomorphic if and only if their sets of fundamental dimensions contain the same (finite) number of elements .

The definition of the system of electro-mechanical analogies presented above is general i.e. each isomorphism Ψ i (Figure 1) constitutes a system of analogies.

Each  type  of  problem  taken  into  consideration  -  e.g.  lumped-parameter  problems, uniaxial  problems  with  linear  distributed  parameters,  continuous  media  -  has  a  suitable isomorphism Ψ i . Thus, a question appears: how to construct the electro-mechanical analogies systems and then choose the best of them (considering its physical interpretation)?

## 2.2. Method of Construction of the Electro-Mechanical Analogies Systems

According to Theorem* the system of electro-mechanical analogies can be obtained by the imposition of n correspondences between the fundamental dimensions of the dimensional spaces Π m and Π e, where n is a number of elements in the set of fundamental dimensions in each  of  these  spaces.  It  should  be  noted  that  every  set  of n dimensionally  independent dimensions (Eq. (2.11)) can be considered as a set of fundamental dimensions of these spaces.

Since each dimension (mechanic or electric) can be expressed as a certain dimensional combination  of n fundamental  dimensions,  the  remaining  correspondences  between  the mechanical and electrical dimensions are the consequence of n fundamental correspondences. For  example,  in  case  of  lumped-parameter  problems  we  have  the  following  sets  of fundamental dimensions

- in the dimensional space Π m :

<!-- formula-not-decoded -->

- in the dimensional space Π e :

<!-- formula-not-decoded -->

Both sets of fundamental dimensions contain in this case three elements n = 3, thus, by virtue of Theorem * , the dimensional spaces Π m and Π e (for lumped-parameter problems) are isomorphic.

The Maxwell's system of electro-mechanical analogies for lumped-parameter problems can be obtained by imposition of the following three correspondences

<!-- formula-not-decoded -->

<!-- formula-not-decoded -->

<!-- formula-not-decoded -->

where the dimensions [force]m, [power]m and [power]e are given by the following dimensional combinations of the fundamental dimensions (2.12):

<!-- formula-not-decoded -->

<!-- formula-not-decoded -->

<!-- formula-not-decoded -->

It  is  easy  to  show  by  using  (2.11),  that  the  dimensions  appearing  on  the  same  side  in correspondences (2.14)-(2.16) are independent.

Correspondences  (2.14)  and  (2.15)  are  of  fundamental  importance  for  the  physical interpretation of this system of analogy, whereas correspondence (2.16) determines the type of the system of analogy (Maxwell's type), [23].

If in correspondences (2.14)-(2.16) correspondence (2.16) is replaced by

<!-- formula-not-decoded -->

we  obtain  another  system  of  electro-mechanical  analogies,  which  is  called  the  Firestone's system of electro-mechanical analogies [10].

In  case  of  lumped-parameter  problems,  both  Maxwell's  and  Firestone's  systems  of electro-mechanical analogies possess the complete physical interpretation.

However,  in  case  of  the  uniaxial  problems  with  linearly  distributed  parameters,  the Firestone's system of electro-mechanical analogies has an advantage. That is to say, in the Firestone's  system  of  analogy,  the  elastic  stress  corresponds  to  the  density  of  an  electric current:

<!-- formula-not-decoded -->

whereas in the Maxwell's system of analogy for the elastic stress we obtain

<!-- formula-not-decoded -->

an electrical quantity: [voltage] ⋅ [surface] -1 , which  does  not  have a clear physical interpretation.

Firestone's system of electro-mechanical analogies for uniaxial problems with linearly distributed parameters,

In case of the uniaxial problems with linearly distributed parameters (electric transmission lines) the following four dimensions constitute a set of fundamental dimensions in the electric dimensional space Π e :

<!-- formula-not-decoded -->

According  to  Theorem * ,  the  mechanic  dimensional  space Π m will  be  in  this  case isomorphic with the electric  dimensional  space Π e ,  if  we conventionally extend  the  set  of three fundamental dimensions [m], [l], [t] to a four elements set [16]. Formally, it is always possible. As a natural extension, we assume the following set of fundamental dimensions in the space Π m for uniaxial problems with linearly distributed parameters:

<!-- formula-not-decoded -->

It means that the dimensions: [displacement] and [distance] are considered as independent  in  the  space Π m .  The  physical  reason  of  this  distinction  is  the  fact  that  the quantities: displacement and distance are physically different, the first is associated with the elastic energy, whereas the second is associated with the geometry of the system.

Thus, the Firestone's system of electro-mechanical analogies for uniaxial problems with linearly distributed parameters is obtained by the imposition of the following four correspondences:

<!-- formula-not-decoded -->

<!-- formula-not-decoded -->

<!-- formula-not-decoded -->

<!-- formula-not-decoded -->

Table 1. Firestone's system of analogies for the problems with linearly distributed parameters (transmission line)

| Electric quantities and dimension                | Mechanic quantities and dimensions          |
|--------------------------------------------------|---------------------------------------------|
| Voltage [u]                                      | Velocity of displacement [l] ⋅ [t] -1       |
| Current [i]                                      | Force [m] ⋅ [l] ⋅ [t] -2                    |
| Magnetic flux [u] ⋅ [t]                          | Displacement [l]                            |
| Electric charge [i] ⋅ [t]                        | Momentum [m] ⋅ [l] ⋅ [t] -1                 |
| Inductance per unit length                       | Mass per unit length                        |
| [I] ⋅ [t] ⋅ [u] -1 ⋅ [L] -1                      | [m] ⋅ [L] -1                                |
| Characteristic impedance [u] ⋅ [I] -1            | Velocity of displacement/force [t] ⋅ [m] -1 |
| Current density [I] ⋅ [L] -2                     | Stress [m] ⋅ [l] ⋅ [t] -2 ⋅ [L] -2          |
| Magnetic flux per unit length [u] ⋅ [t] ⋅ [L] -1 | Strain [l] ⋅ [L] -1                         |
| etc.                                             | etc.                                        |

The remaining correspondences between mechanical and electric dimensions as well as quantities - see Table 1 -result from correspondences (2.25)-(2.28).

The left side of Table 2 presents the equations of the Biot's poroelasticity theory [1-3,5] describing the one-dimensional dynamic state of strain of porous medium. The corresponding equations dealing with the system of two coupled electric transmission lines (Figure 2) are given on the right side of Table 2.

- mechanical quantities

C - permeability constant of the porous medium;

- 2 1 , ρ ρ - dynamical constant of the porous medium;
- a ρ - parameter of dynamic coupling between the skeleton and the fluid;
- electrical quantities

Cw - capacitance between line 1 or 2 per unit length,

Gw - conductance between line 1 and 2 per unit length.

The  equations  describing  both  problems  (Table  2)  demonstrate  that  the  presented analogies belong to the Firestone's system of electro-mechanical analogies given in Table 1. Now, methods, concepts and completed formulas of the electric transmission lines may be transferred to the Biot consolidation theory and applied here for solving numerous problems.

## 4. ELECTRO-ELASTO-THERMO-DIFFUSIVE ANALOGIES

The  methods  and  concepts  of  the  electric  transmission  lines  theory  can  also  be  well applied in the theory of thermoelasticity as well as in the theory of thermodiffusion in solids. In  the  paper  [9]  the  electrical  analogon  for  the  one-dimensional  quasi-static  process  of thermodiffusion  in  solids,  described  by  the  equations  of  the  Podstrigac-Nowacki  theory [17,22],  is  discussed  in  detail.  Here,  we  present  only  the  construction  of  the  analogous systems applied in [9]. The systems of electro-elastic, electro-thermic and electro-diffusive analogies for the uniaxial problems with linearly distributed parameters are obtained by the imposition of the following correspondences:

- (a)  electro-elastic analogies in the Firestone's system of analogies  (isomorphism m e Ψ Π →Π : , see Figure 4) - according to the correspondences (2.25)-(2.28)

## 4.2. Heat and Moisture Transfer in a two-Layer External Building wall During One Year

As an example of a 1D of a process of thermodiffusion in solids we consider the process of heat and moisture transfer in an external building wall made of two material layers possessing different physical properties, under sinusoidally changing (T = 1 year) external  climate  parameters,  i.e.  mean  monthly  values  of  external  air  temperature  and humidity (instantaneous deviations of the parameter values compensate each other at the level of mean monthly value [4]), Figure 6.

We assume that:

1. In the range of the considered values of the temperature, humidity and time, the system (i.e. the building wall) is linear and stationary;
2. In the region of each layer the building material is homogeneous and isotropic with respect to the thermal, humiditive and elastic properties;

3. The temperature and humidity fields in the wall are one-dimensional;
4. The  moisture  thermodiffusion  process  considered  is  slow  and,  therefore,  the mechanical inertia of the building material may be neglected;
5. The  effect  of  stress  on  the  heat  and  moisture  transfer  is  negligible.  The temperature and the moisture potential fields within the region of a two - layer building wall at slow harmonic changes will be determined from the equations of the Podstrigac-Nowacki theory of thermodiffusion in deformable solids [17, 22], using the solution derived in Sec. 4.1.

Let us now introduce the concept of chain connection of the layers of a building wall. The chain connection of the layers of a building wall is called a connection in the case when boundary conditions of the fourth kind are satisfied at the interfaces between the layers, i.e. the  heat  and  moisture  fluxes  are  continuous  (see  (4.96)-(4.99)).  The  concept  of  chain connection of the layers corresponds to the concept of the chain connection of the electric four-terminal network.

Since  the  matrix product is, in general,  noncommutative ( AB ≠ BA ), therefore the sequence of transmission matrices in the product (4.110) is important: the transmission matrices of the wall layers occur in the product (4.110) in the inverse order to that of the layers.

The solution presented in this chapter describes the process of moisture thermodiffusion in  a  two-layer  building  wall  occurring  under  slow  harmonic  changes  of  thermo-humiditive environment  parameters.  Annual  distribution  of  mean  monthly  values  of  external  climate parameters  is  sinusoidal.  The  solutions  have  been  presented  in  a  manner  that  makes  them possible to be used for determining the insulation properties (thermal and humiditive) in the general case of an n-layer building wall, on the assumption that on the surface dividing the wall  boundary  conditions  of  the  fourth  kind  are  fulfilled  (continuity  of  heat  and  moisture fluxes, of temperature and of moisture potentials). The methods used here are taken from the theory of electric four-terminal (or 4 n-terminal) networks.

## 5. ELASTO-ELECTRIC LONGITUDINAL HARMONIC WAVES IN NORMAL AND OSTEOPOROTIC LONG BONES VIEWED AS TRANSMISSION LINES

In  works  by  Uklejewski  [26-28]  the  author  has  presented  a  continuum  theory  of generation of electromechanical potentials (SGPs) in wet cortical bone, which combines the Biot theory of poroelasticity with the differential equations of classical electrokinetics. Now, this theory will be adapted to a wet long bone shaft viewed as a transmission line.

Figure 8. Long bone shaft as a hollow (tubular) conduit; the product Sx(l -Ix) (where: Sx - area of cross-section x, Ix - porosity of an element Sx~x; 0 ~ x ~ L) has practically the same value along the shaft (it is due to the bone adaptive remodelling process).

<!-- image -->

In  Figure  8  the  1ongitudinal  and  transversa1  sections  of  a  long  bone  shaft  have  been shown schematically. We note the interesting properties of long bone shafts, resu1ting from the existence of the remodelling (adaptive) processes which occur in a living bone according to its mechanical strain history. As it was shown by Lazenby [13]: " ... interna1 and externa1 remodelling  do  not  proceed  independently.  Net  bone  1oss  occurs  where  it  can  be  most afforded  without  precipitously  compromising  strength".  It  means  that  the  cross-section geometry of a long bone is strictly connected with the distribution of bone porosity in the cross-section region, and both these parameters, i.e the  cross-section  geometry  and  the  porosity  distribution  in  the  cross-section  region  are remodelled under the influence of bone bending loads. Because every element Sx Δ x of a long bone shaft (where: Sx -  cross-section area at the distance x; 0 ≤ x ≤ L, L -  long bone shaft length)  transfers  in  the  axial  direction  of  bone  the  same  compression  loads  -  it  can  be assumed, in a healthy long bone, that the compression strength of every element Sx Δ x of long bone shaft has the same value. Thus, we can assume that the value of the product: Sx (1-fx) (where f x - the mean bone porosity in element Sx Δ x ) is the same along the bone shaft length.

<!-- formula-not-decoded -->

Moreover, if for  a  given  long  bone  the  cross-section  area  of  its  shaft  varies  very  little along  the  shaft  length,  as  it  usually  occurs  (cf.  [11,20,28]),  then  also  the  following approximate equation holds

<!-- formula-not-decoded -->

i.e. the mean bone porosity f x in the bone shaft element Sx Δ x is approximately the same along  the  length  of  long  bone  shaft.  The  properties  (5.1),  (5.2)  allow  us  to  introduce  in  a natural way the mechanical and electrical parameters per unit length of the long bone shaft, and to treat the long bone shaft as a mechano-electric transmission line with macroparameters distributed practically uniformly along the line length.

## 5.1. Equations of the Biot Theory for a Long Bone Shaft Transmission Line under a Longitudinal Harmonic Load

We consider a porous long bone shaft filled with physiological fluid and assume that at x = 0 on the cross-section of the shaft a longitudinal harmonic load P(t) acts with the pulsation ω

<!-- formula-not-decoded -->

The  cortical  bone  of  long  bone  shaft  wall  can  be  treated  in  these  conditions as  an isotropic material  -  it  was  experimentally  proved  by  Huiskes  [12],  who  wrote:  "It  can  be concluded  from  these  results  that  when  assuming  cortical  bone  material  to  exhibit  linear elastic,  homogeneous and transversely  isotropic behaviour an excellent agreement between theoretical and experimental results can be obtained, although some local inaccuracies due to inhomogenity should be expected. [ ... ] However, when torsion is not considered and only the most significant longitudinal stress components are of interest, a good approximation can be obtained from assuming the cortical bone to be isotropic" .

The deformation of the porous cortical bone of a long bone shaft wall is described by the following one-dimensional dynamic state equations of Biot's theory, given in Table 2.

Because the system under discussion is linear and stationary (i.e. the values of its parameters  do  not  change  in  time),  and  the  mechanical  load  at  the  cross-section x =  0  is  a harmonic function of time, therefore, the other quantities also vary sinusoidally, i.e.

<!-- formula-not-decoded -->

## 5.3. Electric Potentials Associated with Propagation of Harmonic Longitudinal Acousto-Electric Waves in Porous Long Bones

During the propagation of harmonic longitudinal elastic waves in a wet long bone shaft the  oscillatory  flow  of  ionic  physiological  fluid  in  cortical  bone  pores  occur.  This  flow  described by the relative fluid velocity ) ( ) ( 1 1 x w x W ∗ ∗ -produces the electrokinetic streaming currents and streaming potentials, described by the equations of classical electrokinetics:

where ∗ ∗ -w W is the vector of relative velocity of ionic fluid in bone pores, p - the fluid pressure, V - the electric potentials, J the vector of the electric current density, A11 - the hydraulic permeability of porous bone, A22 - the electric conductivity, A12 - the electrokinetic coefficient of electroosmosis, A21 - the electrokinetic coefficient of streaming current.

Equations  (5.19)-(5.20)  contain  the  description  of  four  electrokinetic  phenomena:  the streaming  potentials  (where J =0 ),  the  streaming  current  (when ∇ V=0 ),  the  electroosmosis (when ∇ p=0 ), and the electroosmotic pressure (when 0 = -∗ ∗ w W ).

In  this  way  electric  voltages  and  currents  associated  with  propagation  of  longitudinal acousto-electric  waves  in  porous  long  bones  are  generated  [28].  Basing  on  this  theoretical description of acousto-electric phenomena in porous long bones the analyses of various states of acousto-electric bone transmission line were performed [7] and the method of determination of density and poroelastic parameters of normal and osteoporotic long bones is currently patented in the European Patent Office [30].

Chapter 5

## THE EFFECTS OF PRIORITY SERVICE ON ELECTRICITY TRANSMISSION: THE CASE OF INTERCONNECTION INVESTMENT *

## Isamu Matsukawa

Faculty of Economics, Musashi University, Tokyo, Japan

## ABSTRACT

Reliability  management  of  electric  power  systems,  which  used  to  be  implemented through coordination among regulated utilities, is one of the main issues in deregulated electricity markets. The efficient management of system reliability is a crucial factor for the generation sector to be competitive because generators rely on transmission networks to send power to consumers. Priority service, which is a nonlinear pricing mechanism and efficiently  rations  the  usage  of  scarce  resources  through  consumer  self-selection  of  a reliability  level  of  service,  can  be  applied  to  electricity  transmission  as  an  efficient method of reliability  management.  Examples include  tariff  options  of  'firm'  and  'nonfirm' transmission services in the PJM Regional Transmission Organization in the United States.  Even  if  the  number  of  classes  is  two,  efficiency  gain  from  priority  service  is sufficient in comparison to random rationing such as rotating outages.

This study investigates the  impacts  of  priority  service  on  transmission  investment, using a mathematical programming model. The model solves a mixed integer programming problem in which the transmission utility's decision to interrupt wheeling demand is represented by binary variables. Prior to the transmission service, customers can  assign  the  various  increments  of  their  transmission  demand  to  different  service options  to  maximize  the  expected  net  benefits.  The  transmission  utility  is  assumed  to determine  service  prices  and  transmission  investment  to  maximize  the  expected  social welfare.

The  model  is  applied  to  a  hypothetical  project  that  adds  an  interconnector  to  the congested power grid in Japan. Examples include frequency converters, which are used for power exchange between 50 Hz regions (the eastern part of Japan) and 60 Hz regions

* This study was supported by the Grant-in-Aid for Scientific Research (C) of the JSPS KAKENHI (20530217), and by the Musashi University Research Project.

ISBN: 978-1-61761-300-5

© 2011 Nova Science Publishers, Inc.

(the  western  part  of  Japan).  As  these  frequency  converters  have  often  been  congested, price  differences  between  the  east  and  west  of  Japan  have  emerged,  and  these  price differences may discourage power trade between two regions. The application of priority service with two classes to the usage of frequency converters is expected to efficiently ration  congested  power  grid.  The  results  indicate  that  high  costs  of  building  an interconnector  exclude  customers  exhibiting  relatively  lower  willingness  to  pay  for transmission  service,  though  priority  service  for  electricity  transmission  enables  other customers to raise their net benefits in comparison to random rationing. The exclusion of these customers leads to a loss of social welfare through a decrease in power trade. A welfare analysis based on a mathematical programming model of this paper is useful for understanding the impact of priority service on interconnection investment.

## 1. INTRODUCTION

Reliability  management  of  electric  power  systems,  which  used  to  be  implemented through  coordination  among  regulated  utilities,  is  one  of  the  main  issues  in  deregulated electricity markets where suppliers compete with each other to grab customers. The efficient management of system reliability is a crucial factor for the market to be competitive because suppliers rely on transmission networks to send power to consumers. If congested networks are  not  properly  managed,  suppliers  who  could  sell  inexpensive  electricity  to  customers cannot access the networks.

On top of that, promoting transmission investment in competitive power markets is one of the most challenging tasks for reliability management because there has been a growing concern about reliability of transmission networks (Joskow, 2005). To promote transmission investment, the Energy Policy Act of 2005 requires United States transmission utilities, which have  been  subject  to  rate  of  return  regulation,  to  comply  with  reliability  standards  set  and enforced by an Electric Reliability Organization (Young, 2006).

Priority  service  is  a  nonlinear  pricing  mechanism  that  efficiently  rations  the  usage  of scarce resources through consumer self-selection of a reliability level of service (Harris and Raviv, 1981; Chao and Wilson, 1987; Viswanathan and Tse, 1989; Wilson, 1989; Spulber, 1993;  Crew  and  Fernando,  1994;  Matsukawa,  2009).  Priority  service  can  be  applied  to electricity  transmission  as  an  efficient  method  of  reliability  management  (Chao  and  Peck, 1998; Woo et al., 1998; Deng and Oren, 2001). Examples include tariff options of 'firm' and 'non-firm'  transmission  services  in  the  PJM  Regional  Transmission  Organization  in  the United States. Even if the number of classes is two, efficiency gain from priority service in the  short  run  is  sufficient  in  comparison  to  random  rationing  such  as  rotating  outages. However, priority service may lead to insufficient transmission investment in the long run, because substantial investment costs of transmission networks could prevent the transmission utility  from  expanding  the  capacity  of  networks  so  that  the  rationing  of  the  usage  of  the networks could frequently occur.

This  study  investigates  the  impacts  of  priority  service  on  electricity  interconnection investment, using a mathematical programming model. The model used in this study relies on Smith  (1987),  who  applies  a  mixed  integer  programming  to  the  analysis  of  the  effects  of priority service on the welfare-maximizing capacity and usage of electricity generation plants. This study applies the model of Smith (1987) to the analysis of the effects of priority service

on  the  welfare-maximizing  capacity  and  usage  of  an  electricity  interconnector,  which promotes power trade across regions.

As an illustration, the model developed here is applied to a hypothetical project that adds an  interconnector  to  the  congested  power  grid  in  Japan.  Examples  include  frequency converters, which are used for power exchange between 50 Hz regions (the eastern part of Japan)  and  60  Hz  regions  (the  western  part  of  Japan).  As  these  frequency  converters  have often been congested, price differences between the east and west of Japan have emerged, and these price differences may discourage power trade between two regions. The application of priority  service  with  two  classes  to  the  usage  of  frequency  converters  is  expected  to efficiently ration the congested power grid.

The organization of this study is as follows. Section 2 briefly describes the property of priority  service  with  discrete  classes.  Section  3  explains  the  structure  of  a  mixed  integer programming model and Section 4 presents its application to a hypothetical project that adds an interconnector to the congested power grid in Japan. Section 5 discusses the impacts of alternative regulatory schemes on priority service. Section 6 concludes this study.

## 2. PROPERTY OF PRIORITY SERVICE

Prior to the supply of the transmission service, the transmission utility offers grid users with a menu of tariff options characterized by two non-negative variables: the reliability level of the transmission service, denoted by r ( r ≤ 1), and the price of the service, denoted by p . The  grid  user's  willingness  to  pay  for  the  supply  of  transmission  service  is  denoted  by v , which distributes according to a function D ( v ) over the interval [ V 0 , V 1].  The willingness to pay is private information, and the transmission utility only knows the distribution about the willingness  to  pay  prior  to  offering  priority  service.  Grid  users  subscribe  to  one  of  the transmission tariff options so as to maximize their expected surplus, which is given by rv -p . If  no  tariff  option  provides non-negative expected surplus, grid users choose not to receive any service from the transmission utility, and their service reliability and price become zero. Grid users choosing a lower reliability level are rationed more frequently than those choosing a higher reliability level. Grid users in each priority class are rationed at random within that class when the available transmission capacity is insufficient to supply the entire class (Chao et al., 1988).

The  amount  of  available  transmission  capacity  is  uncertain,  and  the  supply  of  the transmission service is rationed whenever the level of available capacity is less than the total demand for the service. The probability that the supply of the transmission service is rationed depends on total demand and the level of transmission capacity. Prior to the provision of the service,  the  transmission  utility  has  chosen  the  capacity  and  has  obtained  the  information about the probability that the supply of the transmission service is rationed.

Each priority class of the transmission service is defined as the interval of willingness to pay for the service; [ v 0 , v 1 ], [ v 1 , v 2 ], [ v 2 , v 3 ],..., [ vn -1 , vn ], where V 0 ≤ v 0 &lt; v 1 &lt; v 2 &lt; ... &lt; vn -1 &lt; vn = V 1.  The  menu  of  priority  class i consists  of  the  price pi and  reliability ri ,  which  are determined by the transmission utility. For the menu of priority service to be consistent with the maximization of the grid users' expected surplus, two constraints must be satisfied: the individual  rationality  and  incentive  compatibility  constraints.  The  individual  rationality

constraint  indicates  that  the  expected  surplus  of  any  grid  user  who  subscribes  to  the transmission service must be non-negative. Thus, for grid users subscribing to priority class i , riv ≥ pi . The incentive compatibility constraint indicates that for the choice of priority class to be optimal, grid users must receive the maximum expected surplus from that choice. Thus, for grid users subscribing to priority class i , riv -pi ≥ r jv -pj for j ≠ i . The incentive compatibility constraint  also  implies  that  a  grid  user  whose  willingness  to  pay  is  equal  to vi must  be indifferent between the class i , [ vi -1 , vi ], and class i + 1, [ vi , vi +1]. Then, the price for class i + 1 is written as

<!-- formula-not-decoded -->

As  an  illustrative  example,  suppose  that  the  transmission  utility  provides  two  priority classes,  [0.2,  0.6]  and  [0.6,  1.0],  for  grid  users  whose  willingness  to  pay  for  the  service distributes according to a function D ( v ) = 1 -v over the interval [0, 1]. Suppose also that the transmission utility assigns 0.4 to the value of r 1 and 0.8 to the value of r 2, respectively. Then, from equation (1), the incentive compatibility constraint implies that

<!-- formula-not-decoded -->

<!-- formula-not-decoded -->

Grid users whose willingness to pay for the transmission service is less than 0.2 do not subscribe to the service. This is because their expected surplus would become negative and the  individual  rationality  constraint  would  not  be  satisfied  if  they  choose  either  class  1  or class 2, as indicated by equations (2) and (3). Grid users with v = 0.2 are indifferent between subscribing  to  class  1  and  no  subscribing  to  the  transmission  service,  and  their  expected surplus becomes zero. For 0.2 &lt; v &lt; 0.6, subscribing to class 1 would enable the grid users to earn the positive expected surplus. This surplus is larger than the expected surplus they would earn  if  they  choose  class  2.  Grid  users  with v =  0.6  are  indifferent  between  subscribing  to class  1  and  subscribing  to  class  2,  and  their  expected  surplus  becomes  0.16.  For  0.6  &lt; v , subscribing to class 2 would enable the grid users to earn the positive expected surplus. This surplus is larger than the expected surplus they would earn if they choose class 1.

## 3.1. Assumptions

The  model  assumes  that  two  regions  in  a  single  electricity  market  are  connected  by  a direct current (DC) interconnector, and suppliers in Region 1 send electricity to customers in Region 2, as shown by Figure 1. The operator of the interconnector is a transmission utility that is independent from suppliers and customers. This transmission utility is to determine the capacity and usage of the interconnector. Due to unexpected events, there exits uncertainty over  the  availability  of  the  interconnector.  When  total  demand  for  the  access  to  the interconnector exceeds its available capacity, some portion of the transmission demand must be curtailed. To efficiently ration the capacity of the interconnector, the transmission utility applies  priority  service  with  two  classes  to  interconnection  users.  The  decision  of  the transmission utility on the interruption of the demand is represented by a binary variable that takes one if the demand is served and zero otherwise. The decision of interrupting the demand depends on how much capacity is available.

The  priority  service  applied  to  the  access  to  the  interconnector  is  classified  into  two products, each with a reliability level and price that are specified prior to the subscription to the  service.  Prior  to  the  transmission  service,  interconnection  users  can  assign  the  various increments  of  their  transmission  demand  to  different  service  options  to  maximize  their expected surplus. The transmission utility is assumed to determine the price and reliability of the service  and interconnection investment to maximize  the  expected  social welfare associated with the transmission market in Figure 1.

## 3.2. Model Specifications

Specifically,  the  capacity  and  usage  of  the  interconnector together  with  users'  choice  of  the  priority  service  are  determined  so  as  to  maximize  the expected social welfare:

The  expected  welfare  in  (4)  is  defined  as  the  difference  between  the  expected  gross benefit  of  users  and  the  costs  associated  with  the  construction  and  operation  of  the interconnector.  Interconnector  users  choose  one  of  the  priority  services  or  choose  not  to obtain service, as indicated by (5). If X θ i = 0, users θ choose not to subscribe to class i . As implied by constraint (6), there exists an upper limit on the capacity of the interconnector, because of technical and environmental restrictions. The power flow must be equal to or less than available capacity of the interconnector as indicated by (7). The ratio of total hours for transmission  service  of  class i to  total  hours  of  the  contract  period  (8,760  hours)  must  be equal to or larger than the minimum value of service probability, which is designated prior to the  service.  This  constraint  is  given  by  (8).  In  practice,  interruptible  power  contracts  often specify the upper limits on duration and frequency of service interruption, which correspond to  the  minimum  value  of  service  probability.  The  quantity  constraint  on  interconnection demand is given by (9) where supply must be equal to or larger than the demand of class i users. This constraint is binding only if the transmission demand of class i is not interrupted (i.e., Yi  s  T =  1).  For  simplicity,  the  ratio  of  the  demand  assigned  to  each  class  to  the  total demand for interconnection users ( X θ i ) is assumed to be identical among time periods. The solution of the mixed integer programming problem (4) subject to constraints (5) ~ (9) yields the transmission utility's optimal choice of capacity and operation of the interconnector and the users' optimal choice of priority service classes.

Note  that  the  expected  welfare  in  (4)  does  not  contain  fixed  costs  of  interconnection investment. The inclusion of fixed costs in (4) does not affect the optimal solutions. While a two-part  tariff  is  often  applied  to  transmission  service  to  recover  fixed  costs  (Matsukawa, 2008),  for  simplicity,  fixed  costs  of  interconnection  investment  are  ignored,  and  the  unit prices  of  transmission  service  are  constant  so  that  total  expenditure  of  each  user  is proportional to his or her transmission demand in this study.

Given the  optimal  values  of Yi  s  T that  are  obtained  through  the  solution  of  the  primal problem in Section 3.2.1, the optimal prices of priority service can be computed by solving the following dual problem:

Suppose that users θ assign their entire demand to priority class i and that they do not subscribe to the other class j , j ≠ i . Then, X θ i becomes strictly positive and X θ j becomes zero.
