| Variable   | Definition                                                                                                                   |
|------------|------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| p θ i      | Price of transmission service for users θ choosing class i (US cents per kWh),                                               |
| σ i s T    | Incremental cost for raising the service reliability to r is or more than r is under state s for period T (US cents per kWh) |
| μ θ        | Expected unit surplus of users θ under the optimal choice of priority class (US cents per kWh)                               |

Table 4. Dual variable and constraint correspondence

| Primal problem   | Dual problem   |
|------------------|----------------|
| (5) h θ μ θ      | (11) X θ i     |
| (7) t T ξ sT     | (12) K         |
| (9) t T σ i s T  | (13) Q s T     |

The dual variable ξ sT represents the marginal gross benefit of expanding the capacity of the interconnector. This variable is associated with the capacity constraint (7) in the primal problem.  If  constraint  (7)  is  not  binding  under  scenario s during  period T ,  the  available capacity  of  the  interconnector  exceeds  power  flow.  Then,  there  is  no  need  to  expand  the interconnector capacity, and ξ sT becomes zero. In contrast, if constraint (7) is binding, the increase  in  the  available  capacity  of  the  interconnector  would  lead  to  some  welfare improvement, because this increase enables interconnector users to send more electricity to customers.  Thus, ξ sT becomes  strictly  positive  when  constraint  (7)  is  binding.  For  the available capacity of the interconnector to be welfare optimal, the marginal gross benefit must be equal to the marginal cost of interconnection investment. When constraint (7) is binding and ξ sT becomes strictly positive, constraint (12) in the dual problem also becomes binding and the marginal gross benefit is equal to the marginal cost of interconnection investment.

The dual variable σ i s T , an incremental cost for raising the service reliability to ris or more than ris , affects the price of priority service, as shown by (14). This dual variable is associated with constraint (9) in the primal problem. If Y i s T is zero and constraint (9) is not binding, σ i  s  T also  becomes zero, because there is no need for raising service reliability as long as transmission demand is interrupted. In contrast, if Y i s T is unity and constraint (9) is binding, σ i s T becomes strictly positive and constraint (13) in the dual problem is also binding. Then, the  sum  of σ i  s  T over  all  priority  classes  is  equal  to  the  marginal  operation  cost  of  the interconnector, unless constraint (7) is binding and ξ sT becomes positive. When constraint (7) is binding and ξ sT becomes positive, ξ sT is equal to the marginal capacity cost, and the sum of σ isT over  all  priority  classes  reflects  both  the  marginal  capacity  and  operation  costs  of  the interconnector.

As proved by Smith (1987, Theorem 3.3), total revenue earned by the transmission utility from offering priority service is equal to total costs associated with the interconnector (the sum of investment and operating costs), when λ , welfare improvement obtained by raising the capacity bound M by one unit, is zero. For λ &gt; 0, the total revenue exceeds the total costs by the product of λ and M .

## 4. AN APPLICATION

As an illustrative example, this section applies the model to a hypothetical project that would  construct  an  electricity  interconnector  to  mitigate  congestion  in  some  power  grid. Examples include frequency converters in Japan, which are used for power exchange between 50 Hz regions (the eastern part of Japan) and 60 Hz regions (the western part of Japan). As these frequency converters have often been congested, differences in prices of the wholesale power market between the east and west of Japan have emerged, and these price differences

may discourage power trade between two regions. The application of  priority  service  with two  classes  (i.e.,  firm  and  non-firm  transmission  services)  to  the  usage  of  frequency converters is expected to efficiently reduce excess demand for transmission in the short run. In the long run, the addition of an interconnector to the power grid is expected to mitigate congestion, thereby promoting interregional power trade.

## 4.1. Data Description

There  are  six  types  of  interconnection  users  who  are  supposed  to  have  identical transmission demand during peak and off-peak time periods. The contract period consists of the  peak  and  off-peak  time  periods.  Of  all  hours  of  the  contract  period  (i.e.,  8,760  hours), 4,116 hours correspond to the peak time period and 4,644 hours correspond to the off peak time period. Each type of interconnector users exhibits a different willingness to pay (WTP) for  the  priority  service  of  transmission,  as  shown  in  Table  5.  As  for  the  availability  of  the interconnector, two states are assumed to occur with the probability of 0.5 each. In state 1 ( s = 1), the ratio of the available capacity to the installed capacity of the interconnector is unity, while in state 2 ( s = 2), that ratio is reduced to 0.5, as indicated in Table 6. Some portion of transmission demand could be interrupted in state 2, while all installed capacity is available in state 1. The minimum probability that each class is served is 1.0 for both priority classes in state  1  where  no  interruption  of  transmission  demand  is  allowed.  In  state  2,  that  minimum probability for class 2 (high priority) is 1.0 while the one for class 1 (low priority) is 0.5. The peak demand for transmission amounts to 1.2 GW, which approximately corresponds to the capacity of the frequency converters connecting 50 Hz and 60 Hz regions in Japan.

Table 7 summarizes the attributes of the interconnector. The interconnector to the power grid can be installed up to 2.4 GW. The annualized cost of transmission investment is 290 US dollars per kW (assuming that one US dollar is equal to 100 yen), which is estimated from the capital  recovery factor of 0.1 and the construction cost of 3.5 billion dollars for a 1.2 GW interconnection. The unit operation cost of transmission is 0.16 cents per kWh, which is based on a transmission loss of 1.3% and marginal generation cost of 12.66 cents per kWh. These estimates  of  the  construction  cost,  transmission  loss  and  marginal  generation  cost  were obtained from ESCJ (2007).

## 4.2. Computational Results

Tables 8 to 10 summarize a computational result of the primal problem of maximizing the expected welfare subject to constraints (5)~(9) for the hypothetical project of interconnection  investment.  The  algorithm  of  a  mixed  integer  programming  in  Ibaraki  and Fukushima  (1991)  was  used  to  obtain  these  results.  As  shown  by  Table  8,  in  state  1,  the installed capacity of the interconnector is equal to the total demand for transmission during the peak time period. In state 2, the available capacity is reduced to a half of the installed capacity of the interconnector. This reduction leads to the curtailment of some peak demand, as shown by Table 9. In fact, 0.6 GW of the demand of interconnector users choosing the low priority  class  are  interrupted.  In  both  states,  off-peak  demand  for  transmission  is  never curtailed in this project. For both classes of priority, the minimum reliability constraint in (8) is  satisfied  in  both  states.  Table  10  indicates  that  all  users  choose  either  priority  class. Interconnector users with higher willingness to pay for the transmission service choose the high priority class. These users are types 4 to 6. In contrast, users with lower willingness to pay, i.e., types 1 to 3, choose the low priority class.

Given  the  optimal  values  of Yi  s  T ,  the  solution  of  the  dual  problem  (10)  subject  to constraints (11) ~ (14) yields the optimal prices of priority service for transmission. Table 11 presents the optimal prices of the service, which are defined in (14). Since all users exhibit the identical  load  pattern,  they  face  the  identical  price  of  each  priority  class  (Smith,  1987, Theorem 3.2). As expected, those who choose higher priority must pay more. Total revenue from priority service amounts to 358 million US dollars per year and is equal to the total cost of the interconnector per year, because the constraint (6) is not binding and λ is zero.

In  comparison  to  random  rationing  such  as  rotating  outage,  priority  service  increases expected social welfare through self-selection of interconnection users. The expected surplus of users θ at the optimum under priority service is given by

## 4.3. Effects of Investment Costs

The investment costs of interconnectors depend on technological progress that reduces equipment and construction  costs.  Increasing  difficulty  in  finding  appropriate  sites  and  the emergence  of  environmental  concerns  raise  expenses  for  land  use  and  environmental protection, thereby increasing investment costs of interconnectors. This section investigates the effects of a change in the investment cost of an interconnector. Specifically, the unit cost of  interconnection  investment  is  assumed  to  be  either  reduced  to  half  (i.e., c =  14.5)  or doubled (i.e., c = 58).

The 50% reduction of the unit cost of investment leads to the same capacity, power flow, interruption  of  demand,  and  choice  of  priority  classes  as  those  in  Tables  8  to  10.  This decrease in investment cost results in lower prices of priority service of transmission, i.e., p θ 2 = 3.8 cents per kWh and p θ 1 = 1.5 cents per kWh.

The 100% rise in investment costs, however, affects the optimal capacity, power flow, interruption of demand and choice of priority classes, as shown in Tables 12 to 14. Because of high costs of investment, the optimal capacity of the interconnector is decreased to 0.4 GW. This reduction of the installed capacity leads to the exclusion of some users whose expected surplus  from  priority  service  becomes  negative.  In  fact,  only  two  types  of  users  would subscribe to a priority service. Interconnector users of type 6 would choose high priority class and those of type 5 would choose low priority class. Users of other types would not receive

any service of transmission, because high prices, i.e., p θ 2 = 12.0 cents per kWh and p θ 1 = 8.2 cents per kWh, lead to the violation of the individual rationality constraint for these users. For any class of priority, these users would be worse off by subscribing to a priority service of transmission.

## 5. REGULATORY IMPACTS ON PRIORITY SERVICE

## 5.1. Alternative Forms of Regulating the Transmission Utility

Although  the  generation  of  electricity  has  been  liberalized  in  many  countries,  local monopoly is still a dominant structure for the transmission sector. In the transmission sector, how  to  regulate  the  monopolist  that  offers  priority  service  is  an  important  policy  issue, because there is a serious concern about the lack of incentives for the monopolist to properly expand the capacity of transmission facilities.

In practice, the transmission utility is subject to various forms of regulation. The welfaremaximizing operation and investment of an interconnector in the previous sections would be different  from  the  operation  and  investment  chosen  by  the  transmission  utility  subject  to regulation.  The  regulatory  impacts  on  the  interconnection  operation  and  investment  of  the transmission utility depend on how to regulate the utility.

A  straightforward  way  of  securing  a  sufficient  level  of  interconnection  capacity  is  to impose a minimum reliability constraint on the transmission utility. In electricity supply, a reliability level can be defined in terms of an engineering index, for example, as the product of  annual  duration  and  frequency  of  demand  interruption  per  user  who  experienced  power interruption. This reliability regulation prevents excessive rationing and requires the transmission  utility  to  properly  expand  the  interconnector  to  observe  minimum  reliability requirements. Although minimum reliability regulation is expected to achieve secure supply of  the  transmission  service,  it  may  result  in  the  exit  of  additional  users  who  prefer  an inexpensive  service,  thereby  reducing  welfare  if  the  regulator  sets  a  substantially  high minimum reliability standard.

Rate of return regulation, which has been a traditional form of regulating the investment of  electric  utilities,  is  an  alternative  way  of  securing  service  reliability.  Rate  of  return regulation  is  expected  to  achieve  a  secure  supply  of  service  by  providing  the  transmission utility  with  economic  incentives  for  investment.  However,  it  leads  to  an  allocatively inefficient  choice  of  input  in  production  (Crew  and  Kleindorfer,  1980).  In  contrast  with  a minimum reliability  standard,  reliability  levels  of  lower  priority  are  not  directly  regulated. Thus,  the  transmission  utility  can  lower  service  reliability  to  users  preferring  inexpensive service through an increase in market size, while it can achieve a high reliability for users preferring high priority through additional investment.

Rate of return regulation has raised a serious concern about substantial regulatory costs and the lack of incentives for the regulated utility to reduce production costs. This concern has led to the application of price cap regulation, which imposes a ceiling on the price level of service,  to  such  industries  as  telecommunications,  water,  gas,  and  electricity  supply.  In practice, price cap regulation has been applied to the transmission sector of such countries as Argentina, Norway and the United Kingdom (Armstrong et al., 1994; Braton, 1997; Rudnick and Raineri, 1997). Price cap regulation is expected to raise the number of users receiving the service  by  constraining  the  price  level  of  service.  However,  the  application  of  price  cap regulation to the transmission utility offering priority service leads to excessive rationing if the transmission utility does not have any incentive to expand the interconnection capacity.

## 5.2. A Comparison of Alternative Schemes of Regulation

The effects of alternative regulatory forms on priority service are difficult to establish in the  mixed  integer  programming  model  of  this  study.  In  this  section,  these  effects  are compared among alternative forms of regulation, using a simple numerical example with two priority classes. The willingness to pay for the service is assumed to distribute according to a function D ( v ) = 1 -v over the interval [0, 1]. The expected gross benefit is the product of the willingness to pay and reliability, i.e., u = vr . The service reliability for class i , denoted by ri , is assumed to be given by

Equation (15) implies that interconnector users in each priority class are rationed at random within that class when the capacity is insufficient to supply the entire class. Assuming that F ( Z , K ) = 1 -( Z / K ), the service reliability for class i is given by

To  compute  the  optimal  values  associated  with  the  operation  and  capacity  of  the interconnector, three first-order conditions for K , v 0 ,  and v 1  and a regulatory constraint with equality  under  each  regulatory  scheme  are  simultaneously  solved  by  a  numerical  method. These  first-order  conditions  are  derived  from  the  profit  maximization  of  the  transmission utility that determines K and vi ( i =  0,  1)  so  as  to  maximize its expected profit subject to a regulatory  constraint.  Using  (2)  and  (3)  for  the  service  prices  and  (16)  and  (17)  for  the reliability levels, the expected profit of the transmission utility, denoted by Π , is written as a function of K , v 0, and v 1 :

where R , a and P denote the minimum reliability level, an allowed rate of return and the price ceiling set by the regulator, respectively. Note that the rate of return on capital is defined as the ratio of total revenue to capacity, and a &gt; c for rate of return regulation to be effective. Under price cap regulation, there is no need for constraining the price for the low priority class, p 1, because p 2 always exceeds p 1 , as shown by (2) and (3).

Table  15  summarizes  the  optimal  values  of  key  variables  under  alternative  forms  of regulation  for  the  case c =  0.2.  Table  15  also  presents  the  welfare  maximizing  solutions ('welfare maximization') and the profit maximizing  solutions without any regulatory constraint ('no regulation'). The expected profit of the transmission utility becomes zero in the case of welfare maximization, because the revenue earned from priority service must be equal to  the  total  costs  net  of  fixed  costs,  as  indicated  at  the  end  of  Section  3.  Thus,  consumer surplus is equal to welfare under welfare maximization. Welfare maximization leads to the

largest capacity of the interconnector. In contrast, the profit -maximizing transmission utility under no regulation would install the interconnector with the smallest capacity.

For comparison, the allowed rate of return, a , is set equal to the rate of return on capital at the optimum under a minimum reliability regulation with R = 0.6. A price ceiling, P ,  is  set equal to p 2 at the optimum under rate of return regulation. Rate of return regulation results in a  larger  capacity  and  more  interconnector  users  subscribing  to  the  priority  service  than minimum  reliability  regulation.  Minimum  reliability  regulation  leads  to  higher  reliability levels and  prices for both priority classes than rate of return regulation. Note  that interconnection capacity under rate of return regulation is larger than that under no regulation. The capital-enhancing effect of rate of return regulation, which seems to be counterintuitive, is also indicated by Baumol and Klevorick (1970, Proposition 5) and by Lyon (2007, p.52).

In  comparison  to  the  profit  maximization  under  no  regulation,  minimum  reliability regulation  with R =  0.6  worsens  both  consumer  surplus  and  welfare,  while  rate  of  return regulation contributes to an improvement in consumer surplus and welfare. The improvement in consumer surplus and welfare is also found in the case of price cap regulation that sets P equal  to p 2 under  rate  of  return  regulation.  In  comparison  to  rate  of  return  regulation,  the effects on capacity and market size (1 -v 0) of price cap regulation are modest because a price ceiling, which is set equal to the optimal value of p 2 under rate of return regulation, is slightly lower than the optimal value of p 2 under no regulation. In Table 15, rate of return regulation leads to larger consumer surplus and welfare than price cap regulation.

Note that in Table 15, interconnection capacity under price cap regulation exceeds that under  no  regulation.  The  capacity-enhancing  effect  of  price  cap  regulation  seems  to  be counterintuitive. In fact, Armstrong et al. (1994, p.173) argue that the regulated firm has an incentive  to  underinvest  in  quality  for  the  given  price  level  under  price  cap  regulation, because  the  regulated  firm  neglects  the  effect  of  quality  on total consumer  surplus,  and  is interested only in the effect of quality on demand at the margin . Price cap regulation forces the regulated firm to lower the price, thereby decreasing the willingness to pay for quality at the margin. The decrease in the willingness to pay for quality leads to underinvestment.

In the case of priority service, however, the regulated firm needs to consider the effect of reliability  on total consumer  surplus,  along  with  the  effect  of  reliability  on  demand  at  the margin .  This  is  because  the  prices  of  higher  priority  classes  reflect  the  surplus  loss  of consumers choosing lower priority (Chao and Wilson, 1987). The cap on these prices forces the  regulated  firm  to  reduce  the  surplus  loss  of  lower  priority  consumers.  To  reduce  this surplus loss, the firm must raise reliability levels for lower priority consumers, and therefore must increase capacity. This capacity-enhancing effect is more or less offset by a decrease in v 0, the willingness to pay for reliability at the margin, which reduces capacity. Thus, whether price  cap  regulation  raises  capacity  depends  on  the  effects  of  reliability  on  total  consumer surplus  and  demand  at  the  margin.  In  Table  15,  the  effect  of  reliability  on  total  consumer surplus  exceeds  that  on  demand  at  the  margin,  and  price  cap  regulation  leads  to  larger capacity than no regulation.

## 6. CONCLUSION

This  study  investigates  how  the  application  of  priority  service  to  transmission  demand affects  the  operation  and  investment  of  an  electricity  interconnector,  using  a  mathematical programming model. Priority service contributes to an increase in economic efficiency in the short-run through price rationing of transmission service in congested networks. The issue of applying  priority  service  to  transmission  demand  is  how  to  secure  the  efficient  level  of reliability  in  the  long  run  through  the  appropriate  addition  of  an  interconnector  to  the congested network of electricity.

The  model  employed  in  this  study  explicitly  solves  a  mixed  integer  programming problem  in  which  the  transmission  utility's  decision  to  interrupt  interconnector  usage  is represented by a binary variable. In the model, interconnector users can assign the various increments  of  their  transmission  demand  to  different  service  options  to  maximize  the expected net benefits. Then, the transmission utility determines interconnector capacity and the interruption of transmission demand to maximize the expected social welfare. Prices of priority service of transmission are computed by solving a dual problem in the model, given the optimal decision of demand curtailment.

The results indicate that priority service for electricity transmission enables most users to raise  their  net  benefits  in  comparison  to  random  rationing.  The  results  also  imply  that  an increase in costs of building an interconnector would exclude users exhibiting relatively lower willingness to pay for transmission service. The exclusion of these interconnector users leads to a loss of social welfare through a decrease in power trade.

In the assessment of interconnection investment, a cost-benefit analysis is often applied to  examine  the  economic  impacts  of  the  investment  on  the  power  market  (Giesbertz  and Mulder, 2008; Kupper et al., 2009). In addition to the cost-benefit analysis, a welfare analysis based on a mathematical programming model of this study is useful for understanding the impact of priority service on interconnection operation and investment. The welfare analysis of this study is particularly relevant to the case where transmission rights could be allocated according  to  users'  preferences  over  priority  in  access.  Examples  include  tariff  options  of 'firm' and 'non-firm' transmission service in the PJM Regional Transmission Organization in the United States. Those who choose firm service are entitled to hold transmission rights that

enable them to use an interconnector whenever they want, and those who choose non-firm service can access the interconnector only if capacity in the interconnector exceeds the total usage of rights holders. Thus, those who choose firm service receive high priority while those who choose non-firm service receive low priority. Such priority service could be applied to electricity interconnection as an efficient method for reliability management of transmission networks.

Chapter 6

## MODELLING OF STRAIN ACCUMULATION PROCESS IN CYCLICALLY LOADED TRANSMISSION BELTS

## Barbara Zupancic and Igor Emri

Centre for Experimental Mechanics, Faculty of Mechanical Engineering, University of Ljubljana, Ljubljana, Slovenia

## ABSTRACT

This  chapter  presents  the  analysis  of  time-dependent  behaviour  of  polymeric (viscoelastic) materials under cyclic loading conditions that polymeric products, such as transmission belts, are exposed to during their operation. Time-dependent behaviour of cyclically loaded transmission belts is studied through the newly developed methodology for analyzing strain accumulation process that may be one of the mechanisms responsible for the hardening of material, crack formation, and ultimately for the failure of polymeric products.

Strain  accumulation  analysis,  presented  here,  is  based  on  the  theoretical  approach that  takes  in  consideration  time-dependent  material  properties  expressed  with  material retardation spectrum. By using developed methodology one can analyze the intensity of strain  accumulation  process  in  relation  to  different  material  retardation  spectra,  and operating  loading  conditions,  determined  by  frequency  of  cyclic  loading,  number  of loading cycles, and belt-drive geometry. It turns out that critical operating frequency at which  the  strain  accumulation  in  the  material  is  the  most  intensive,  depends  on  the material  retardation  time  (location  of  retardation  spectrum),  whereas  the  magnitude  of accumulated  strain  depends  on  the  strength  of  corresponding  discrete  spectrum  lines. Thus, the spectrum distribution defines the intensity and the magnitude of accumulated strain and is, in this aspect, one of the most important functions for predicting durability of cyclically loaded polymeric products through proposed strain accumulation approach. Based  on  here  presented  analysis  a  new designing  criterion is  proposed  for  use  in engineering applications for selecting a proper material for general drive-belt operations.

Keywords: transmission  belts,  cyclic  loading,  strain  accumulation,  polymers,  elastomers, time-dependent; mechanical spectrum, durability, failure

ISBN: 978-1-61761-300-5

© 2011 Nova Science Publishers, Inc.

## INTRODUCTION

Since  noticeable  differences  between  the  fatigue  process  in  polymers  and  metals  have been  observed,  one  should  be  aware  of  the  basic  mechanisms  that  are  responsible  for  the fatigue  failure  in  both  cases  in  order  to  model  the  process  appropriately.  Fatigue  failure mechanism in  metals  is,  in  general,  a  process  of  crack  initiation,  propagation,  and  failure. Fatigue  damage  in  metals  is  cycle  dependent,  but  basically  remains  independent  of  test frequency. Unlike that of metals, fatigue response of polymers strongly depends on polymers' viscoelastic effects [1].

There are two basic competitive mechanisms that govern the failure of cyclically loaded polymers, which are both affected by the frequency of cyclic loading. At higher frequencies usually  dominates  local  overheating  of  the  material  due  to  the  hysteresis  dissipation  of mechanical energy within each cycle of loading. Whereas, at lower frequencies the fatigue failure is essentially caused by localized strain accumulation process that leads to the crack initiation, followed by its propagation, and failure of product's functionality.

Transmission  belts  of  different  types,  as  some  of  the  most  widely  used  rubber-based structural elements in the automotive industry, are one of the representative products that are exposed  to  cyclic  loading  conditions  during  their  operation.  Belts'  durability  is  a  critical element for sustainable operation of the engines. Thus, understanding the mechanisms which affect their durability is extremely important. Their rather complex hybrid structures consist of  components  with  very  different  time-dependent  (viscoelastic)  mechanical  properties  [2]. Although  the  components  of  belts  exhibit  time-dependent  behaviour,  several  papers  [3-5] consider  rubbers  and  cord-rubber  composites  as  linear  elastic  materials  in  the  stress-strain analysis for the composites failure.

In  the  present  chapter  we  focused  on  the  study  of  strain  accumulation  process  in transmission  belts  by  developing  and  applying  the  theoretical  model  that  is  based  on  the constitutive law of stress-strain relations for viscoelastic (time-dependent) materials.

Due to the time-dependent viscoelastic nature of polymeric materials we can expect that under cyclic loading conditions material will undergo a combination of creep and retardation process within each loading cycle. At certain conditions, that are implied by the frequency of cyclic loading and geometry of the belt-drive, the retardation process between two loading cycles  cannot  be  fully  completed  to  a  strain-free  state.  Therefore  material  enters  the  next loading  cycle  with  a  strain  residual.  This  means  that  the  strain  starts  to  accumulate,  and consequently leads to the hardening of material, crack formation, and ultimately to its failure.

First part of this chapter is dedicated to short introduction on material functions' in timeand frequency-domain. Since material time-dependency, which governs the product's durability, is most intensive in shear [6] we discuss the shear creep compliance, ( ) J t , and the corresponding retardation spectrum, ( ) L ξ , in more detail.

Starting from the constitutive relation between the shear stress loading conditions and the shear strain response the next part of this chapter presents development of the model for strain accumulation analysis. As a first step, cyclic loading conditions in terms of the shear stress are modelled by implementing geometry parameters of transmission belt and angular velocity of rotating pulleys. By introducing modelled loading conditions and time-dependent material

properties (retardation spectrum) into the constitutive law the strain accumulation model is obtained.

Implementation  of  the  developed  model  is  then  demonstrated  on  the  3-parametric material  model  presented  with  the  single  spectrum  line.  Rather  simple  description  of  the material  time  dependence  enables  to  treat  derived  equations  analytically,  and  analyze  the importance of the spectrum line location (retardation time) and its strength in relation to the strain  accumulation  process  intensity.  This  analysis  shows  that  critical  angular  velocity,  at which the strain accumulation in the material is the most intensive, depends on the material retardation  time,  whereas  the  magnitude  of  accumulated  strain  depends  on  the  strength  of corresponding spectrum line. Thus the mechanical spectrum proves to be the most important material function for predicting durability of cyclically loaded polymeric products.

Due  to  the  fact,  that  real  time-dependent  materials  posses  more  complex  spectra, continuation of this chapter turns to analysis of the effect of mechanical spectrum distribution, i.e.  'shape' of material time-dependent property [6], on the process of strain accumulation. The  analysis  is  demonstrated  on  five  different  synthetic  spectra  that  mimic  most  of  the polymeric materials used in production of drive belts and other polymeric products. We also analyze the effect of operating angular velocity, and belt geometry one the process of strain accumulation.

Results  show  that  by  knowing  spectrum  distribution  of  the  time-dependent  material property we may predict the critical operating angular velocity that may lead to the highest level  of  accumulated  strain.  Based  on  these  results  the  durability  criterion  is  proposed  for predicting the life-span of cyclically loaded polymeric products.

Based on the theoretical treatment special software DAB ('Durability Analysis of Belts') was developed. The software enables user-friendly systematic study of the strain accumulation  process  as  function  of  (a)  different-spectrum-distribution-shapes,  (b)  belt geometry, (c) number of loading cycles, and (d) operating angular velocity. Features of this DAB software are presented at the end of this chapter.

## TIME-DEPENDENT BEHAVIOR OF POLYMERS

## 2.1. Viscoelastic Functions

Polymers  belong  to  viscoelastic  materials  which  are  characterized  by  their  timedependent mechanical properties. In practice, this means that the functionality and applicability  of  polymeric  products  can  change  considerably  after  a  certain  period  of  time. This  may  lead  to  appearance  of  defects  and  loss  of  product  functionality.  It  is  therefore important to understand material functions that describe time-dependency and durability of polymeric products.

The stress-strain relations describing the mechanical behaviour of polymers are given in the  form  of  convolution  integrals.  Material  constants  are  replaced  by  material  functions defined as the response of material to a step load in the form of stress or strain. [6, 7]. The form of material function depends on the mode of loading, i.e., static or time-dependent, and dynamic  or  frequency-dependent  type  of  loading.  There  are  21  material  functions  that

describe viscoelastic behaviour of solid polymers, seven of which are determined by static, and 14 by dynamic methods of measurements (see Table 1).

Material functions determined in shear represent changes of specimen geometry, whereas bulk material functions represent changes of its volume. While the bulk material functions change  with  time  very  little,  the  shear  material  functions  show  very  distinctive  time dependency. This means that time-dependent behaviour, which determines product's durability, depends mostly on shear [6].

## 2.2. Material Functions Expressed in Terms of Discrete Retardation Spectra

Material functions are in principle transfer  functions.  In  general,  for  each  excitation  to which a system has been exposed we may find the corresponding transfer function. Timedependent structural rearrangements caused by the stress excitation may be represented by the 'transfer  function'  which we  call  the  retardation  spectrum. Calculation of the material functions from the retardation spectrum requires integration or summation if the corresponding discrete form of the relation is used. These procedures belong to a group of so-called straight solutions, and they are numerically stable. Thus, knowing a spectrum  that  keeps  the  complete  information  on  time  dependence  of  the  material,  allows exact calculation (within computational accuracy) of all corresponding time- and frequencydomain material functions.

Discrete retardation spectrum will in continuation serve as input information on the timedependent behaviour of the material that is exposed to cyclic loading.

## 3. MODELLING OF THE TIME-DEPENDENT STRAIN ACCUMULATION PROCESS IN TRANSMISSION BELTS

## 3.1. Loading Conditions

During the normal operation of transmission belt certain segments of the belt structure are loaded with the tooth-like periodical loading in terms of the shear stress. More detailed analysis  of  the  stress-strain  state  of  the  operating  belt  can  be  found  in  [14].  The  loading conditions of the belt were determined through the finite element analysis, assuming elastic behaviour  of  all  belt's  components.  The  belt  was  pre-stressed  with  a  certain  force F and loaded with a desired torque M , so that the pre-stressing force was adequately divided into the strand  on  the  tension, 1 F ,  and  on  the  slack  side, 2 F .  Geometry  of  the  belt-drive  was determined by the distance between the two pulleys, l , and the radius of the two pulleys, R , Figure 2.

Stress-strain analysis presented in [14] has shown that for selected point on the tooth of the belt the time evolution of the shear stress within one loading cycle can be modelled with the tooth-like function schematically presented in Figure 3.

Time 1 t in  Figure  3  denotes  the  moment  when  selected  point  on  the  belt  enters  the contact  with  the  driving  pulley,  and 2 t indicates  the  moment,  when  it  leaves  the  driving pulley. 0 t is the time period of one loading cycle.

The  time-dependent  shear  strain  response  of  a  viscoelastic  material  to  the  shear  stress loading may be expressed with constitutive law described in Eq. (2). If we introduce Eq. During the loading phase (from 1 t to 2 t ) the material starts to creep. At point 2 t the load is  removed and the retardation process begins. Under certain conditions, determined by the drive belt geometry and the operating angular velocity, the retardation process between two loading  cycles  cannot  be  completed  to  a  strain  free  state.  Thus,  the  residual  strain  in  each cycle, ΔΓ , will start to sum up from cycle to cycle.

Developed Eqs. 26-30 present theoretical tool to model the strain accumulation, and to evaluate the magnitude of accumulated strain. Input parameters of the model that can affect the  magnitude,  either  within  each  loading  cycle  or  within  completed  number  of  loading cycles, are:

- 0 τ , shear stress intensity;
- l R κ = , non-dimensional geometry parameter with non-linear effect to the magnitude of accumulated strain;
- N , number of completed loading cycles with non-linear effect to the magnitude of accumulated strain;
- ω ,  operating  angular  velocity  that  determines  time  period  of  one  loading  cycle (frequency), and is in non-linear relation to the magnitude of accumulated strain;
- material  parameters, { } , ; 1, 2, , i i L i K λ = " ,  i.e., K retardation  times, i λ ,  and  K corresponding spectrum lines, ( ) i i L L λ = .

We will proceed now with the analysis of the effects of these input parameters on the process of the strain accumulation, and demonstrate applicability of the proposed methodology.

## STRAIN ACCUMULATION ANALYSIS

As it was analyzed in [14], the contribution of accumulated strain in each cycle, n ΔΓ , will be smaller and smaller with increasing consecutive number, n . Besides that, this paper reveals  that  for  very  small  or  very  large  operating  angular  velocities  strain  accumulation contributions n ΔΓ will diminish, and that there practically will not be any strain accumulation. From this perspective there should exist critical angular velocity, 0 CR &lt;ω &lt;&lt; ∞ at which the magnitude of accumulated strain reaches its maximum.

## 4.1. Strain Accumulation Process in Material Modelled with Single Spectrum Line

In order to obtain analytical solution for the critical angular velocity and further on, the maximal  magnitude  of  accumulated  strain,  the  simplest  3-parametric  description  of  timedependent material is used. In this case the material property of creep compliance is presented with a single spectrum line as follows,

This  finding  implies,  that  mechanical spectrum is the most important material function for predicting the durability of dynamically loaded polymeric products.

To  get  further  insight  into  this  investigation,  while  keeping  the  material  property described with a single spectrum line, we analyze here how the belt drive geometry and the consecutive  number  of  loading  cycle  determine  critical  angular  velocity  and  consequently affect the maximal magnitude of accumulated strain. Further on, by applying Eq. 35 we can demonstrate how the critical angular velocity for determined single spectrum line depends on every individual consecutive loading cycle and different values of geometry parameter, Figure 12.

From this diagram we may observe that for a given geometry of the belt drive the critical angular velocity is increasing with each consecutive loading cycle (shown with solid black line).  From  Figure  12  we  may  also  observe  that  for  larger R l / κ = ,  i.e.,  for  longer  belts, critical angular velocity will also move towards the higher angular velocities (dashed black line).  Thus,  proper  selection  of  belt  geometry  offers  possibility  to  bring  critical  angular velocity out of the operating range of the belt and avoid the most critical conditions for strain accumulation.

Let us now consider the most severe conditions. This is when the belt operates with the critical angular velocity in every consecutive cycle. The result is presented in Figure 13. Of course, such operating conditions are very unlikely as the operating angular velocity should change in every loading cycle. From Figure 13 we clearly see how rapidly accumulated strain in each consecutive cycle decreases for larger values of R l / κ = , i.e., for longer belts.

From  the  diagram  above  we  may  also  observe  that  under  critical  loading  conditions, when  in  each loading cycle the maximal  possible magnitude  of accumulated  strain contribution is generated, the process of strain accumulation is the most intensive in the first few loading cycles, and it rapidly decreases with the increased number of loading cycles. This means that the process of accumulation slows down.

Summing up the maximal possible contributions of accumulated strain in each loading cycle yields the evolution of cumulative accumulated strain as a function of loading cycles and belt geometry. The results are shown in Fig 14 and represents 3D representation of Eq. 37. The diagram allows two important conclusions: (i) belt will certainly fail if the belt drive

operates close to the critical angular velocity, and (ii) longer belts will always exhibit slower strain accumulation process, and will generally have longer life span. .

## 4.2. Strain Accumulation Process in Materials Modelled With Complex Spectrum Distributions

In  the  previous  section  we  have  used  a  rather  simple  description  of  the  material  time dependence in order to analyze the strain accumulation process analytically in a close form manner. Due to the fact, that real time-dependent materials posses more complex spectra, we proceed to  a  more  realistic  description  of  their  time-dependent  behaviour  by  selecting  five different  distributions  of  discrete  retardation  spectra  that  mimic  most  of  the  polymeric materials used in production of drive belts and other polymeric products:

- (i) box distribution , ( ) L B λ ,
- (ii) Gauss distribution , ( ) L G λ ,
- (iii) Lorentzian distribution, ( ) L L λ ,
- (iv) ramp distribution, ( ) L R λ , and
- (v) wedge distribution, ( ) L W λ .

We  generate  41  spectrum  lines  for  all  five  spectra,  ranging  from  log 10 λ = to log 10 λ = , with the spectrum line density - two lines per decade.

While  we  as  a  first  step  chose  different  shapes  of  the  retardation  spectra,  and  then calculate the corresponding response functions, ( ) J t ,  we should be aware that in reality the situation  is  just  opposite.  The  retardation  spectrum  can  not  be  measured  directly,  but calculated by using one of the existing algorithms, (e.g., the Windowing Algorithm [9]) from measured creep compliance response functions in time, ( ) J t .

## 4.2.1.  The  effect  of  material  spectrum  distribution  on  strain  accumulation  regarding geometry

As  it  was  analyzed  in  details  in  the  paper  [14]  for  a  single  spectrum  line  material description,  it  turned  out  that  belt-drive  geometry,  expressed  with / l R κ = ,  and  timedependent properties of the viscoelastic material used in belts manufacturing are 'interrelated'. Analysis of the effect of geometry on strain accumulation process for a single spectrum line has shown that process would be less intensive for bigger values / l R κ = (i.e., longer belts).

How  these  findings  are  applicable  for  more  complex  spectra,  we  will  analyze  in continuation.

In  order  to  analyze  only  the  effect  of  geometry  on  the  strain  accumulation  process  by using Eq. 30 one should fix the operating angular velocity, and the number of loading cycles after which we are interested in accumulated strain.

Since we know from investigation above, that operating conditions strongly influence the intensity of accumulation, we analyze the cumulative strain accumulated as function of κ for two different angular velocities, 1/sec ω = ,  and 100/sec ω = after 10000 N = loading cycles. Since the 2 κ &gt; according to its definition, and in most cases does not go beyond, let say, 40, we show the diagrams ( , 1/ sec, 10000) N κ ω Γ = = , and ( , 100/sec, 10000) N κ ω Γ = = on the scale of (2, 40] κ ∈ for all five spectra. Diagrams are displayed in Figures 17 and 18.

Figure 17. Diagram of cumulative accumulated strain, ( , 1/ sec, 10000) N κ ω Γ = = as function of geometry parameter, / l R κ = , for five different material spectra.

<!-- image -->

Figure 18. Diagram of cumulative accumulated strain, ( , 100/sec, 10000) N κ ω Γ = = as function of geometry parameter, / l R κ = , for five different material spectra.

<!-- image -->

From the two figures above one can see for the both cases, i.e., for 1/sec ω = , and for 100/sec ω = , that the magnitude of strain accumulation decreases with increasing values of κ parameter for all selected time-dependent material properties. From practical point of view we  may  conclude,  that  in  case  of  any  viscoelastic  material  that  has  similar  spectrum distribution as one of the analyzed spectra, the process of strain accumulation will be slower for longer belt in comparison to the shorter one, when they are manufactured from the same material.

However, if we compare the evolution of accumulated strain for different materials, we observe the difference in magnitudes of accumulated strain, especially in the case of smaller

values of κ . For example, a drive belt made of material with Lorentzian type of retardation spectra  that  exhibits  the  most  intensive  strain  accumulation  process  for  small κ in comparison to other materials, shows practically the same magnitude of accumulated strain for  large  values  of κ as  other  materials.  This  brings  the  important  information  on  the importance  of  the  shape  (distribution)  of  the  material  spectrum  from  which  the  belt  is manufactured.

Besides that, by comparing the accumulated strain in Figures 17 and 18 (scales on the diagrams  are  the  same)  we  can  observe  twice  smaller  magnitudes  at  operating  angular velocity 1/sec ω = than are the magnitudes of accumulated strain at 100/sec ω = .

Diagrams  of  cumulative accumulated strain ( , 3, 1/ sec) N κ ω Γ = = as function of the number of loading cycles, N , are schematically drawn in Figures 19-23 with dashed lines, and presented together in Figures 24 and 25. From the diagrams in Figures 24, and 25 we may observe the significant difference in the magnitudes of accumulated strain considering two different operating angular velocities. In the case of 1/sec ω = the material with the Gauss retardation spectrum will experience the most  intensive  strain  accumulation,  while  in  the  case  of 100/sec ω = the  material  with Lorentzian spectrum distribution.

But nevertheless, in both cases the evolution of accumulated strain has similar tendency. After first few cycles the magnitude of accumulated strain increases faster, while later on still increases,  but  slower  and  slower.  This  was  actually  already  observed  in  the  case  of  single spectrum  line  when  the  contribution  of  accumulated  strain  in  every  cycle  was  analyzed.  It turned out that the maximal possible contribution to the magnitude of accumulated strain is getting smaller and smaller with every next loading cycle, what consequently points out that the strain accumulation process slows down with the number of loading cycles.

Starting from the same diagrams in Figures 19-23 we need to fix the number of loading cycles, N ,  in  order  to  analyze  the  influence  of  operating  angular  velocity, ω ,  for  a  given geometry, 3 κ = . Evolution of the accumulated strain as a function of angular velocity after 10000 loading cycles is for all five spectra schematically presented in Figures 19-23 with the black  solid  lines,  and  for  comparison  among  spectra  brought  together  on  the  diagram  in Figure 26.

From  Figure  26  it  can  be  seen  that  magnitude  of  accumulated  strain  within  certain operating angular velocity range is strongly related to the time-dependent material property with certain spectrum distribution.

For  example,  the box spectrum  where  the  strength  of  all  spectrum  lines  within  certain range of retardation times is the same, exhibits the same magnitude of accumulated strain in the  whole  analyzed  range  of  operating  angular  velocities.  From  this  perspective,  every operating angular velocity is critical, since it leads to the maximal magnitude of accumulated strain.

Figure 26. Diagram of cumulative accumulated strain, ( , 3, 10000) N ω κ Γ = = as function of operating angular velocity, ω , for five different material spectra.

<!-- image -->

In  the  case  of wedge spectrum  the  magnitude  of  accumulated  strain  is  negligible  in comparison  to  other  spectra.  In  Figure  26  the  magnitude  of  accumulated  strain  for wedge spectrum is multiplied by factor 100 for better visualization of results.

Ramp spectrum exhibits more intensive strain accumulation at lower angular velocities, which corresponds the fact that spectrum lines at high response times are responsible for the strain accumulation at low drive-belt angular velocities (high frequencies), and vice versa .

The most critical from the point of view of the largest magnitude of accumulated strain are  the Gauss and Lorentzian spectra.  Since  their  spectrum  distributions  are  bell-shaped, which means that spectrum lines with bigger intensity are placed in narrow area of retardation times,  these  spectra  exhibit  the  most  intensive  strain  accumulation  in  narrower  range  of operating angular velocity. From this we may conclude, that material with narrower peak in spectrum  distribution  will  exhibit  the  most  intensive  strain  accumulation  in  the  narrower angular  velocity  domain,  while  for  material  with  broader  spectrum  distribution  broader operating angular velocity area will be responsible for the most intensive strain accumulation. Besides  that,  we  can  expect  higher  magnitude  of  the  accumulated  strain  for  material  with narrower spectrum peak in comparison to broader spectrum.

In  respect  to  these  findings  we  can,  for  a  given  spectrum,  define  the  critical  angular velocity area, where  the strain accumulation  process is the most  intensive, and  by determination of critical operating conditions obtain the information on durability prediction of the belt made from a selected material.

## PROPOSED DESIGNING CRITERION FOR DURABILITY PREDICTION

Based on the important conclusions on interrelation between the time-dependent material spectrum  and  critical  operating  conditions  we  propose  designing  criterion  to  determine critical  angular  velocity  area,  where  the  strain  accumulation  is  the  most  intensive.  To demonstrate the establishment of criterion, we selected representation of accumulated strain as a function of angular velocity and the number of cycles for Gauss spectrum. Let us set the hypothetical upper limit of accumulated strain that could lead to the failure of the material. In Figure 27 is shown with the cross-section plane (solid black lines).

By projecting the cross-section curve (dashed black line) onto N ω plane, we obtain the critical  angular  velocity  area  as  shown  in  Figure  28.  Within  the  'critical  area'  of  angular velocities we may expect the most intensive strain accumulation.

Determining  other  levels  of  accumulated  strain  with  the  cross-section  planes,  and projecting cross-section curves onto N ω we  obtain  iso-curves  of  the  same  magnitude of accumulated strain as a function of operating angular velocity and number of loading cycles.

The  two  arrows  in  the  diagram  present  the  following  hypothetical  situation:  if  the operating angular velocity is 100/ sec ω = ,  the  process  of  strain  accumulation  will  lead to the critical level of accumulated strain, and the failure of the cyclically loaded product will occur after about 7000 loading cycles.

Diagram in Figure 28 is a representative diagram for making prediction on the expected life  span  (durability)  of  cyclically  loaded  polymeric  products,  since  for  a  given  material brings the information on how many loading cycles can lead to the failure in relation to the cyclic operating conditions.

Figure 27. 3D diagram of cumulative accumulated strain, ( , , 3) N ω κ Γ = , as a function loading cycles, N , and angular velocity, ω , for Gauss spectrum with the cross-section plane that determines hypothetical critical upper limit of accumulated strain.

## DAB - 'DURABILITY ANALISYS OF BELTS' SOFTWARE

Based  on  presented  theoretical  approach  for  strain  accumulation  analysis  the  assigned software  DAB  ('Durability  Analysis  of  Belts')  was  developed.  It  enables  user  friendly systematic study of different effects on the strain accumulation process. It offers possibility either  to  generate  six  types  of  retardation  spectra  by  determining  coefficients  in  analytical expressions for the box , Gauss , Lorentzian , ramp , wedge , and kobeko spectrum distributions, or to import spectrum of the real material for the further strain accumulation analysis. When entering  the  information  on  the  parameter,  effect  of  which  will  be  analyzed  (number  of loading cycles, operating angular velocity, or geometry of the belt-drive), one has to fix the values for the other two. In the Figure 29 the interface of the DAB software is presented.

In  continuation  we  briefly  present  features  of  DAB  software  capabilities  on  several examples.  As  a first case selection of material spectrum  is demonstrated, including determination of spectrum parameters. We may choose among 6 different types of analytical descriptions of time-dependent material properties; each type has specific parameters that is possible  to  vary.  After  choosing  the  type  of  material  spectrum  the  spectrum  diagram  will appear  in  the  upper  graphical  window  In  Figure  30  the  choice  of Gauss spectrum  and  its parameters is presented.

Figure 29. User interface of DAB ('Durability Analysis of Belts') software for the strain accumulation analysis based on developed theoretical approach.

## CONCLUSION

Within  this  chapter  the  theoretical  approach  for  analysis  of  strain  accumulation  in cyclically loaded transmission belts was presented. Developed approach takes in consideration time-dependent material behaviour expressed with material retardation spectrum. By  using  developed  methodology  one  can  analyze  the intensity of strain accumulation  process  in  relation  to  different  material  retardation  spectra,  and  operating loading conditions, determined by the angular velocity (frequency) of cyclic loading, number of loading cycles, and belt-drive geometry.

It turns out that there exist critical operating conditions at which the strain accumulation in the material is the most intensive, and that this critical conditions are strongly interrelated to  the  time-dependent  material  properties.  In  this  respect,  the  critical  angular  velocity  of rotating pulleys of the belt-drive depends on the retardation times of the material, while the magnitude of accumulated strain depends on the strength of corresponding discrete spectrum lines.

Considering different material spectrum distributions we may conclude that the area of critical angular velocities widens, when widening the peak of material spectrum. This means, that if we want to limit the area of critical operating conditions to a narrow vicinity of certain angular velocity the material should be manipulated in a way of getting the spectrum peak as narrow as possible. On the other hand, when widening the material spectrum peak the area of critical operating velocities widens, but the magnitude of accumulated strain is getting lower, which may in certain cases be even more important; for example, when the possible operating conditions extend through the wide range of loading angular velocities (frequencies).

Based on this conclusion it would be possible for cyclically loaded polymeric products to give a suggestion on appropriate selection of time-dependent material properties, considering given operating conditions, so as to control and avoid the most intensive strain accumulation process.

From  this  perspective  the  developed  theoretical  approach  could  be  incorporated  as  a complementary software part of experimental methods for time-dependent material characterization.  Based  on  measured  time-dependent  creep  compliance  of  the  material  the retardation spectrum can be calculated by using one of the existing algorithms, e.g. [9], and after  that  submitted  to  the  strain  accumulation  analysis  in  order  to  give  prediction  on material's durability.

Editor: Dana M. Welton

ISBN: 978-1-61761-300-5

## STEPPED TRANSMISSION LINE AND IMPEDANCE MATCHING

## Chunqi Qian *

National Institute of Health, Bethesda, Maryland

## ABSTRACT

Transmission line is routinely used as power transfer medium. A propagating wave will experience reflection at the boundary between the transmission line and the loading device. Because the wave reflection is generally undesirable for efficient power transfer, impedance transformation is required to match the load impedance with the characteristic impedance  of  the  transmission  line.  Circuit  networks  composed  of  discrete  reactive components, such as inductors, capacitors and transformers are often used as impedance transformation devices. Versatile as they are, each particular network can only match a specific  range  of  load  impedance.  And  in  order  to  match  the  impedance  of  different loading  components,  different  circuit  networks  need  to  be  used.  In  many  practical circumstances however, there is a need to use the same impedance matching network to cover a broader range of load impedance, thus greatly simplify the circuit design. In this chapter, we will introduce the concept of impedance matching by segmented transmission line which consists of a short section of uniform transmission line inserted with multiple sliding  dielectric  segments.  We  will  demonstrate  that  the  proper  positioning  of  each individual element can result in the total cancellation of internal reflections. And a broad range of load impedance can be matched without the necessity to reconnect the circuit. After  a  brief  review  of  the  impedance  transformation  relation,  we  will  deduce  an analytical expression for the position of each dielectric segment. A pictorial description of  the  analytical  method  will  be  presented  with  the  language  of  Smith  chart.  The applications and restrictions of stepped transmission line will be addressed at the end of the chapter.

* Corresponding author: Email: qianchq@hotmail.com Tel: 301-435-2146, Fax: 301-480-2558

## INTRODUCTION

Impedance  matching  [1]  is  one  of  the  most  important  concepts  in  communication electronics. It ensures optimal power efficiency between the antenna and the amplifier. Many different circuits have been proposed for impedance matching to an RF antenna. The most widely known may be the L-network of two adjustable capacitors [2-5]. A 'tuning' capacitor in  parallel  with  the  inductor  cancels  the  inductive  reactance  of  the  antenna,  and  a  series 'matching' capacitor steps down the impedance to match the characteristic impedance of the input cable, which is normally 50 Ω . The presence of two independent adjustments, which is a  common  element  in  matching  networks,  reflects  the  need  to  control  both  the  real  and imaginary parts of the impedance. The widespread availability of good nonmagnetic variable capacitors is an important reason for their popularity, this simple method, however, can only match  the  load  impedance  within  a  specific  range  if  the  circuit  connection  is  not  to  be changed. Moreover, adjustable capacitors are often buffered by fixed capacitors for protection against high voltages, but generally at a significant cost to reduced tuning or matching range.

Transmission  lines  have  often  been  employed  as  impedance  transformers  in  multiresonance  circuits  [6-8].  The  transmission  line  elements  are  used  to  provide  impedance matching  and  to  improve  isolation  between  the  various  channels.  Here,  the  length  of  the transmission lines can be used to allow the adjustable capacitors to be located remotely from the  antenna, which is often advantageous [9, 10]. While adjustable transmission lines have been used in some cases [11], most often commercial capacitors are the adjustable elements. The  'Apex'  (A.  Palmer)  and  related  commercial  designs  (Varian  Inc.)  are  based  on concentric coaxial capacitors that exhibit some transmission line behavior [12]. Tuning and matching  adjustments  are  carried  out  by  inserting  or  withdrawing  sleeves  of  dielectric material  between  the  shield  and  inner  conductor.  These  coaxial  capacitors  withstand  high voltages  and  have  been  shown  to  have  low  loss  [12].  But  the  mechanical  implementation requires  close  tolerances  and  the  electrical  design  requires  computer  simulation  of  the network  because  of  transmission  line  effects  and  inductance  in  the  adjustable  capacitors. Also, segmented transmission lines employing a single dielectric slug have been incorporated into solid state NMR probes developed at Bruker Biospin, Inc.

Here,  we  demonstrate  a  method  of  adjustable  impedance  matching  based  on  a  coaxial transmission  line  within  which  two  quarter-wave  dielectric  slugs  can  be  independently positioned. The line can be thought of as a series of four connected transmission lines, two of which have a lower characteristic impedance due to the presence of the dielectric slugs. By changing the positions of dielectric slugs, the lengths of two of the transmission line segments can be changed and the voltage reflection at the input point can be minimized by the proper cancellation of internal reflections. Tuners based on this principle are used commercially for automatic testing of power  amplifiers, where they are known  as 'double slug' or 'interferometric' load-pull tuners, and the slugs are typically driven by computer-controlled motors [13]. The basic structure is derived from the stepped transformer [14] which has long been used in wireless communication. There are obvious attractive features of the adjustable segmented line-it requires no adjustable capacitors, and it has no sliding contacts that might wear. Its long cylindrical shape is well suitable for remote tuning. But as we will show, this tuner  has  advantageous electrical  properties  as  well.  It  is  capable  of  matching  a  very  wide range  of  loads,  both  inductive  and  capacitive,  unlike  the  L-networks  used  in  both  lumped

element and fixed-length transmission line circuits. This interesting feature has allowed us to interchange  the  load  antenna  easily  without  modifications  to  the  tuning  circuit,  so  that  the same tuning circuit can be used for different loads.

In this chapter, we will first go through the principles of segmented transmission lines, deriving formulas to predict the required position of the tuning slugs and the power efficiency for a given load. We will then describe how this design has been incorporated into our nuclear magnetic  resonance  probe  [15]  and  compare  the  performance  with  conventional  matching networks. Finally, we will explore the potential applications of the segmented transmission line  and  demonstrate  that,  for  a  high  quality  antenna,  this  approach  works  best  when  the antenna  is  roughly  tuned  and  matched  by  fixed  elements  to  the  variable  segmented transmission line.

Our design is shown in Figure 2a and consists of a coaxial tube into which two dielectric slugs of dielectric constant ε r have been inserted. The empty coaxial tube has a characteristic impedance of Z 0, equal to the impedance of the input cable, so that there are four electrically different segments T1 through T4. Segments T1 and T3 have the same characteristic impedance Z 0 as the input cable. In  the  above  analysis  we  have  derived  equations  for  the  lengths l 1 and l 3 of  our  four element  segmented  transmission  line  in  terms  of  the  load  reflection  coefficient  and  the relative dielectric constant of the slugs. We can draw two additional conclusions from Eqs. (13) and (14).  First, the requirement that the right hand sides are less than unity means that the four element segmented transmission line is only capable of matching loads for which

<!-- formula-not-decoded -->

This relation defines the maximum radius of the circle on the Smith chart within which the load impedance can be matched. We define this circle as the maximum reflection circle. Slugs with larger dielectric constants will be able to match a wider range of loads. Highly reflective loads will need to have | ρ 1| initially reduced by fixed capacitors or inductors, but a segmented transmission line can be used effectively as the adjustable component. Second, we can determine the length L in free-space wavelengths λ free of an adjustable transmission line of the type in Figure 2a that can match all loads within the limit given by Eq. (15). We have seen that l 1 is never more than λ free /2, and l 3 is never more than λ free /4. The two dielectric slugs each have a length of / 4 free r λ ε . So the total length of the adjustable line is

<!-- formula-not-decoded -->

Adjustable slugs with a larger dielectric constant will lead to a shorter adjustable line, but never less than 3 λ free /4. For NMR probes, it is clear that the adjustable transmission line will be most useful when L is less than the overall length of the RF probe, so that the adjustable line fits completely into the probe.

It  may  be  instructive  to  consider  the  impedance  matching  process  of  the  segmented transmission line from the familiar perspective of the reflection coefficient. For purposes of this discussion, we will start from the matched impedance at the input port ( ρ 5=0), and move along the segmented transmission line toward the load, ending up at ρ 1. This corresponds to a counter  clockwise  rotation  along  the  transformation  trajectory  that  starts  at  the  center  of Figure 2b. We assume that ρ 1, the reflection coefficient of the antenna, lies on the edge of the disk defined by Eq. (15). The goal is to move from the origin all the way out to ρ 1 at the limit of  the  matching  range  of  the  four  segment  transmission  line.  The  first  step  is  the  lowimpedance  quarter  wave  segment  T4,  which  increases  the  magnitude  of  the  reflection coefficient  to  a  negative  real  value ρ 4.  The  next  segment,  T3,  has  the  same  characteristic

impedance as the input cable, so it transforms only the phase of the reflection coefficient to a positive real value ρ 3. In this case, the length of T3 is one quarter wave, which, as indicated by Eq.  (13),  is  required  to  match  the  largest  values  of  | ρ 1|.  The  low-impedance  quarter  wave segment  T2  again  increases  the  magnitude  of  the  reflection  coefficient  to ρ 2.  Subsequent transformation along the dashed circle to ρ 1  is  carried out by segment T1. The radius of the dashed  circle,  indicating  the  largest  value  of  | ρ 1|  that  can  be  matched  by  the  four-segment transmission line, is given by Eq. (15).

As  seen  above,  a  segmented  transmission  line  matching  network  uses  a  series  of mismatched  transmission  lines  to  transform  the  impedance  of  load  to  the  characteristic impedance  of  the  input  cable.  These  local  mismatches  lead  to  standing  waves  on  the transmission line segments that contribute additional loss. While a full consideration of loss in segmented transmission line networks is beyond the scope of this paper, we did carry out a numerical evaluation to estimate the loss from standing waves in our prototype network. Here we briefly illustrate how to set up the problem. For the loss calculation, it is more convenient to define the reflection coefficient with respect to Z0,i , the complex characteristic impedance of the i th transmission line, than to the characteristic impedance of the input cable as in Eq. (3). To emphasize the difference, we designate this reflection coefficient ' i ρ .

The power efficiency ( PE ) of the segmented transmission line is just the ratio of the power dissipated in the load versus the incident power from the input cable:

Numerical evaluation of the power efficiency for a given load impedance using Eq. (21) is straightforward as long as the propagation constants of the transmission line segments are known.

In  the  above  discussion,  we  mainly  dealt  with  the  case  when  both  dielectric  slugs  are quarter  wave  length.  But  in  practice,  the  entire  circuit  is  not  always  operating  at  a  fixed frequency  and  fixed  length  dielectric  slugs  may  often  be  shorter  or  longer  than  a  quarter wave. As we have mentioned previously, the frequency offset will result in a reduced radius of the maximum reflection circle. But we will show here that this reduction is minimal over a pretty wide frequency range. ## EXAMPLE

To  demonstrate  the  design  principle  proposed  in  this  chapter,  we  incorporated  the segmented transmission line into the Variable Angle Spinning probe that was designed and built  in  our  lab  for  a  500  MHz  wide  bore  magnet  [15].  This  probe  employs  a  double frequency resonator that is capacitively coupled to the main circuit by four coaxial capacitors. The  coaxial  capacitors  are  utilized  to  achieve  contactless  power  transfer  between  the stationary  tuning  circuit  and  mobile  sample  coil  so  that  a  robust  electric  junction  can  be achieved  for  variable  angle  spinning  and  switched  angle  spinning  experiments.  Originally, both 1 H  and 13 C  channels  were  impedance  matched  by  an  L-network  consisting  of  a transmission  line  with  a  single  movable  dielectric  slug  and  a  shunt  variable  capacitor  as shown in Figure 4a. This L-network, based on a tuning structure found in a Bruker Biospin probe,  can  be  analyzed  following  a  similar  approach  to  that  outlined  in  the  'Principle' section. The L-network for the 1 H channel was later replaced with an adjustable segmented transmission line using dual quarter-wave slugs as shown in Figure 4b. The impedance of the probe was measured for a set of four frequencies both with and without a sample, and Eqs.

(13) and (14) were used to predict the positions of the slugs required to match this impedance to 50 Ω .

<!-- image -->

The outer shield

Figure 4. (a) The circuit diagram of the matching network that was originally used in our Variable Angle Spinning probe. The portion enclosed in the dashed box represents the double frequency resonator that is capacitively coupled to the main circuit. In this original matching network, the proton channel utilizes a single dielectric slug and a shunt capacitor. But in diagram (b), the proton channel is matched by a two-slug segmented transmission line. A pair of dielectric slugs is inserted into a single uniform coaxial line and no parallel capacitor is required. (c) The schematic representation of the mechanical system used for position adjustment. Each dielectric slug is connected to a position controlling unit in the center. Each position controlling unit consists of a 6-32 threaded collar into which a short 4-40 threaded rod is mounted. This threaded rod connects to the dielectric slug through a 1/8'' wide slot on the outer cylinder of the coaxial transmission line. The bottom figure shows the slot positions. It is important to leave enough intact surface at both ends of the outer tube to preserve the mechanical integrity. (Use with permission by Elsevier.)

The four-segment transmission line was constructed by inserting two dielectric cylinders into  a  rigid  coaxial  line.  Both  the  inner  and  outer  tubes  of  the  coaxial  line  were  made  of OFHC copper tubing. The outer tube has an OD of 0.625 inch and an ID of 0.571 inch, while the inner rod has an OD of 0.25 inch. These particular dimensions were chosen so that the empty coaxial line would have a characteristic impedance of approximately 50 Ω , equal to the system impedance of the spectrometer. The two dielectric slugs were fabricated from Macor (Corning,  Inc.),  a  ceramic  material  chosen  for  its  machinability.  The  slugs  were  fabricated with an OD of 0.564 in. and an ID of 0.254 in., which gave them enough clearance within the copper tubes to move freely up and down. To allow quantitative comparison with Eqs. (13) and (14), the slugs should be exactly one quarter wave length at the operating frequency of 500 MHz. For Macor, the relative dielectric constant ε r ≈ 5.7  [18].  However,  the  gaps  that allow  free  motion  of  the  slugs  reduce  the  effective  dielectric  constant  of  the  relevant segments. We can take into account the effect of the two gaps on the capacitance per unit length of the cable by treating the gaps and the dielectric cylinder as three coaxial capacitors in series. Using the standard expression for the capacitance of the coaxial cylinders [19], the resulting effective dielectric constant ε eff can be defined using the expresssion:

<!-- formula-not-decoded -->

where Rout is the radius of the shield, Rin the radius of the inner tube, Rb and Ra are the outer and inner radii of the dielectric slug, respectively. For the dimensions above, ε eff = 4.9. The length of the quarter wave slug must then be 6.8 cm, about 8% longer than would have been obtained  without  the  gaps.  The  slugs  were  cut  to  this  length  and  inserted  into  the  copper tubes. As shown in Figure 4c, the position of each dielectric slug is adjusted independently by a  brass  6-32 threaded rod placed parallel to the line (Figure 3c). The threaded rod drives a position controlling unit made of G-10 fiberglass laminate which is connected to the dielectric slug. The entire coaxial tube is 0.6 m in length. Because the dielectric slug is much larger than the  G-10  screw,  and  because  the  electromagnetic  field  inside  the  coaxial  line  is  only minimally affected by the 1/8'' slots on its outer cylinder, the mechanical system is expected to have negligible influence on the impedance properties of transmission line.

To  verify  its  operation,  and  to  test  the  validity  of  Eqs.  (13)  and  (14),  the  adjustable segmented transmission line was used to tune and match the probe at a set of four frequencies both with and without a sample. The sample used for these tests was a 4 mm zirconia rotor filled  with  20 μ L  of  2%  H2O/D2O.  To  accurately  obtain  the  load  impedance  needed  to calculate the required slug positions, the two dielectric slugs were removed from the coaxial line and a vector network analyzer was used to directly read out the unmatched impedance at the probe's input. The load impedance was calculated based on the known transmission line length using Eq. (1). Next, the dielectric slugs were inserted and their positions empirically adjusted to minimize the voltage reflection at the probe's input. Table 1 gives the measured lengths of transmission line segments required to match the probe under the above conditions and  the  predictions  based  on  Eq.  (13)  and  Eq.  (14),  as  well  as  the  load  measurements expressed both as impedance and reflection coefficient. Good agreement was found, tending to  confirm  the  validity  of  our  theoretical  model.  We  should  also  note  that  the  segmented transmission line was able to match the probe both with and without the aqueous sample.

We found that moving the two dielectric slugs together is similar to adjusting the tuning capacitor in a conventional network, and moving the bottom slug (T4) with the top one fixed is similar to adjusting the matching capacitor. As is the case in a conventional network, the 'tuning' and 'matching' are not completely independent, and several iterations are required to minimize the reflected voltage.

We  also  compared  the  power  efficiency  of  the  two  different  matching  circuits.  The probe's input power was determined from the peak-to-peak voltage (Vp-p) displayed on a 1 GHz  digital  phosphor  oscilloscope  that  was  connected  to  the  input  cable  via  a  50  dB directional coupler. The power value was then calculated from the following equation:

<!-- formula-not-decoded -->

where 'Vp-p' is in units of volts and 'Power' is in units of watts. When the proton channel was matched by the L-network shown in Figure 3a, the 90 û time was measured to be 4.65 μ s with 22.9 watts of input power. In comparison, when the proton channel was matched by the segmented transmission line shown in Figure 3b, the 90 û time was 4.3 μ s with the same input power level.  This  corresponds  to  a  8  %  increase  of  B1  field  or  a  17  %  increase  of  power efficiency.  We  believe  the  improved  efficiency  comes  from  the  fact  that  the  four-segment transmission  line  does  not  have  loss  from  the  branching  capacitor  or  soldering  junction present in the L-network.

To  further  explore  the  question  of  power  efficiency,  we  performed  a  numerical simulation  for  the  500  MHz  prototype  segmented  transmission  line  using  the  method proposed in the 'Design Principle' section. A conductivity of 5.998 × 10 7 S/m was assumed for the inner and outer copper tubes, and a value of 4.7 × 10 -3 for the loss tangent of the Macor dielectric  slugs  [14].  For  each possible  load  reflection coefficient,  the  lengths  of T1  and  T 3 were numerically optimized to minimize the reflected power at the input port. As predicted from the analytical derivation, Figure 5a indicates that T1 is always shorter than half a wavelength.
