## CONCLUSION

The adjustable segmented transmission line utilizes the multiple reflections between each segment to achieve overall impedance matching at the input port. Because there are no sliding contacts  on  the  transmission  line,  this  kind  of  tuning  network  should  have  excellent durability. By empirically adjusting the position of its two quarter wave slugs both inductive and  capacitive  loads  can  be  matched.  Because  highly  reflective  loads  such  as  copper solenoids  cannot  be  completely  matched  with  the  network,  it  works  best  when  employed along  with  a  network  of  fixed  series  and  parallel  capacitors  to  reduce  the  reflection coefficient.  Such  a  configuration  is  embodied  in  a  prototype  500  MHz  Variable  Angle Spinning  Nuclear  Magnetic  Resonance  probe  for  which  the  adjustable  transmission  line  is used to tune and match the 1 H channel.

## TO THE THEORY OF MAGNETICALLY INSULATED TRANSMISSION LINES

Svyatoslav Ya. Belomyttsev, V. Alexander Kirikov and V. Victor Ryzhov *

Institute of High Current Electronics, Siberian Branch, Russian Academy of Sciences, Tomsk, Russia

## ABSTRACT

In  this  paper,  the  model  of  the  wave  propagating  in  the  magnetically  insulated transmission line (MITL) is proposed allowing to determine the potential of the anode boundary of the electron sheath near the cathode and thus the total current in the wave. The model is based on energy and axial momentum conservation, as well as on 'average electron' approximation, which means that all the electrons leaking to the anode at the front of the wave have the same energy and incidance angle.

Conversion  of  the  sheath  current  to  the  cathode  current  (retrapping)  at  the  wave reflection from the load is considered. Noticeable retrapping is predicted even in a nearmatched  mode,  i.e.,  when  the  variation  of  voltage  and  current  after  the  reflection  is negligible.

## INTRODUCTION

In the last few decades, the high-current electronics is facing the problem with respect to determination of the parameters of the wave propagating along the MITL (Figure 1). In such a wave, the current-carrying electron sheath appears around the cathode. The potential at the anode, U ,  and the potential at the anode boundary of the cathode sheath, Um ,  determine the main characteristics of the MITL wave: the total current, the cathode current, and the leak current to the anode at the front of the wave.

* Corresponding author: E-mail: ryzhov@to.hcei.tsc.ru

In  most of the papers devoted to this problem (see, for example, [1-2]), the relativistic potential at the anode boundary of the cathode sheath, 2 1 / m m eU mc γ = + ( e and m are the charge and the mass of the electron, c the speed of light), is found in the assumption, that the total  current  in  the  MITL  wave, I ,  is  the  minimum  current I min ( U ),  providing  magnetical insulation of the transmission line, i.e. I = Imin(U) . Below this assumption is referenced as the I min -model. In this paper, the potential γ m is found by taken into account the characteristics of the electrons striking the anode at the front of the wave propagating along the MITL. These characteristics, together with energy and axial momentum conservation in the system, allow to derive equations for the γ m determination. If the electrons strike the anode at zero angle, then  the  corresponding  current  is  considered  to  be  the  maximum  possible  MITL  current Imax ( U ).  Further  increase  of  current  would  prevent  the  electrons  from  leaking  to  the  anode, which is impossible for the wave propagating along the MITL [4, 5].

We also study the conversion of a part of the sheath current into the cathode current (retrapping) when the MITL wave reflects from the load, and demonstrate that if the current in the forward going MITL wave is not the minimum current I min ( U ), a considerable retrapping occures even in the near-matched mode, i.e when the variation of voltage and current after the reflection is negligible.

## PARAMETERS OF THE FORWARD GOING MITL WAVE

Let  us  consider  the  wave  propagating  along  the  coaxial  MITL  with  center  cathode electrode (Figure 1). This wave consists of the low-voltage precursor, the front of the wave where the electrons are leaking to the anode, and the wave body including the electron sheath around the cathode. We assume that the parameters of the wave are stable, the electron sheath behind the wave front is homogeneous, and the solution obtained for the total current in the wave body in the hydrodynamic approximation [3] is valid. The problem of this solution is that it is not definite, i.e., the total current depends not only on the vacuum impedance of the line ZV and  the  potential  of  the  anode 2 1 / a eU mc γ = + ,  but  also  on  the  potential  of  the anode boundary of the electron sheath, γ m ,  which  may  vary  from  zero  to γ a .  This  problem appears because the solution in [3] is obtained without of considering the parameters of the precursor and the electrons leaking to the anode on the front of the wave.

Below we derive the equations providing definite solution of the MITL problem, as it was discussed above. These equations are based on energy and axial momentum conservation in  the  MITL,  as  well  as  on  'average  electron'  approximation  [4,  5],  which  claims  that  all electrons  striking  the  anode  on  the  front  of  the  wave  have  the  same  energy  and  insidence angle ϕ (see Figure 1).

## c) Results

Figure 2 shows γ m as a function of γ a for the different values of k , which is the ratio of the voltages in the precursor and in the MITL wave. For comparison, this figure shows also the function γ m min ( γ a ), which is the potential of the anode boundary of the electron sheath in I min -approximation, i.e. when the total current in the MITL wave is assumed to be the minimum current I min .  One  can  see  that  our  solution  differs  from  the min m γ ( γ a ),  and  the  difference increases  with  the  energy  in  the  precursor.  As  suggests  Figure  3,  compared  to  the I min -approximation the increase of k results in appreciable increase of the current I b flowing in the electron sheath and in the decrease of the cathode current I c .

At the same time, the impedance Z of the MITL is affected only a little. It can be seen in Figure 4, that the impedance reduces for few tens of percent at low voltages (10% at U = 0.5 MV), and for few percents at high voltages (5% at U = 15 MV). Thus, the total MITL current in the two approximations is almost the same (see Figure 3).

Figure  5  shows  the  MITL  wave  front  velocity  as  a  function  of γ m in  the  two approximations  under  consideration  ( / f f V c β = and 1/ 2 1/ 2 max ( 1) /( 1) f a a β γ γ = -+ ).

The greatest difference (up to 20%) between the MITL wave front velocities are observed in the low-voltages range in which the velocities predicted in our approximation is lower. The same figure shows experimental data on β f at U = 0.46 [6] and U = 3.4 MV [7] taken from [8],  and  results  of  numerical  calculations  by  the  PIC  code  KARAT.  The  calculated  curves agree well with the measurement data on the velocity at U = 3.4 MV in the close range of which the curves γ m lim(U) and γ m min(U) intersect (see Figure 2). However, the experimental results for the velocity at U = 0.46 MV agree more closely with the calculations made in this work.

According to our calculations, the total MITL current and the MITL wave front velocity are  scarcely  affected  by  the  energy  lost  in  the  precursor  wave.  Taking  into  account  the precursor wave gives an additional increase in γ m , and hence a decrease in cathode current.

Comparison of the results obtained with no precursor and the data on the MITL wave in the minimum current approximation shows that the two approaches provide close values of the total current (see Figure 3). However, the relativistic factor γ m and the cathode current in the electron layer in the two approximations differ greatly; in this connection, the proposed approximation requires experimental tests.

## d) Conversion of the Current on MITL Wave Reflection from a Load

For the load impedance lower than the MITL impedance (Figure 6), the electromagnetic wave reflecting from the load converts a considerable portion of the electron current to the cathode current (retrapping), and this increases the efficiency of the generation of the electron beam and bremsstrahlung in the diode [10].

The  theory  of  reflection  of  a  self-magnetic  insulation  wave  proposed  in  [11]  uses  the assumption of the minimum current min I in the incident wave in a MITL for which magnetic insulation  is  still  possible.  The  MITL  characteristics  after  reflection  of  an  electromagnetic wave from a load in the cold MITL approximation were calculated in [10]. The necessity of taking  into  account  the  electron  flow  whose  parameters  vary  with  the  load  resistance  in  a MITL is the key problem in the development of retrapping theory, since the parameters of the electron layer affect all SMIW characteristics and are to be taken into account in analyzing the conversion of the current on reflection of an electromagnetic wave from a load.

Let the load impedance be z, and the voltage in the incident wave, the current, and the relativistic factor at the outer boundary of the electron layer be U1 , I 1 , and γ m1 , respectively. After the reflection of the wave, the above quantities in the MITL take on the values U2, I2, γ m2 . (Figure 6).

It  is  assumed  that  these  quantities  are  constant  along  the  line.  We  neglect  the  radial electron motion in the layer and take the above solution with no precursor for m γ in the wave incident on the load [4, 5]. The linear electric charge density in the electron layer is

For  the  MITL-matched  load,  the  voltage,  current,  and  state  of  the  electron  layer  are retained (no reflection takes place). For the load impedance higher than the MITL impedance in the incident wave, the beam precipitates on the anode tube in the immediate vicinity of the load such that the net impedance becomes matched and no reflection occurs. Thus, a decrease in current on reflection in a hot MITL is impossible. Note that equations (45-49) do provide for  a  solution  with  a  decrease  in  current  after  reflection,  but  we  neglect  it  for  the  above reason. For the load impedance lower than the impedance in the incident wave, reflection of the wave takes place.

Let the incident MITL wave corresponds to the right branch of the ( ) m I γ curve (point max γ in Figure 7.). On reflection of the wave from the load, the total MITL current increases and the voltage decreases. In this case, the electron layer is bound to be pressed against the cathode, i.e., the linear electric charge density and the relativistic factor decrease as it ρ m γ

follows from (48) and (45). However, this is impossible on the right branch of the magnetic insulation curve because the increase in total current for the branch correspond to that in γ m . Therefore, the system is bound to turn into the state corresponding to the left branch for which this condition holds true (points 2 m γ in Figure 7). The assumption of the necessity of this transition is made in [1] . ( ) m I γ k B

Surely,  after  reflection  the  curve corresponds  to γ a ,  and  hence  the  transition  is bound to be to the corresponding point on this curve. For reflection with a minimum increase in current and decrease in voltage, the curve can be considered invariant. ( ) m I γ

The current and voltage obtained from (45)-(49) agree very closely with those found in the cold line approximation [10]; this approximation assumes that the wave is reflected to a cold line. In particular, from the model it follows that the reflected wave velocity does not depend on the mismatch between the MITL and the load and is equal to the velocity of light, which  is  inconsistent  with  our  results  (Figure  8).  It  can  be  seen  from  Figure  8  that  the numerical calculations of the reflected wave velocity by the PIC code LSP [10] agree with our model.

At  the  same  time,  the  calculations  performed  in  [12]  show  that  the  difference  in  the impedances of the cold and hot lines is no greater than 10% throughout their range, and this can explain the applicability of the cold line model for calculations of the current and voltage after reflection.

The characteristics  of  the  incident  wave  were  calculated  by  the  PIC  code  KARAT  [9] (Figure 9). It can be seen from the figure that the simulation results agree more closely with the  data  obtained  in  the  maximum  current  approximation. The  calculations show that for high voltages in the incident wave at which the system is on the right branch of the  magnetic  insulation  curve, ,  and  in  the  reflected  wave, and  the system passes to the left branch of the curve. The transition involves considerable variation in the potential at the boundary of the electron layer and in the ratio of the MITL currents. The  gain  factor  of  the  cathode  current and  the  power  efficiency  of  the diode in the reflection mode are determined by the mismatch between the MITL and the load and by the incident wave voltage (Figure 10). In this case, the dependence of the useful power on the mismatch between the MITL and the load has a maximum at kz ≈ 0.6-0.7  (Figure  11).  However,  these  modes,  though  optimum  in  power  terms,  are  not necessarily  useful  for  radiographic  diodes,  since  they  involve  an  abrupt  decrease  in  diode voltage  and  hence  a  decrease  in  bremsstrahlung  photon  energy. Thus,  the  developed  retrapping  theory  and  the  numerical  simulation  prove  that  in  a vacuum  transmission  line,  the  self-magnetic  insulation  wave  reflected  from  the  load  is necessarily described by the left branch of the curve . Therefore for the reflected wave of voltage higher than at which the state of the incident wave is described the right  branch  of  the  curve ,  the  relativistic  factor decreases  steeply  even  in  nearmatched modes due to the transition of the MITL state from the right to left branch of the self-magnetic insulation curve [14]. This determines the conversion of a considerable portion of the electron layer current to the cathode current (retrapping). ( ) m I γ 3 a U MV ≈ ( ) m I γ m γ

Of practical importance for radiography is the operation of the MITL into the diode with a minimum MITL and load mismatch in which the MITL voltage is constant and the cathode current increases in response to a change in the state of the electron layer.

## CONCLUSION

The numerical calculations invalidate the assumption of the homogeneity and laminarity of the electron layer. The electron layer in the incident MITL wave broadens with an increase in z and the flow is non-laminar. The inhomogeneity of the electron layer is likely to be due to the presence of the emission boundary and MITL wave front. The average angle of electron incidence  on  the  anode  is  nonzero  and  decreases  with  an  increase  in  voltage  (Figure  12). Therefore,  the  results  reported  in  the  paper  are  approximate.  However,  the  values  of obtained in numerical calculation are closer to those found in the model under consideration rather than to the values in the minimum current approximation (Figure 9). The qualitative conclusions about the MITL wave current on the right branch at U &gt; 3MV and on the left branch at U &lt; 3MV, and associated conversion with a near-matched load are supported by numerical calculations. m γ

Chapter 9

## PERIODIC REGIMES FOR DISTORTIONLESS LOSSY TRANSMISSION LINES TERMINATED BY PARALLEL CONNECTED RCL-LOADS

G. Vasil Angelov *

University of Mining and Geology 'St. I. Rilski', Department of Mathematics, Sofia, Bulgaria

## ABSTRACT

The  processes  in  a  distortionless  lossy  transmission  line  terminated  by  parallel connected RCL -loads  can  be  formulated  as  a  mixed  problem  for  first  order  hyperbolic system with constant coefficients. It  can be  reduced  to  a  neutral  functional  differential system with polynomial nonlinearities. The main purpose of the present paper is to obtain periodic  solutions  of  a  functional  differential  system  obtained  by  fixed  point  method instead of usually accepted numerical methods.

Keywords  and  phrases: Lossy  transmission  line,  neutral  system,  fixed  point,  periodic solutions.

## 1. INTRODUCTION

The principal importance of the investigations of transmission lines has been discussed in many papers [1]-[11]. In a previous paper [12] we have investigated an existence problem for lossless transmission lines terminated by series connected RCL -loads. Here we consider the case of parallel connected RCL -loads. Unlike [12] here we take into account the losses in the transmission  lines  that  means 0 0, ≠ ≠ G R which  generates  additional  difficulties.  Here  we

* Corresponding author: E-mail: angelov@mgu.bg

ISBN: 978-1-61761-300-5

© 2011 Nova Science Publishers, Inc.

assume that Heaviside condition is fulfilled, that is, R/L = G/C .  This  means that the line is without distortion.

Our analysis is based on the reduction of the mixed problem for the first order hyperbolic partial  differential  systems  describing  the  distortionless  lossy  transmission  lines  to  an equivalent initial value problem for a neutral functional differential system on the boundary (cf.  [4]).  The  equivalence  of  both  problems  is  treated  also  in  [12].  Here  we  formulate  and solve the problem for the existence of periodic solution of the neutral functional differential system. The main difficulties are the following: 1) the availability of polynomial nonlinearities arising most often in the applications (cf. [5]-[10]) and 2) unlike [13] the righthand  side  of  the  neutral  system  contains  an  integral  of  the  unknown  function.  The  first difficulty  is  by  using  fixed  point  method  overcome,  while  the  second  one  by  choosing  a suitable function space and proceeding as in [14]. Here we consider the case when R and L are nonlinear loads while C is a linear one.

Editor: Dana M. Welton

Chapter 10

## SPATIAL AUCTION MARKETS WITH UNIQUE CONSUMER PRICE ∗

E. Allevi a , A. Gnudi b , I.V. Konnov c , M.T. Vespucci d a Department of Quantitative Methods, Brescia University, Contrada S. Chiara, 50, Brescia 25122, Italy

b Department of Mathematics, Statistics, Computer Science and Applications,

Bergamo University, Via dei Caniana 2, Bergamo 24127, Italy c Department of System Analysis and Information Technologies,

Kazan University, ul. Kremlevskaya, 18, Kazan 420008, Russia d Department of Information Technology and Mathematical Methods, Bergamo University, Via Marconi 5, Dalmine 24044, Italy

## Abstract

We consider a collection of auctions representing zonal electricity markets, which are joined by transmission lines in a spatial system. At each market, generating companies (traders) and customers (buyers) submit their fixed offer/bid prices together with maximal offer/bid volumes, respectively. In addition to the usual balance and capacity constraints, we consider also the additional requirement of utilizing the minimal unique purchase price for all the zones. As a result, we obtain spatial equilibrium type problems with special parameter for finding zonal prices and offer/bid volumes. We show that the streamlined formulation can be inconsistent under rather natural assumptions and propose a relaxed formulation. This problem admits suitable solution methods. We propose a parametric method combined with a bisection type procedure to solve this problem.

Keywords: Spatial auction markets; fixed prices; unique purchase price; parametric method.

∗ In this work, the first three authors were supported by Brescia University Grant PRIN 2006: 'Oligopolistic models and order monotonicity properties'.

ISBN: 978-1-61761- 300-5

c © 2011 Nova Science Publishers, Inc.

## 1. Introduction

Spatial equilibrium models play a significant role in mathematical physics, economics, transportation and other sciences. For instance, in the classical work of Cournot [1], a competitive price was determined by the intersection of supply and demand curves in the context of two spatially separated markets and the cost of transporting the good between markets was considered. In what follows, spatial equilibrium problems appeared to be very suitable for modeling various complex competitive economic systems; see e.g. [2, 3] and references therein. Recently, the process of restructuring large energy systems discovered many new challenges and new kinds of spatial models devised to overcome difficulties related to deregulating and restructuring energy sectors have received considerable attention; see e.g. [4, 5] and references therein. Since most parts of the energy sector are attributed to natural monopolies, perfect competition models seem unrealistic here. As a result, NashCournot type non-cooperative games appear suitable for the situation where any explicit regulation can be neglected. At the same time, in the case where a state keeps certain tools for influence in the energy sector, the auction market mechanisms are mostly utilized; see e.g. [6, 7] and references therein. Nevertheless, most models of restructuring large energy systems with taking into account rather complex behavior of participants and the presence of joint network capacity and balance constraints are represented as rather complicated mathematical problems, such as multi-level games or global optimization problems with equilibrium constraints (MPEC).

Recently, the variational inequality approach for modelling separate auction markets of a homogeneous commodity was proposed in [8]- [10]. In [11]- [14], this approach was extended to problems of managing systems of spatially separated auction markets with joint capacity and balance constraints under different assumptions on behavior of participants.

However, peculiarities of such distributed energy systems in different countries and regions may lead to additional constraints. For instance, in Italy, the additional requirement of guaranteeing the minimal unique purchase price for all the zones was imposed; see e.g. [7, 15]. The corresponding models were formulated as global optimization or mixed integer optimization problems which created certain difficulties in case of highdimensionality. In particular, the problem of organizing and managing transactions in the day-ahead electricity market was stated in [15], where Procedure UPPO (Uniform Purchase Price Optimization), utilized by GME (Gestore dei Mercati Energetici S.p.A.), is described. It is based on exploration of the total demand curve in its vertical and horizontal parts and is heuristic essentially.

In this paper, being based on the results from [11]- [14], we consider the above problem in a more general and formal setting. The problem under solution is to manage a system of spatially separated auction markets defined in a general network with joint capacity and balance constraints and also with the additional requirement of guaranteeing the minimal unique purchase price for all the zones. Therefore it is treated as a spatial equilibrium type problem with special parameter. We show that the streamlined formulation can be inconsistent under rather natural assumptions and propose a new approach which is based on a certain relaxed formulation. This problem admits suitable solution methods. We propose a parametric method combined with a bisection type procedure to solve this problem.

## 2. Statement of the Problem

Let us first consider a system of n auction markets of a homogeneous commodity, which are joined by links (transmission lines) in a network. We denote by I k and J k , respectively, the index sets of traders and buyers of the k -th local market associated with the k -th node. We suppose that each i -th trader chooses his fixed price g i and offer value x i ∈ [0 , α i ] with α i ≥ 0 for i ∈ I k , and each j -th buyer chooses his fixed price h j and bid value y j ∈ [0 , β j ] with β j ≥ 0 for j ∈ J k . Next, we denote by A the set of all arcs in the oriented graph. Let f a denote the commodity flow on arc a = ( k, l ) , f a ∈ [ γ ′ a , γ ′′ a ] where γ ′ a and γ ′′ a are lower and upper bounds on commodity flow on arc a respectively, and c a the cost of shipment of one unit of the commodity along this arc. Given a node k , we denote by A + k and A -k the sets of incoming and outgoing arcs at k . We first describe the set of equilibrium conditions for this problem; see [11,14]. For the k -th auction, we define the sets of offer/bid bounds:

Let p k and u k denote the auction clearing price and excess supply of the k -th market. Then the solutions x ( k ) = ( x i ) i ∈ I k and y ( k ) = ( y j ) j ∈ J k must satisfy the auction market conditions and

That is, each solution ( x ∗ , y ∗ , f ∗ , p ∗ , u ∗ ) of problem (1)-(6) yields a solution ( x ∗ , y ∗ , f ∗ ) of problem (7)-(9). Conversely, if ( x ∗ , y ∗ , f ∗ ) is a solution of problem (7)(9), then there exist p ∗ , u ∗ such that ( x ∗ , y ∗ , f ∗ , p ∗ , u ∗ ) solves (1)-(7) , moreover, each p ∗ is the Lagrange multiplier corresponding to the k -th balance constraint in (8), and each u ∗ can be found from (3) or (5). Observe that the negative value in (7) can be treated as the social surplus of the system. In fact, if all the transmission lines are costless, i.e. c a ≡ 0 , it gives the difference between the bought and sold amounts; see also [7].

In [11,14], being based on the above equivalence result, several efficient solution methods were developed for problem (7)-(9), i.e., for problem (1)-(6) as well.

Now we consider the problem of managing a system of spatially separated auction markets under the additional requirement of guaranteeing the minimal unique purchase price for all the zones. Hence, we should modify the above problem in conformity with this additional requirement. First of all we add the following condition:

## 4. Solution of the Reduced Auction Market Problem

We first describe algorithms for solving the reduced problem (12)-(14). Of course, since (12)-(14) is a linear programming problem, a great number of finite algorithms can be

applied to find its solution; see e.g. [16, 17]. We give here certain adjustments of the corresponding ones from [11, 14] suggested for problems of form (7)-(9) or (1)-(6). They take into account the essential features of the problem, such as separability of constraints.

For instance, the well-known projection method consists in generating the iteration sequence { ( x s , y s , f s ) } in conformity with the formula: Find ( x s +1 , y s +1 , f s +1 ) ∈ W ( π ) such that where

<!-- formula-not-decoded -->

<!-- formula-not-decoded -->

<!-- formula-not-decoded -->

θ s &gt; 0 is the stepsize parameter. Clearly, (16) is always a convex quadratic programming problem, which has a unique solution if the set W ( π ) is nonempty, i.e. under very mild assumptions. Moreover, its solution can be found by finite algorithms, for instance, by the dual Uzawa type method. In fact, we can solve (16) via the dual problem

<!-- formula-not-decoded -->

<!-- formula-not-decoded -->

<!-- formula-not-decoded -->

where

Clearly, the computation of the values of ϕ and its gradient can be made componentwise, i.e. (18) is decomposed into a set of separable one-dimensional problems each of them has the explicit solution formula. That is,

<!-- formula-not-decoded -->

In order to solve (17) we can apply a suitable conjugate gradient method. On the other hand, method (16) coincides with the proximal point method and possesses the finite termination property; see [18]. Besides, some other decomposition type methods seem also suitable for the reduced problem (12)-(14). For instance, the variant of the alternating direction method described in [14, Section 6] is even simpler for implementation, however increases the dimensionality after reformulation of the initial problem. Anyway, these methods were implemented for solving several spatial auction problems arising in electricity market systems and showed rather fast convergence to a solution; see [11,14].

## 5. Solution Finding for the Relaxed Formulation

Being based on the above properties, we can propose the following algorithm for solving problem (15).

Algorithm. Step 0 . Rearrange all the prices of buyers from J k , k = 1 , . . . , n in the common finite sequence of indices ˜ J = { j 1 , j 2 , . . . , j k , . . . } such that h j k ≤ h j k +1 for all k = 1 , 2 , . . . Set h j 0 = 0 and k = 1 .

Step 1 . Solve problem (12)-(14) with π = h j k and determine the function Ψ( π ) on ( h j k -1 , h j k ] .

Step 2 . If ψ &lt; 0 for ψ ∈ Ψ( h j k ) or the cutting price π is not correct, set k = k +1 and go to Step 1. Otherwise choose π ∗ as the minimal π ∈ ( h j k -1 , h j k ] such that ψ ≥ 0 for ψ ∈ Ψ( π ) and stop.

We note that a solution of the auxiliary problem (12)-(14) can be found by help of the iterative algorithm of the previous section. We illustrate the work of this algorithm by the following examples.

It has been noted that Procedure UPPO for determining a solution of a spatial electricity market with minimal unique consumer price and potentially different zonal prices was proposed in [15]. Its description is not complete and its work is only illustrated by

✬✩

✫✪

<!-- image -->

✫✪

Figure 3. Example 5.2

several examples. For this reason, we should indicate that our algorithm was developed independently from that in [15].

It is possible to understand from the description that Procedure UPPO investigates of the behavior of the total demand curve along its horizontal and vertical pieces while varying the uniform purchase price P ∗ from the zero value (at which all purchase bids are accepted) to a maximum value given by the highest zonal price of the equilibrium solution without uniform purchase price. For each value of P ∗ a preliminary solution is determined which satisfies at the minimal cost (determined on the basis of sell bids) the demand determined by P ∗ . Then those preliminary solutions will be selected that satisfy the monetary balance condition. Finally, among the preliminary solutions with monetary balance, the solution will be detected that maximizes the net transaction value. It also involves certain heuristic rules.

Further extensions and more complete formulations of this model were proposed in [7]. Nevertheless, those models were formulated as parametric mixed integer optimization problems which created certain difficulties in developing efficient solution methods, especially in the case of high-dimensionality.

We observe that our formulation is placed in a more general setting and admits rather simple solution methods described.

✬✩

✫✪

## 6. Conclusions

We considered the problem of managing a system of spatially separated auction markets with joint capacity and balance constraints and also with the additional requirement of providing the minimal unique purchase price for all the zones. We showed that the streamlined formulation can be inconsistent under rather natural assumptions. We suggested a relaxed formulation as a spatial equilibrium type problem with special parameter. A new iterative algorithm which combines solution an auxiliary linear programming problem at each iteration and a bisection type procedure for finding the optimal value of the unique purchase price was proposed. This problem admits suitable solution methods. We illustrated its work by examples which showed rather satisfactory results.
