Chapter 11

## TESTING OF CMOS DRIVEN VLSI INTERCONNECTS

## Devendra Kumar Sharma 1 , B.K.Kaushik 2 and R.K.Sharma 3

1 Department of Electronics and Communication Engineering, Meerut Institute of Engineering and Technology, Meerut, UP, India 2 Department of Electronics and Computer Engineering, Indian Institute of Technology, Roorkee, Uttarakhand, India 3 Department of Electronics and Communication Engineering, National Institute of Technology, Kurukshetra, Haryana, India

## ABSTRACT

Wiring-up  of  on-chip  devices  takes  place  through  various  conductors  produced during  fabrication  process.  In  past,  on-chip  interconnect  wires  were  not  considered important in circuit analysis except in high precision analysis. The shrinking feature size of  MOSFET  devices  is  largely  responsible  for  growth  of  VLSI  circuits.  In  deep submicron (DSM) technology, the interconnect geometry is scaled down for high wiring density. The  complex  geometry  of  interconnects and high operational frequency introduce  wire  parasitics  and  inter-wire  parasitics.  These  parasitics  cause  delay,  power dissipation  and  crosstalk  that  may  affect  the  signal  integrity  in  VLSI  system.  Accurate analysis, sophisticated design and effective test methods are the requirement of the day to ensure the proper functionality and reliability of VLSI  circuits. The testing of interconnect  is  becoming  important  and  a  challenge  in  the  current  technology.  This chapter  provides  an  overview  of  on-chip  interconnects.  Furthermore,  the  parasitics  of interconnects and  their effects on circuit  performance  have  been  discussed.  The transmission  line  models  for  on-chip  interconnects  have  been  discussed  along  with Enhanced Transmission Line (ETL) model which is effective in modeling high frequency effects in RF ICs. Full wave analysis is also described. Recent years have seen the rapidly growing  prominence  of  new  techniques  for  testing  CMOS  based  VLSI  chip  with innovative features to allow high quality and fast test time. Besides basic techniques of testing,  BIST,  an  important  technique  has  been  described  with  its  different  approaches and economics. The defects in VLSI circuits and fault models are described. The type of test pattern generation (TPG) circuitry is the most relevant issue while dealing with BIST. This  issue  of  TPG  is  discussed  in  the  chapter.  The  most  important  requirement  is  that

ISBN: 978-1-61761- 300-5

©2011 Nova Science Publishers, Inc.

TPG  must  have  high  fault  coverage  and  low  area  overhead.  Later,  the  testing  of interconnect defects affecting the circuit performance has been reviewed. Finally, future challenges in VLSI interconnect and their testing is explored.

## 1. INTRODUCTION

The task of testing of chips for its complete functionality is complex and time consuming. However, when faulty chips are used in system integration, they can cause system failures and create much difficulty in system debugging. It is known that the debugging cost increases by about ten times at each level of system integration as per the relationship known as the rule of ten [100]. Thus, it is of great importance to detect faults as early as possible. The rule of ten says: if a chip fault is not detected by it's testing, then finding the fault at PCB level costs ten times as much as the chip level. Similarly, if a board fault is not found by PCB testing, then finding the fault at the system level costs ten times as much as the board level.

The fabrication technology of integrated circuits is still in the process of evolution which is leading to smaller feature size, line width and high packing density on chip. Scaling is an important factor for the designers however, the details of its implementation and limitations is not in the scope of this chapter. Readers interested in preliminary study of scaling can refer [41,100, 101]. The scaling down of device size in ICs i.e .  scaling of integrated circuits has resulted  in  more  complex  chips  having  millions  of  interconnections.  Also  with  increase  in switching speed and using drivers with varied driving capability on the same chip results in crosstalk  effects.  The  prime  objective  of  technology  scaling  is  to  increase  packing  density, decrease  gate  delay  and  power  dissipation.  Higher  densities  are  only  possible  if  the interconnects  are  also  scaled.  In  interconnect  scaling,  the  width  reduces  with  increase  in density.  Reduced  width  means  increased  resistance  and  denser  interconnect  means  higher coupling  capacitance.  With  huge  functionalities  being  integrated,  long  interconnects  have become a common feature. The increase in wire density and high operational frequency of the circuit on-chip causes coupled noise or crosstalk because of associated parasitics, which may lead to critical delays or logic malfunctions and hence become an important parameter to test. The  major  difficulties  in  performance  improvement  are  increased  interconnect  delays  and coupling effects. Crosstalk effect induces pulse and unpredictable delay. The pulse or glitch is induced on victim line which is static and when neighboring lines called aggressors have a transition.  The  crosstalk  pulse  produced  on  victim  line  due  to  coupling  parasitics  has  an impact  on  performance  of  the  circuit.  The  crosstalk  effect  produces  delay  in  signal transmission  when  both  victim  and  aggressor  lines  have  simultaneous  transitions.  When transitions of the lines are in opposite direction, then the worst case occurs wherein the delay is maximised. This is because of Miller effect stated as 'a capacitor experiencing identical but opposite voltage swings at both its terminals can be replaced by a capacitor to ground whose value is twice the original value'. So, due to coupling capacitor the delay of the n th wire is the function  of  transitions  on  the  neighboring  wires  n-1  and  n+1.  The  capacitively  coupled interconnects driven by unbalanced drivers causes high enough interference to affect the logic value of the digital signal [1]. So crosstalk which is due to poor design can be seen as a defect in the circuit which produces logic errors [2].

Although physical defects causes electrical faults and logical faults in ICs, the existing fault models viz. stuck open, stuck on, bridging and stuck at fault models do not ensure the

coverage for crosstalk faults. The use of Ultra Deep Submicron technology (UDSM)/nanotechnology causes continuous miniaturization of circuits on chip that leads to a large  number  of  long  interconnects  and  with  rapid  increase  in  operational  frequency,  the parasitics  are  dominated  and  signal  integrity  becomes  a  major  problem  for  chip  designers. The  problems  are  further  aggravated  by  fabrication.  Although  many  of  researchers  have analyzed and modeled crosstalk noise and delay [3-8], but less attention is paid to test these effects. Efforts has been made to test the interconnect faults in PCBs and FPGA architecture [9-16].  However,  some  work  has  been  done  to  test  VLSI  interconnect  defects  [17-25,  27]. Few researchers have adopted scan based BIST technique and some have proposed their own technique for testing of interconnect faults.

## 2. INTERCONNECTS

Interconnects are metal or polysilicon structures used to connect different cells/devices. The function of interconnects or wiring systems is to distribute clock and other signals and to provide power/ground to and among the various circuits/system functions on the chip. The performance s.a. time delay and power dissipation of a high-speed chip is highly dependent on  the  interconnects  which  connect  different  macro  cells  within  a  VLSI  chip.  To  escape prohibitively large delays, designers scale down global wire dimensions more sluggishly than the transistor dimensions. As technology advances, interconnects have turned out to be more and more important than the transistor resource, and it is essential to use global interconnects optimally.  For  high-density  high-speed  chips  of  submicron-geometry,  it  is  mostly  the interconnection rather than the device performance that determines the chip performance.

Wide wires are frequently encountered in clock distribution networks, power and ground lines, and other global interconnects such as data bus and control lines in upper metal layers. These  wires  are  low  resistive  lines  that  can  exhibit  significant  inductive  effects  at  high frequencies. Due to presence of these inductive effects, the new generation VLSI designers have been forced to model the interconnects as distributed RLC transmission lines, rather than simple RC -ones.  Modeling  interconnects  as  distributed RLC transmission  line  has  posed many  challenges  in  terms  of  accurately  determining  the  signal  propagation  delay,  power dissipation through an interconnect, crosstalk between co-planar interconnects and interconnects  on  different  planes  due  to  capacitive  and  inductive  coupling,  and  optimal repeater insertion.

If we compare a interconnect line with the transmission line, the purpose of both is same i.e to transmit electrical signals from one point to another. One familiar example includes the connections between devices on a circuit board or PCB which is designed to operate at high frequencies.  The  concept  behind  it  is  that  the  devices  to  be  connected  through  conducting lines  or  wires  are  separated  by  distances  of  the  order  of  a  wavelength  or  much  larger. However in basic circuit analysis, the connections between elements are of negligible length. This ensures that the voltage across a resistor on one side of a circuit is exactly in phase with the voltage source on the other side, so there is no delay induced. The basic elements in the circuit  such  as  resistors,  capacitors,  inductors  and  the  connections  between  them  are considered lumped elements if the time delay to traverse the elements is negligible. But if the elements or interconnections are large enough so that the time to traverse is significant, then

these are considered as distributed elements. This means that R , L , C characteristics must be considered on per unit distance basis. Transmission lines have this property in general. If we look into the VLSI interconnects, the same principle is applied here for the characteristics of parasitics.  Since  with  the  high  speed  ICs,  the  length  of  interconnect  is  comparable  to wavelength,  so  distributive  elements R,  L,  C called  interconnect  parasitics  have  to  be considered  as  shown  in  Fig.1.  In  addition  to  this,  interwire  or  coupling  parasitics  are  also present in the circuit because of complex geometry, short spacing of interconnects between themselves, its long length and high frequency of operation. The interwire parasitics lead to mutual  inductance  ( M )  and  coupling  capacitance  ( CC ).  All  the  parasitics  lead  to  crosstalk noise, propagation delay and power dissipation which affects the signal integrity and degrade the performance of the circuit. Signal integrity is the ability of a signal to generate correct responses in a circuit.

Figure 1. Interconnect wire distributed parasitic.

<!-- image -->

Distribution of the clock and signal functions is accomplished on three types of wiring (local, intermediate, and global). An interconnect depending on its length, can be classified as local, semi-global and global [41]. Local wiring, consisting of very thin lines, connects gates and transistors within an execution unit or a functional block (such as embedded logic, cache memory, or address adder) on the chip. Local wires usually span a few gates and occupy first and sometimes second metal layers in a multi-level system. The length of a local interconnect wire approximately scales with scaling of technology, as the increased packing density of the devices makes it possible to similarly reduce the wire lengths. Intermediate wiring provides clock and signal distribution within a functional block with typical lengths up to 3-4  mm. Intermediate  wires  are  wider  and  taller  than  local  wires  to  provide  lower  resistance signal/clock paths. Global  wiring  provides  clock  and  signal  distribution  between  the functional blocks, and it delivers power/ground to all functions on a chip. Global wires, which occupy the top one or two layers, are longer than 4mm and can be as long as half of the chip perimeter. The length of global interconnect wires grow proportionally to the die size. The length of semi-global interconnect behaves intermediately. The global interconnects are much wider  than  local  and  semi-global  interconnects.  Thus  resistance  of  global  interconnects  is small and therefore their behavior resembles that of lossless transmission lines.

At lower frequencies, interconnects cause no difficulty for signal propagation. However, at  higher  frequencies,  they  cause  severe  signal  degradation  such  as  signal  delay,  crosstalk, ringing, reflections and distortions which must be addressed by VLSI designers. It has been

predicted since long time that interconnect wiring delays rather than transistor logic delays would be the major contributor to the overall global path delays for ICs fabricated by deep submicron CMOS processes. The increasing dominance of the interconnect coupled with the aggressive scaling at operating frequencies to increase chip performance has fundamentally changed  the  nature  of  IC  design.  The  impact  of  the  interconnect,  therefore,  needs  to  be considered  during  all  stages  of  design  and  at  all  levels  of  design  hierarchy.  Even  during process development, interconnect performance is an important consideration in the design of the on-chip metal system. With the upgrade of technology from micron to nanometer regime, technological, device and interconnect challenges are closely examined by different researchers.

Propagation delay, crosstalk and power dissipation in global interconnects have become a core research problem. Therefore, a lot of work is being carried out to address these problems. Various models have been suggested in literature to analyze interconnects.

## 2.1. Interconnect Parasitics

Capacitive parasitic of a wire is a function of shape, distance to nearly wires and distance to  the  substrate.  Fig.2  shows  different  parasitic  capacitances  [28,  127].  For  denser  wiring, width 'W' is  small, W/H ratio  becomes  even  below  unity  in  the  advanced  process.  In  this situation,  the  parallel  plate  capacitance  ( Cp )  model  is  no  more  accurate  and  the  effect  of fringe  capacitance Cf (the  capacitance  between  side  wall  and  substrate)  must  also  be  taken into account while estimating the parasitic capacitive effect.

Figure 2. Capacitive Parasitic between two Parallel Lines on the same level.

<!-- image -->

Inter wire capacitances starts dominating in multi layer interconnect structures. The effect is more pronounced in higher interconnect layers as these interconnects are further away from the substrate.  However,  in multilayer structure, wires on adjacent layers are routed orthogonally to minimize the crosstalk. Inter wire capacitance causes crosstalk. Crosstalk is an  unwanted  coupling  from  neighbouring  signal  wire  to  a  network  node  that  introduces interference.  This  disturbance  is  the  source  of  noise.  The  value  of  noise  depends  on  the transient  value  of  the  neighbouring  signal.  Noise  in  the  digital  circuits  is  the  unwanted

variations of voltage and current at logic nodes. Mostly the noise in digital circuit is internally generated  and  its  value  is  proportional  to  the  signal  swing.  Crosstalk  adversely  affects  the circuit  operating  at  high  frequencies  in  GHz  range.  The  crosstalk  induced  faults  are  glitch fault and delay fault. A crosstalk induced glitch occurs when the victim line is intended to be at stable state and a noise pulse on the net occurs. A crosstalk induced delay occurs when both the affecting and victim lines have simultaneous transitions. If both lines make transitions in the same direction, the effective delay is reduced, resulting in a phenomenon called crosstalk speedup. If the affecting line and victim line make transitions in the opposite direction then delay  will  be  increased,  so  called  crosstalk  slow  down.  In  most  of  the  circuits,  crosstalk induced delay mainly slowdown delay causes chip failure more than crosstalk induced glitch. The crosstalk faults can be minimized by resizing drivers, shielding interconnect, rerouting signals and repeater insertion techniques. However, these redesign techniques are expensive in terms of time and design efforts. Encoding of data before transmitting leads to crosstalk minimization under worst case patterns. This minimizes the delay and can also help to reduce power dissipation by minimizing the number of transitions.

In  DSM  technology,  use  of  low  resistance  interconnect  material  and  with  increasing clock  speed,  increasing  interconnect  lengths  and  decreasing  rise  time,  the  inductance significantly plays a major role in on-chip circuit performance. Apart from other consequences of on-chip inductive parasitic, inductive coupling between interconnects causes crosstalk noise as in case of capacitive coupling and affects the signal integrity as shown in Fig.3.  The  presence  of  significant  coupling  inductance  can  result  in  damped  voltage oscillations superimposed on top of a glitch or delay.

Figure 3. Inductive Coupling.

<!-- image -->

As interconnects are conducting wires, it exhibits some resistance. The current flowing through a resistance causes voltage drop and degrades the signal levels. Also wire delay is proportional  to  the  resistance  of  the  wire  ( RC delay).  For  long  interconnect,  the  delay  is dominated by RC effect. So far, the resistance of interconnect is considered to be linear and constant but at very high frequencies well in to the GHz region, an additional effect called skin effect plays a significant role. The resistance becomes frequency dependent and the high frequency  current  starts  flowing  on  the  surface  of  interconnect  wire  with  current  density falling  off  exponentially  with  the  depth  into  the  wire  resulting  decrease  in  effective  cross

sectional  area  and  so  increase  in  resistance.  This  increased  resistance  causes  an  extra attenuation  which  in  turn  results  in  distortion  of  the  signal  travelling  over  the  interconnect wire. Skin effect results in an increase of propagation delay through the interconnects. The depth into the conductor at which the current falls off to a value of ( e -1 ) of its nominal value is called skin depth. However, skin effect is a problem related to wider interconnects.

## 2.2. Interconnect Models

Electrical  models  are  required  to  analyze  the  effects  of  interconnect  parasitics.  These models estimate and approximate the real behavior of the interconnect wire as a function of its parasitics. An ideal wire is one which does not have any impact on electrical behavior of the circuit i.e. no parasitics are associated with it. But the reality is far beyond it and requires complex electrical models for interconnects.

During early phase of VLSI design, the gate parasitic impedances had been much larger than the interconnect parasitic impedances, since the size of gate (width and length) was quite large. For example, 5µm was a typical minimum feature size in 1980. Therefore, interconnect parasitic  impedances  were  modeled  as  short  circuit.  With  the  conventional  technology  that used a feature size of 1 μ m or above, interconnects resistance was negligible compared to the driver resistance. Thus, the interconnect and loading gates were modeled as a lumped loading capacitance. The interconnect delay was determined by the driver resistance times the total loading  capacitance.  However,  with  the  scaling  of  technology  and  increased  chip  sizes (submicron VLSI technology), the cross-sectional area of interconnects had been scaled down while their  lengths  increased.  Therefore,  the  interconnect  resistance  was  comparable  to  the driver resistance and the interconnect capacitances became comparable to the gate parasitic capacitances,  which  forced  the  designers  to  model  interconnects  as RC line.  With  the introduction of RC models interconnection delay, power consumption and repeater insertions became important in realizing high performance VLSI's. Almost every aspect of the design and analysis was affected by new interconnect model. Interconnects are modelled differently which is dependent on the applications.

Interconnect analytical models can be categorized as lumped and distributed. The lumped model is useful only when a single parasitic component is dominant. When resistance of the interconnect  wire  is  small  and  switching  frequency  is  not  high,  then  only  capacitive component is dominated and it is useful to lump it. If interconnects are having a significant resistance then lumped capacitive model is inaccurate and lumped RC model is considered. However in  actuality,  the  circuit  parasitics  of  a  interconnect  wire  are  distributed  along  its length and it is not lumped. A simple circuit based on one aggressor and one victim is shown in Fig. 4(a). However, this circuit is not practical since in a realistic VLSI chip more number of aggressors are coupled to the victim line.

Figure 4(a). Simple one- aggressor one- victim circuit.

<!-- image -->

Figure 4(b). Equivalent lumped model.

<!-- image -->

The equivalent model is shown in Fig. 4(b). In this model, each pulling resistance ( RV and Ra ) is composed of the driver resistance and the line resistance. The load capacitances CV and Ca are composed of line capacitance and the gate capacitance of the load driven by the line. Earlier,  most  of  the  models  presented  are  similar  to  lumped  model  [29-32].  In  DSM technology, lumped models are no longer accurate. Also on-chip metal wire of more than a few millimeter length has significant resistance. So, distributed coupled RC models become necessary  even  for  early  design  stages.  Some  researchers  [33-35]  have  discussed  this problem. The distributed π , 3 π and 4 π models are proposed in [32, 36, 37]. A distributed RC model of an interconnect is shown in Fig. 5 [127].

Figure 5. Interconnect distributed RC line.

<!-- image -->

With ever-growing length of interconnects and increased clock frequency, the effects of on  chip  interconnects  cannot  be  restricted  to RC models.  The  importance  of  on-chip inductance  is  continuously  increasing  with  faster  on-chip  rise  times,  wider  wires,  and  the introduction of new materials for low resistance interconnects. The usage of higher operating frequencies increases the value of j L ω , which plays an important role in interconnect delay calculation and its design.

In  DSM technology, the inductance is becoming more important and it is necessary to take  transmission  line  properties  in  to  account  when  modeling  long  interconnects.  These properties affect both signal propagation within the interconnect and signal reflections at its transmitting and receiving ends. There are two time constants associated with a distributed

RLC line, an inductive time constant LC and a resistive time constant RC [38]. The onchip  inductance  becomes  significant  when  inductive  time  constant  is  comparable  to  or exceeds the resistive time constant [39, 40]. A distributed RLC transmission line model of an interconnect line is shown in Fig. 6 [127]. This also includes mutual inductance and coupling capacitance.

Figure 6. Interconnect distributed RLC Line.

<!-- image -->

A  distributed RLC model  of  an  interconnect  known  as  the  transmission  line  model, becomes the most accurate approximation of the actual behavior. For copper (Cu) board, the interconnects on PCB are wide and thick enough and furthermore due to the high conductivity of  copper,  the  interconnect  resistance  can  be  safely  ignored.  Thus  it  is  called  lossless transmission line model. But the same is not entirely true for on-chip interconnects where the resistance of the wire has significant value and is considered to be an important factor while determining  the  performance  parameters.  Therefore,  the  lossy  transmission  line  model  is considered  in  case  of  on-chip  interconnects.  Inductance  has  a  significant  effect  on  the response of a signal propagating across an interconnect line for the range of the length given in Eq. (1) [38].

<!-- formula-not-decoded -->

This range depends upon the parasitics of the interconnect per unit length as well as the rise time of the signal at the input of the CMOS circuit driving the interconnect.

As  the  correct  synchronization  of  the  signals  requires  the  accurate  signal  propagation delays  through  chip  interconnect  nets.  Earlier  methods  approximate  the  signal  propagation delay but do not consider the delay due to loading effect on driver. Maffezzoni et al . proposed a technique [87] to determine an equivalent lumped RC net of a distributed RC line and thus enables us to model both intrinsic propagation delay and the loading effect.

Event driven methods are very useful for simulation of large scale linear and nonlinear circuits.  The  draw  backs  of  previous  version  of  event  driven  simulators  are  spurious numerical oscillations and convergence problem. To overcome these drawbacks, Brambilla et al . proposed a rigorous approach to event driven simulation [88]. The proposed algorithm is

based on the staircase approximations of the characteristics of linear and nonlinear elements. This rigorous event driven (RED) method works successfully for very large nonlinear RC -passive circuits and has applications in simulation of VLSI interconnects delays.

## 2.3. Full Wave and ETL Model

The  microwave  integrated  circuits  (MIC)  represent  an  extension  of  integrated  circuit technology  to  extremely  high  frequencies.  Earlier,  nearly  all  microwave  systems  utilized coaxial  or  waveguide  lines,  but  with  the  introduction  of  MMICs,  planar  transmission  lines have become more suitable and extensively used. Commonly used configuration for planar transmission lines are stripline, microstrip and coplanar lines. In microstrip like structure, the fields  around  the  conducting  strip  exist  both  in  the  dielectric  and  in  air  and  leads  to  quasi TEM behavior. This complicates the analysis of this structure [106]. Quasi TEM means that there is electric and magnetic energy stored and that dissipates at the same time and region. In this  model,  telegrapher's  equations  are  employed  to  describe  the  characteristics  of  the transmission  line  in  voltage  and  current  equations.  These  models  include  lumped  and distributed models with frequency dependent or independent parameters.

Unwanted electromagnetic coupling between interconnect lines can cause signal integrity problem in analog and high speed digital circuits leading to distortion of analog signal and digital  pulses.  However,  the  severity  of  coupling  depends  on  the  line  geometry,  the termination impedance and the type of the signals on the lines. The crosstalk can be reduced by introducing additional ground track between the interfering lines [60-62]. The full wave spectral domain analysis for crosstalk reduction has been discussed in [59].

Electrical  length  is  the  criteria  used  for  classifying  interconnects.  If  the  interconnect

<!-- formula-not-decoded -->

<!-- formula-not-decoded -->

then  it  is  electrically  short  else  interconnect  is  called  as  electrically  long  [104,  105]. Electrically short interconnects can be presented using lumped model, and electrically long interconnect  can  be  presented  using  quasi  TEM  or  full  wave  model.  To  analyze  the  high speed effects in simulation, the full wave analysis and ETL models are essential for correct results.

The  simulation  of  microstrip  like  interconnections  using  conventional  techniques  is highly  CPU  intensive.  The  techniques  like  asymptotic  waveform  evaluation  (AWE)  and complex frequency hopping (CHF) are based on the quasi TEM approximation and does not address simulation of interconnects with full wave models. Full wave approach [63, 64] is more accurate and gives better results over a wide frequency range.

The  full  wave  model  includes  partial  element  equivalent  circuit  (PEEC)  and  retarded PEEC  (rPEEC).  PEEC  models  are  based  on  the  electric  field  integral  equation  full  wave formulation  and  leads  to  a  description  of  the  EM  problem  in  term  of  the  inductive  and capacitive coupling between the currents and charges [107-109]. PEEC models are lumped RLC elements which are extracted from the geometry by assuming non retarded quasi state

using  Maxwell's  equations.  This  approach  is  useful  for  the  modeling  of  interconnect  and packaging  structure  for  EM  problem  that  include  VLSI  circuits  or  circuit  elements.  The approximation made in PEEC model gives errors at quite high frequencies since the circuit modeling using capacitances is invalid [110]. So PEEC models that include retardation effects known as retarded PEEC model, is considered to provide full wave solution. These models are useful for the circuit modeling of radiation type problem.

High frequency effects in VLSI interconnects using full wave model has been carried out in [67]. A full wave approach based on finite element method presented in [65] has been used to  determine  the  electrical  properties  of  VLSI  interconnect.  Generally,  spectral  domain approach is used for full wave analysis [66].

If  the  bandwidth  of  the  signals  extends  to  the  frequency  ranges  where  quasi-TEM approach  no  longer  holds,  the  Standard  transmission  line  (STL)  model  is  not  adequate  to record high frequency effects like dispersion, skin effect, crosstalk and leaky phenomenon. Because of these effects, it is necessary to have accurate and efficient models while retaining the simplicity of STL model. Enhanced transmission line (ETL) model is presented in [68-70, 86]  which  is  derived  from  a  full  wave  integral  formulation  in  terms  of  electromagnetic potentials and is able to foresee high frequency effects. The ETL model correctly describes the  propagation  even  when  the  separation  between  two  conducting  lines  is  the  order  of smallest characteristic wavelength of the signals. As the mathematical structure of ETL model is similar to the classical transmission line model, so the computational cost of ETL model is low as compared to full wave simulation and this model gives accurate results.

Analysis of multi conductor transmission lines has been discussed for high speed high density MMICs and VLSI interconnects [71]. The dispersion and loss effects can exactly be described in this model. The dielectric losses of millimeter wave band leaky wave structure is corrected  by  an  iterative  procedure  in  [72].  In  this,  the  leaky  mode  theory  is  applied  to compensate the losses.

The  key  points  while  modeling  transmission  line  interconnects  include  impedance matching,  minimizing  delay,  suppression  of  crosstalk  and  EMI  and  reduction  of  signal overshoots  and  ringing.  When  these  points  are  considered,  the  signal  integrity  in  these interconnects can improve substantially.

## 3. TESTING

## 3.1. Importance of Testing

The  field  of  digital  system  has  undergone  a  major  revolution  in  recent  decades.  The devices are shrinking in size while growing both in speed and capabilities. As the density of transistors  on-chip  is  increasing  rapidly,  the  task  for  testing  the  chip  is  becoming  more difficult. However, it is necessary to test a number of chips in a short time for time delivery to the customer. To overcome this problem, design for testability (DFT) was proposed i.e. the device is designed and fabricated in such a way that the final product is easily testable. But DFT  has  become  even  more  critical.  The  in-circuit  testing  for  complex  PCBs  consisting Surface  Mount  Devices  (SMDs)  and  Plated-Through-Holes  (PTHs)  is  not  feasible.  The

accessibility  to  the  devices  on  PCB  and  to  the  circuitry  in  the  devices  is  reduced.  So  the testability of devices and PCB is also reduced unless appropriate steps (such as incorporating BIST) are taken to overcome testability problems [42]. So the testability is the main design issue  for  VLSI  circuits.  The  alternative  is  to  save  area  by  ignoring  testability  but  penalties even for low complexity circuits raises to 5 to10 times compared to the same circuit/system designed  for  testability.  There  are  two  concepts  in  designing  of  testable  circuits viz controllability and observability. In order to test for a fault on a node, it must be possible to both control and observe the behavior of the node. The controllability is the ability to set a specific signal value at every node internal to the circuit by setting values at input terminal of the  circuit.  An  easily  controllable  node  is  directly  settable  through  an  input  pad.  However, node with little controllability takes time to get it in to the right state. The observability is the ability  to  observe  the  signal  value  at  any  logic  node  in  the  circuit  at  the  output  pins.  This aspect is important to check the output of a gate in the logic circuit for its correct operation.

## 3.2. Review of Testing Techniques

To handle complex VLSI chips with large number of input-output pins, higher operating frequencies and large number of test vectors, extremely expensive automatic test equipment (ATE) is required and is expected to operate for long periods before replacement. As a result, most testers have components slower then the device under test. Because of complexity of devices  and  PCBs,  some  level  of  fault  diagnosis  during  manufacturing  is  needed  to  assist failure mode analysis for yield enhancement. Yield is the percentage of good chips produced in  a  manufacturing  process.  Built-in  Self-Test  (BIST)  is  the  solution  to  these  testing problems.  BIST  is  also  the  solution  for  the  testing  of  critical  circuits  that  have  no  direct connections to external pins. In the near future, even most advanced tester may no longer be adequate  for  the  faster  ICs,  a  situation  when  BIST  may  be  the  best  solution  for.  Some approaches that the researchers have adopted are IDDQ,  Boundary Scan, and Circular BIST. The IDDQ monitors the static power supply in CMOS. The abnormal leakage current is the parameter to detect in this approach [43]. This testing is an effective way to detect open and short defects. The problem with built in IDDQ testing is the need for specific analog circuitry. This technique is of least interest  for  advanced  technologies  for  which  there  is  not  a  large difference in current levels between healthy and faulty devices.

In  Scan testing  or  structured  testing,  the  circuit  is  modeled  as  a  collection  of  the  logic primitives  (gates,  flip  flops)  which  are  tested.  Sequential  logic  poses  a  very  difficult automatic  test  pattern  generation  problem.  Test  vectors  can  be  generated  automatically  for combinational  logic  but  it  is  much  harder  for  sequential  logic.  The  problem  in  sequential circuit testing is in determination of internal state of the circuit. So, sequential structured test approach  converts  sequential  logic  to  combinational  logic.  In  full  scan  design,  every sequential element is replaced with a scan flip flop. This results in an internal form of scan chain to be used in test mode. So, the internal states of the circuit can be observed/controlled by scanning out the contents of the scan path shift registers. However, there is an area and performance penalty for scan design. Level sensitive scan design (LSSD) is a form of scan design  that  incorporates  both  the  level  sensitivity  and  the  scan  path  approach.  The  level sensitivity ensures the sequential circuit response independent of the transient characteristics

of the circuit such as the component and wire delays. The LSSD removes hazards and races problems.  On  the  negative  side,  this  design  uses  more  complex  latches,  flip  flops,  inputoutput pins and interconnect wires, and requires more area on chip and more testing time due to shift in long registers. Boundary scan test configuration consists of placing a shift register cell close to each device input / output pin in order to from a chain that extends around the circuit. When all of these circuits on board are linked together to form a long chain of shift registers, the data is allowed to be sent over all the connections between the ICs on a board. This technique is used to test boards, but it is also useful  to  test  ASICs.  This  technique is further extended when used with BIST.

The circular BIST approach is an off-line and test-per-clock BIST architecture because it uses  the  existing  flip  flops  from  the  circuit  under  test  (CUT)  to  construct  the  test  pattern generator (TPG) and output response analyzer (ORA) functions. The basic idea is to partition the CUT into flip flops and combinational logic. The flip flops are augmented with additional logic to operate in the BIST mode. The main features of this approach are low area overhead and performance penalties. Selective replacement of flip flops in the CUT with circular BIST is important to control the area overhead and performance penalty. The main limitation of this technique is that high fault coverage is not assured. The test patterns generated in the circular BIST are not truly pseudorandom as they are with scan BIST or BILBO (built-in logic block observer) register and can cause significant aliasing due to register adjacency. To overcome the  limitation,  high  fault  coverage  with  circular  BIST  is  achieved  by  using  state  skipping logic [26, 58]. BILBO is built in test generation technique which employs signature analysis in  conjunction  with  a  scan  path.  It  uses  LFSR  and  few  gates.  It  has  four  different  modes controlled by two signals.

## 3.3. Built-in Self-Test

BIST is a technique that modifies the integrated circuit by incorporating testing circuit directly on chip. Online BIST [44] is used to perform the test when the circuit is in normal operation  and  off-line  operation  occurs  when  circuit  under  test  (CUT)  is  idle.  The  basic architecture  of  BIST  includes  test  pattern  generator  (TPG)  and  output  response  analyzer (ORA). The implementation of these blocks can be done with linear feedback shift registers (LFSRs). Fig.  7  shows  the  functional  diagram  of  BIST  [42].  In  normal  working,  the  CUT receives inputs X from other modules and perform the function for which it was designed. In testing mode, TPG generates a sequence of test patterns Y which are applied to CUT. The test responses are evaluated by a response monitor ORA.

In the most common type of BIST, the test responses are compacted in ORA (to reduce chip area penalty) to form signatures. The signature analysis is a popular data compression technique  which  is  based  on  cyclic  redundancy  checking  in  which  polynomial  division  is done.  The  test  output  data  polynomial  is  divided  by  the  characteristic  polynomial  and  the remainder  is  called  as  signature.  The  response  signatures  are  compared  with  the  reference signatures generated or stored on-chip and the error signal shows any discrepancies detected. Possibly some time the fault can go undetected by the circuit and this is called aliasing.

Figure 7. Functional diagram of BIST.

<!-- image -->

To  make  the  BIST  circuit  effective  with  reduced  cost/time  in  design/testing,  four parameters  must  be  considered  while  developing  BIST viz .  fault  coverage,  number  of  test patterns  generated,  overhead  in  chip  area  and  performance  penalty  [46].  The  circuitry  to generate  test  vectors  is  the  most  important  issue  in  BIST  design.  The  general  approach  in TPG design is to have high fault coverage with low overhead area. Area constraints of an IC affects the speed of testing and complexity of the circuit. As an example, a test set covering 100% faults is of no interest if its implementation requires large overhead on chip and also a test set with low overhead is of little use if fault coverage is low. However, for majority of designs, high fault coverage is of more importance than low overhead. The test vectors can be generated by using pseudorandom pattern generator, deterministic test generator, an adaptive test generator or by other means. In pseudorandom testing it is not necessary to generate all 2 n test vectors. A pseudorandom test generator circuit employs an LFSR. LFSR in its basic form is  string  of  D  flip  flops  with  feedback.  X-OR  gates  are  inserted  at  specific  points  in  the feedback loop. Although, this  method requires low overhead implementations but the fault coverage is not acceptable. For deterministic TPG, fault coverage is high with much higher area overhead. This testing method ensures that random pattern resistant faults are covered. After the above discussions of the two methods of TPG, it quickly becomes apparent that a hybrid solution would be excellent for high fault coverage while maintaining low overhead in silicon area. Test set Embedding based on width compression [45] presents a method of doing this in an efficient and simple way.

## 3.3.1. Advantages of BIST and its Different Approaches

Commercially,  it  is  difficult  to  ensure  production  of  high  quality  ICs  in  time  efficient manner. Keeping in mind effectiveness and cost / time, BIST has emerged a major design concerned  in  DFT  methodologies.  BIST  is  advantageous  in  many  ways.  One  of  the  main advantages of BIST is that it can be easily used at all levels of testing often referred to as vertical testability. The meaning is that the same testing approach can be used from wafer to system  level  testing.  BIST  offers  an  inherent  high  diagnostic  resolution  to  the  CUT  with which  it  is  associated.  In  addition,  BIST  offers  at  speed  testing  i.e.  when  the  testing  is performed at the system clock frequency. Apart from this, BIST can overcome pin limitations because with BIST the test patterns are generated internally by TPG and the output responses are checked internally by ORA. Other benefits that BIST offer are economical burn-in test,

reduced need for automatic test equipment (ATE) and usability as on-line and off-line testing. Although BIST offers some demerits like area overhead, performance penalties,  additional design  time  and  efforts  [46]  but  the  case  studies  on  BIST  have  justified  that  its  benefits always make up for the cost of BIST.

Some of  the  approaches  of  BIST  proposed  and  implemented  so  far  for  testing  of  ICs include built in logic block observer (BILBO), pseudo exhaustive BIST, circular BIST, scan based BIST, non intrusive BIST, BIST for regular structures, BIST for FPGAs and CPLDs and mixed signal BIST [42]. Researchers have proposed BIST approaches to test interconnect related defects in integrated circuits, FPGAs and PCBs [9-16, 19-24, 27].

## 3.3.2. BIST Economics

Besides the features of BIST, costs - benefits analysis is also important. Manufactures insert BIST circuitry into large complex ASICs to facilitate test generation and fault coverage. With the appropriate access features, the BIST can be reused at subsequent levels of product integration such as boards and systems. An economics framework of BIST and boundary scan has been carried out by various researchers [95-99].

Lu and Wu [96] has presented cost and benefit models of BIST for logic and memory cores.  They  have  described  the  recurring  and  nonrecurring  benefits  of  BIST.  The  results shown in their paper are for the specific cases, however, different projects and manufacturers may  have  different results and  conclusions  because of parametric variations. As an illustration ,  they  have  found  from  empirical  data  that  test  development  time  for  circuits containing logic BIST, TtdLB, is only 1/590 th the time without BIST, Ttd.

<!-- formula-not-decoded -->

Therefore, the benefit to test programming , BTP, is

<!-- formula-not-decoded -->

<!-- formula-not-decoded -->

Thus, BIST eliminates 99.83% of the test program development time.

Using  BIST  at  right  level  offers  significant  cost  saving,  however  the  determination  of levels is complex. Successful design and test involves many factors such as time to market, quality perception, market place, product life time and so on. Economic analyses can be quit helpful for designers in decision making [97-99].

## 4. FAULTS

The chip testing is a multipurpose task to detect faults in fabrication, design and failure due to stressful operating conditions. The fabrication of an IC is a complex process requiring a  number  of  processing  steps.  Any  problem  during  fabrication  may  introduce  a  physical defect which in turn may lead to a fault. When a fault changes the circuit behavior, the change is  called  the  fault  effect.  A  defect  is  an  unintended  difference  between  the  implemented

hardware  and  its  desired  design.  Defects  occur  either  during  the  use  of  ICs  or  during  its manufacturing. A fault is a representation of a defect at the abstracted function level. An error is caused by a fault or it is a design error.

Faults  through  out  the  system  life  can  be  classified  into  three  categories viz design, fabrication  and  operational.  Design  faults  are  the  mistakes  because  of  human  designer  or CAD  software  during  the  design  process.  The  fabrication  defect  is  due  to  imperfect manufacturing process. These defects include defects in silicon substrate, photolithographic defects,  mask  contamination  and  scratches,  process  variations  and  oxide  defects,  over  and under etching and bad contact. The physical defects are mapped into few electrical faults to simplify the things. Some of these are short circuit, open circuit, change in threshold level, delay fault. The causes of short circuit are under etching of metal, spiking, pin holes or shorts across  the  gate  oxide  and  diffusion  shorts.  The  short  circuit  faults  usually  occur  in interconnect and often called bridging faults. Bridging faults occur when two or more of the interconnect  lines  are  shorted  together.  The  open  circuit  faults  result  from  a  bad  contact, missing  or  over  etching  of  metal  or  a  break  in  a  interconnect  line.  A  delay  fault  causes  a critical  path  being  slower  then  specified.  Some  of  the  reasons  are  improper  estimation  of interconnect delays, variations in fabrication process, opens in conducting lines, connecting parallel transistors, hot carrier induced delay. Now we need a model to predict the effects of faults. The most popular model is stuck-at fault model. The single stuck-at fault models are used frequently, however, CUT can have defects that cannot be modeled to a single stuck-at fault. In single stuck-at fault model, a single signal line at a time is assumed to have a fixed 0 or  1  value  independent  of  the  other  signal  values.  A  multiple  stuck-at  fault  model  is  too complicated to implement. In this model, multiple signal lines may be stuck- at some values. There are other fault models viz stuck-on and stuck-off.

Interconnect crosstalk defect induces delay and noise glitch in the response of the circuit. Noise glitch generated by cross coupling can propagate and amplify while traveling along a path. This can upset circuitry logic and can cause functional failure. Several fault models have been proposed to capture the crosstalk effects [2, 21, 25, 27, 47, 48].

## 5. TESTING OF INTERCONNECTS

The  performance  of  chip  designed  using  latest  technology  does  not  match  with  the technology. The major factors are unexpected side effects of interconnect parasitics such as crosstalk, delay and power dissipation. The parasitics start dominating with the introduction of  DSM  technology.  With  DSM  technology,  as  frequency  increases  over  1GHz  and interspacing of interconnects is narrow, unexpected crosstalk effects can degrade the circuit performance  significantly.  So,  it  is  necessary  that  the  circuit  designer  should  be  aware  of these effects on circuit before finalizing the design. In the last decade, researchers have paid lot of attention to the analysis of crosstalk effects [3-8, 18, 27, 49, 50] and suggested remedies for  minimization  of  such  hazardous  effects  [29,  51,  52].  However,  less  efforts  have  been made to test the effects of on-chip interconnect parasitics. Thus, testing is gaining importance with the current technology migrating into an Ultra -DSM and Nanotechnology.

To  ensure  correct  circuit  operation,  coupling  effects  like  crosstalk-induced  pulses, slowdown  and  speedup  must  be  considered.  Chen et  al .  addressed  the  issues  on  crosstalk

induced  pluses  and  related  test  generation  technique  [73]  and  proposed  a  test  generation algorithm that considers speedup, slowdown and pulses as new logic value. The problem of testing crosstalk errors i.e. glitch (noise) and delay is addressed in [6] in which the Cuviello et al. has  focused  on  developing  test  and  diagnosis  method  for  crosstalk  errors  on  SoC interconnects. Maximum aggressor fault (MAF) model is used which provides the coverage of all physical defects that lead to crosstalk errors. Also it has been shown the necessity of MAF tests in detecting the crosstalk defects. The Maximum aggressor (MA) tests provide the worst glitch or delay effects and are considered to be the most effective test sets for crosstalk defects in interconnects. However, it may not be possible to achieve very high crosstalk fault coverage for deeply embedded chip interconnects by using these test vectors. To have high quality testing of on-chip interconnects, a self test methodology which is based on Maximum Aggressor Fault Model is presented in [23]. The proposed methodology involves the insertion of self test structures such as test generator, error detector and a test controller in SoC. The physical defects and the process variations that lead to crosstalk errors on the victim wire are represented by Maximum Aggressor Fault Model.

External at-speed testing of crosstalk is expensive because it requires high speed, large pin count, large memory and good timing accuracy testers. Therefore, self testing technique to test crosstalk in SoC is employed. Although BIST eliminates the need of high speed testers but  it  may  lead  to  high  test  overhead  and  possibility  of  over  testing.  These  issues  are addressed  in  [74]  in  which  a  new  software  based  self-test  technique  for  system  level interconnects using embedded processor has been proposed. The technique allows at speed testing of interconnect crosstalk defects by executing self-test program by on-chip processor in operational mode of SoC.

Occasionally self  test  structures  are  inserted  in  the  SoC  to  generate  test  vectors  which have  100  %  crosstalk  defect  coverage.  But  this  method  requires  high  area  overhead.  To reduce this overhead, existing logic BIST structures like LFSRs are reused to generate test vectors. But LFSRs vectors have very poor crosstalk defect coverage. These issues have been addressed in [75] in which a new methodology named Logic-Interconnect BIST (LI-BIST) is proposed to test both the logic cores and interconnects on SoC. The MAF model is used for this  purpose.  This  methodology  produces  high  crosstalk  defect  coverage  with  low  area overhead and low interconnect power consumption.

The power consumption during testing is a major concern. Studies have shown that the power consumption in long interconnects is a significant fraction of the total system power. The power consumption varies in the presence of inter wire parasitics depending on the type of transitions on the interconnects [76].

Su et  al. proposed  a  decentralized  interconnect  BIST  technique  [77]  for  systems  with multiple scan chains. In this, the complexity of system level interconnect testing is divided and  conquered  using  decentralized  approach.  The  design  and  implementation  cost  of  this approach  is  very  low  as  all  the  scan  chains  share  the  same  BIST  module.  This  method achieves 100% coverage of stuck-at and short faults.

A  fast  and  accurate  technique  to  evaluate  the  effectiveness  of  test  sets  for  crosstalk defects in on-chip interconnects is developed in [21]. The technique presented in [21] is based on  a  key  covering  relationship  between  test  vectors  and  crosstalk  faults.  In  this  context,  a crosstalk  model  for  computation  of  fault  coverage  of  a  given  test-set  is  presented.  Two different techniques viz. simulation based and graph based for defect/fault  coverage computation are developed for a given test set in terms of defect coverage. Furthermore, it has

been shown that the proposed technique is faster than the alternative method of SPICE-level simulation.

A BIST method to test interconnect defects targeting non stuck-at faults (shorts, opens or any other) is presented in [19] using IDDQ testing, delay testing and boundary scan. A novel approach to estimate crosstalk noise in presence of multiple lines is presented in [53]. In the proposed model, active and passive aggressors are represented simultaneously on the victim line.  The  active  aggressors  are  represented  by  current  sources  and  passive  aggressors  are modeled as equivalent capacitances. The minimization of crosstalk effects is proposed in [29, 54]. An efficient method for generating test vectors to detect crosstalk glitch in digital circuits is given in [25]. An automatic test extractor for glitch (ATEG) algorithm was developed for the purpose which efficiently generates test vectors to create a glitch at candidate nodes and propagates it to latches or primary outputs.

Lee et  al. developed  an  automatic  test  pattern  generation  (ATPG)  algorithm  [24]  to generate test vectors. The algorithm utilizes condition for creation of worst case coupling and propagation  of  delayed  signal.  Among  various  ATPG  methods  for  crosstalk  faults,  some consider  noise  effects  caused  by  crosstalk  induced  glitch  faults  [73].  However,  testing  for crosstalk  induced  delays  has  been  addressed  in  [78-80].  Because  timing  information  is  the requirement for  test  pattern  generation  for  crosstalk  induced  delay  faults,  so  reducing  high complexity of ATPG is the main issue of several methods. Sunghoon et  al .  addressed  this problem  and  proposed  a  test  generation  algorithm  [81]  for  delay  faults  based  on  a conventional  delay  ATPG  technique  in  order  to  reduce  the  complexity  of  previous  ATPG algorithm and to consider crosstalk faults induced by multiple aggressors.

In  the  path  based  delay  testing,  testing  a  subset  of  paths  in  the  circuit  is  done  that  are more  likely  to  fail  and  hence  more  critical.  To  identify  the  real  critical  path,  the  dynamic delay effects such as coupling noise and supply noise are considered. Previously researchers have considered the process variation in path selection, but dynamic variability effect on path selection has not drawn much attention. The sources of dynamic variability include coupling noise, supply noise, multiple input switching noise and temperature. Tayade et al. addressed this issue and has proposed a technique to estimate the maximum delay of a path in presence of  coupling  noise  [85].  Estimating  the  delay  of  a  path  under  the  effect  of  other  dynamic effects  such  as  supply  voltage  noise,  temperature  and  multiple  input  switching  noise  is  a future area of research.

Various problems on signal integrity have been discussed by many researchers for RF circuits  and  high  frequency  DSM  chips.  The  key  problems  are  crosstalk,  delay,  overshoot (signal  rising  momentarily  above  the  supply  voltage),  reflection  (echoing  back  a  portion), EMI, power supply noise. Some solutions to resolve the problems are: 3-D layout modeling, parasitic extraction, accurate RLC simulation of on chip power grid, to limit the maximum dv/dt with decoupling capacitors, to improve IR drop, inserting buffers on the interconnects and shielding wires. Nourani et al. addressed these issues and proposed BIST based technique [82] to test signal integrity faults (noise and skew) on the high speed interconnects of SoC.

Tehranipour et al. proposed enhanced boundary scan architecture to test signal integrity in  high  speed  interconnects  [20].  The  proposed  architecture  is  used  to  generate  and  apply multiple  transition  (MT)  patterns.  The  proposed  MT  fault  model  is  a  superset  of  the  MA model and is much  capable of testing capacitive and inductive couplings amongst interconnects. For testing long interconnects for signal integrity, BIST based architecture is reported in [55] and the other proposed methods are boundary scan and IDDQ for testing buses

[56].  The  short  interconnects  which  are located  near to  long interconnects also  suffer  from integrity problem. So, methods need to be developed to test both short and long high speed interconnects  [57].  Pendurkar et  al. developed  a  novel  scheme  for  performance  testing  of system-level interconnects [22]. For this, a BIST scheme based on cascaded structure of precharacterized  test-pattern  generator  (P-TPG)  has  been  developed.  .Chun et  al developed maximal dominant signal integrity (MDSI) fault model for a given RLC interconnection [83]. This model is based on the analysis of the impact of aggressor lines on signal integrity effect at  a  victim line. The results shown conclude that MDSI fault model is more exact than the MA fault model and the MT fault model and the defect coverage is much higher. Mehta et al. discussed  the  analysis  of  multiple  delay  fault  diagnoses  problem  in  [17]  with  a  novel approach  to  solve  it.  A  BIST  method  is  proposed  to  test  PCB  level  interconnects  using boundary-scan architecture  [15].  A  technique  is presented  in  [16]  to  test  PCB  interconnect through the multiple input linear feed back shift registers (MISR). Several researchers have proposed BIST based scheme to diagnose interconnect faults in FPGA [9-14].

Crosstalk exists  among word lines, bit lines and between word lines and bit lines of a DRAM and can cause data retention problems. Yang et  al .  studied  the  effects  of  crosstalk induced faults on the DRM read operations [84]. This class of faults is referred to as crosstalk reading  faults  (CRFs).  Also  two  test  algorithms  have  been  developed  [84]  that  detect  the crosstalk induced faults.

The diagnosis of opens using physical information has been addressed in [89, 90]. The effect of coupling signals on interconnect opens is discussed in [91, 92]. The condition to test opens in context of delay fault testing is described in [93]. Gomez et al . proposed a test vector generation method [94] called open vector generator (OPVEG) to improve the detectability of interconnection opens by applying proper logic levels at the coupled signals.

## 6. FUTURE CHALLENGES

The effects of  interconnect  on  signal  integrity  become  significant  in  DSM  technology. For  proper  performance  of  the  circuit,  testing  of  interconnect  is  important  and  emerging challenge  in  the  nanotechnology.  Although,  some  work  has  been  done  for  testing  of interconnects  however,  it  is  still  an  open  area  to  test  the  parasitics  effects  of  VLSI/ULSI interconnects. Efforts are required to analyze develop test methods for estimation of crosstalk, delay and power dissipation in current technology with solutions to minimize these effect.

In addition, skin effect comes into the picture at very high frequency. This effect causes distortion  of  the  signal  being  transmitted  over  the  interconnect.  This  effect  needs  to  be analyzed and tested however, such problems are encountered with wide interconnects.

In  the  present  scenario,  a  SoC  consists  of  variety  of  components  communicating  with each other. It is the responsibility of interconnects to ensure correct and reliable data routing. The  interconnect  delay  will  increase  to  9  times  than  gate  delay  at  65  mm  technology according  to  [114,115].  In  addition,  on  chip  wire  length  is  expected  to  increase  to  2.22 km/cm 2 by  the  year  2010  [115].  Thus,  an  increase  in  power  dissipation  in  interconnect  is expected to be 50 times more than logic circuits. Therefore in DSM technology, the reliable performance  of  interconnect  is  more  important  than  logic  gates  on  chip.  In  addition  to available  bus  based  interconnect  architecture,  networks  on  chip  (NoC)  has  recently  been

proposed as interconnect architecture by the researchers [116]. For both of these architecture, copper based interconnects are used for data transmission. But copper interconnects poses the problems of delay, power dissipation and reliability. To overcome the limitations of copper interconnects, on chip Optical interconnects, RF /Wireless interconnects and Carbon nanotube (CNT)  interconnects  are  proposed  [117-122].  However,  these  emerging  technologies  have several issues and open problems for research.

In voltage mode signaling, repeaters are inserted in the interconnect line to reduce delays in signal transmission. However in DSM technology, there is a limit to improve power and delay.  Current  mode  signaling  for  data  transmission  over  interconnect  is  used  to  improve speed of data transmission, to reduce noise and to reduce power consumption than voltage mode signaling. In current mode, the system latency due to interconnect is very small because there is no capacitive charging and discharging. In this technique, the latency and power are largely  independent  of  wire  length,  thus,  making  the  system  attractive  for  global  and  semi global interconnects over usual voltage mode [111-113, 123]. Various issues of this technique need to be studied s.a. impact of on-chip wire inductance on signal integrity.

In DSM  technology,  crosstalk and delay fault are mainly responsible for poor performance  of  the  circuit.  So,  efficient  test  generation  techniques  for  testing  of  VLSI interconnects  are  in  demand.  The  simplicity,  robustness,  efficiency  and  effectiveness  of Genetic  Algorithm  (GA)  makes  this  tool  very  promising.  Few  researchers  have  proposed techniques  based  on  GA  for  testing  of  VLSI  circuits  /  PCB  boards  (124-126).  On-  chip interconnect testing based on GA is a challenging area of research.

## 7 . CONCLUSION

The importance and behavior of VLSI interconnects have been presented in this chapter. The design of interconnect and its testing has become a dominant issue in high speed ICs. The on-chip  inductance  is  becoming  more  important  with  faster  on-chip  rise  time,  higher switching  frequency,  longer  wire  length  and  with  the  use  of  low  resistance  material.  The parasitics  and  their  effects  on  circuit  performance  have  been  discussed  and  adequate interconnect models are presented. The RLC distributed model equivalent to transmission line is more effective in current scenario because of significant inductive effects. Furthermore, this chapter presents brief survey of testing VLSI interconnects. The testing techniques have been reviewed briefly with main focus on BIST. One of the major advantages of using BIST is that test is done internally and no expense is used on external components. Skin effect influences the performance of on- chip interconnects signals s.a clock and power distribution networks, therefore, accurate modeling of this effect is required. It can be concluded that in near future, the rapid advances in technology will cause new challenges to testing.
